Bond Market Operations & Analytics — Complete End-Semester Study Guide

How to use: read each unit top to bottom (intuition → definition → formula → worked examples → self-check), then drill the solved course material and the question bank; revise from the cheat sheet on exam morning.

Built from your syllabus, the Malkiel & Duration handout, the Yield Curve student handout, the Practice Numerical Questions, the Unit 5 presentation-topic list, the August 2026 mid-sem paper with its official scheme of valuation, the Lecture 5 scenario-analysis exercise and the CIA briefs. Every number was checked by computer; rounding is stated where it matters.

The course plan does not state the ESE duration. Numericals from Unit 2 (pricing, YTM, duration, convexity), Unit 1 (auction allotment) and Unit 3 (forward rates, bootstrapping) are the most likely Section C material.

Unit 1 — Foundations of Bond Markets

Syllabus: structure, participants and instruments (India and global); primary and secondary markets; uniform vs discriminatory auctions; repo markets and liquidity. (9 teaching hours, CO1)

By the end of this unit you should be able to: define a bond and every term on its "label"; draw the structure of the Indian bond market and name who does what; list every major instrument; describe how a G-Sec is auctioned and how a corporate bond is placed; allot securities in a uniform and in a multiple-price auction; explain how bonds are traded and settled (NDS-OM, OTC, CCIL); and explain repo, reverse repo and TREPS and why they matter for liquidity.

1.1 What is a bond? Basic terminology

A bond is a formal IOU. You lend ₹1,000 to the Government of India today. In return the Government promises to pay you a fixed amount every year (say ₹72) and to give your ₹1,000 back on a fixed date. Because the promise is written down in a standard form, you can sell that IOU to someone else before the date — that is why there is a "market" for bonds.

A bond is a debt instrument (a fixed-income security) under which the issuer borrows money from investors and contractually promises to pay periodic interest, called the coupon, and to repay the principal, called the face value, on a specified maturity date.

Bonds finance governments (the fiscal deficit), companies and infrastructure; they are the largest part of Indian banks' and insurers' investments; and their yields set the benchmark "risk-free" rate used to value almost every other asset.

The bond's "label": terms you must know

TermMeaningExample (7.10% GS 2034, illustrative)
IssuerThe borrower who promises to payGovernment of India (RBI manages the issue)
Face / par value (F)Principal repaid at maturity; coupons are a % of it₹100 per unit (G-Secs are quoted per ₹100)
Coupon rate (c)Fixed annual interest as % of face value; set at issue7.10% p.a.
Coupon (C)Rupee interest per period = c × F / m₹3.55 every six months
Coupon frequency (m)Payments per year2 (semi-annual) for dated G-Secs
Maturity date / tenorDate principal is repaid / original life2034; 10-year tenor
Term to maturity (n)Remaining life from todayFalls every day — drives pull-to-par
Market price (P)What the bond trades for todaye.g. ₹101.25 per ₹100
Yield / YTM (y)Return implied by the price if held to maturityMoves daily with the market
Premium / par / discountP > F / P = F / P < FPremium when c > y
Redemption valueAmount repaid at maturity (normally = F)₹100
Accrued interestCoupon earned by the seller since the last coupon dateAdded to clean price to get dirty price
Call / put optionIssuer may redeem early / investor may demand early redemptionCommon in bank Tier-2/AT1 and some NCDs
Security / senioritySecured by assets or not; rank in liquidationSecured NCD ranks above subordinated debt
Credit ratingAgency opinion on default probabilityG-Secs are unrated sovereign; corporates e.g. CRISIL AAA
Debenture trust deed / trusteeLegal contract; trustee protects investorsMandatory for listed NCDs under SEBI rules
ISIN12-character unique security identifierIndian ISINs start with "IN"
Record dateCut-off date to decide who receives the next couponBuyer after record date does not get that coupon

Classification of bonds

BasisTypes
IssuerSovereign (central government), sub-sovereign (state governments – SDLs), municipal, PSU/quasi-sovereign, banks and financial institutions, corporates, supranationals (World Bank, ADB, IFC)
CouponFixed-rate; floating-rate (FRB, coupon = benchmark + spread); zero-coupon / deep-discount; step-up / step-down; inflation-indexed
MaturityMoney-market (up to 1 year: T-Bills, CPs, CDs); short, medium and long-dated bonds; perpetual (no maturity)
SecuritySecured (charge on assets) vs unsecured; senior vs subordinated (Tier 2, AT1)
Embedded optionsCallable, puttable, convertible (into equity), exchangeable

PlacementPublic issue vs private placement; listed vs unlisted

Bonds vs equity shares

PointBond (debt)Equity share
Holder's statusCreditor / lenderOwner
ReturnContractual coupon (fixed or formula)Dividend (discretionary) + capital gain
MaturityFixed (except perpetuals)None
Priority in liquidationBefore shareholdersLast (residual claim)
Voting rightsNone (except covenants)Yes
RiskLower; main risks are interest-rate and credit riskHigher; business risk
Tax for issuerInterest is tax-deductibleDividend paid from post-tax profit

The benchmark 10-year Government of India bond is the most watched number in Indian finance: newspapers report "the 10-year G-Sec yield", banks price loans off it, and companies borrowing for 10 years pay "G-Sec + spread". Masala bonds were pioneered by the IFC (World Bank group) in 2014, and HDFC was among the first Indian corporates to issue them in 2016.

1. A ₹1,000 bond has a 7.5% coupon paid semi-annually. What is each coupon?

C = 7.5% × 1,000 / 2 = ₹37.50 every six months.

2. Is the coupon rate or the yield fixed for the life of the bond?

The coupon rate is fixed at issue. The yield changes every day with the market price.

3. Give one difference between a bondholder and a shareholder.

A bondholder is a creditor with a contractual claim to interest and principal and priority in liquidation; a shareholder is an owner with a residual claim.

1.2 Structure of the bond market and its participants

Think of the bond market as a two-floor building. On the ground floor (primary market) new bonds are born: the issuer sells them to the first buyers and receives cash. On the first floor (secondary market) existing bonds change hands between investors; the issuer receives nothing, but the prices set here tell the issuer what it will cost to borrow next time. Around the building sit the regulators (RBI, SEBI) and the plumbing (CCIL, depositories, exchanges).

The bond market (debt or fixed-income market) is the market in which debt securities are issued (primary market) and subsequently bought and sold (secondary market), together with the institutions, regulations and infrastructure for pricing, clearing and settling them.

Segments of the Indian debt market

  1. Government securities (G-Sec) market – central government dated securities, Treasury Bills, Cash Management Bills and State Government Securities (SDLs). By far the largest and most liquid segment; regulated by RBI.
  2. Corporate bond market – bonds/NCDs of PSUs, banks, NBFCs, housing finance companies and private companies; regulated by SEBI (listed issues) with RBI oversight of banks/NBFCs.
  3. Money market – instruments up to one year: call/notice/term money, T-Bills, CPs, CDs, market repo and TREPS; regulated by RBI.

Participants and their roles

ParticipantRole in the bond market
Government of India / State governmentsLargest borrowers; finance fiscal deficits through dated securities and T-Bills.
Reserve Bank of IndiaDebt manager for the Centre (and States by agreement), regulator of the G-Sec and money markets, conducts auctions, runs LAF/repo operations, open market operations (OMOs), maintains SGL accounts.
SEBIRegulates issue and listing of corporate debt (SEBI (Issue and Listing of Non-Convertible Securities) Regulations, 2021), credit rating agencies, debenture trustees, mutual funds, online bond platform providers.
Commercial banksLargest investors in G-Secs, partly because of the Statutory Liquidity Ratio (SLR) which requires them to hold a percentage of deposits in liquid assets, mainly G-Secs; also issuers of CDs, Tier-2 and AT1 bonds.
Primary Dealers (PDs)RBI-authorised market makers who underwrite G-Sec auctions, bid in every auction and give two-way quotes in the secondary market. Can be standalone PDs or banks running PD business.
Insurance companies, provident and pension fundsLong-horizon investors; buy long-dated G-Secs and SDLs to match long liabilities (a natural "preferred habitat" – see Unit 3).
Mutual fundsDebt funds (liquid, gilt, corporate bond, target-maturity funds) that pool retail money into bonds.
Foreign Portfolio Investors (FPIs)Invest within limits or through the Fully Accessible Route (FAR) for specified G-Secs.
Retail investorsThrough RBI Retail Direct, stock-exchange non-competitive bidding, debt mutual funds and SEBI-registered online bond platforms.
Credit rating agenciesCRISIL, ICRA, CARE Ratings, India Ratings & Research, Acuité, Infomerics and others rate corporate debt.
Debenture trusteesMonitor covenants and security on behalf of bondholders.
CCILClearing Corporation of India Ltd: central counterparty that clears and settles G-Sec, repo, TREPS and forex trades.
Stock exchanges and their clearing corporationsNSE and BSE run EBP, RFQ and reporting platforms for corporate bonds; NSE Clearing and ICCL settle trades.
DepositoriesNSDL and CDSL hold corporate bonds in demat form; G-Secs sit in RBI's SGL/CSGL accounts.
FBIL and FIMMDAFinancial Benchmarks India Ltd publishes G-Sec valuation prices and money-market benchmarks; FIMMDA is the self-regulatory/industry body for fixed-income and money markets.

Global context

Globally, the bond market is larger than the equity market by outstanding value. The US Treasury market (bills up to one year, notes 2–10 years, bonds 20–30 years, and inflation-protected TIPS) is the world's benchmark risk-free market; other large sovereign markets are Japan (JGBs), Germany (Bunds) and the UK (gilts). Other global segments: investment-grade and high-yield corporate bonds, municipal bonds, mortgage- and asset-backed securities, and the international market of Eurobonds and foreign bonds.

FeatureIndiaDeveloped markets (e.g. US)
Dominant segmentGovernment securitiesGovernment, corporate and securitised all large
Corporate bondsMostly privately placed, concentrated in AAA/AA and financial-sector issuersDeep market across ratings including high-yield
Secondary liquidityConcentrated in a few benchmark G-Secs; thin in corporatesDeep across many issues
Retail participationLow but rising (Retail Direct, online bond platforms)Higher, mainly via funds
Main investorsBanks (SLR), insurers, PF/pension fundsFunds, pension funds, foreign central banks

India's Fully Accessible Route (FAR) G-Secs were added to the J.P. Morgan GBI-EM Global index series from June 2024, bringing index-tracking foreign money into Indian government bonds. Such flows affect demand, and therefore yields, especially at the long end.

Knowing who holds bonds explains price behaviour: when RBI changes the SLR, when insurers get large inflows or when FPIs buy on index inclusion, demand for specific maturities shifts and the yield curve changes shape.

1. Which regulator oversees G-Secs and which oversees listed corporate bonds?

RBI regulates G-Secs, T-Bills, SDLs and the money market; SEBI regulates issue and listing of corporate debt.

2. Why are banks the biggest holders of G-Secs in India?

Because of the Statutory Liquidity Ratio requirement, plus G-Secs' zero credit risk, eligibility as repo collateral and liquidity.

3. What does CCIL do?

It acts as the central counterparty, guaranteeing and settling G-Sec, repo and TREPS trades so that participants face CCIL instead of each other (removes counterparty risk).

1.3 Instruments in the Indian (and global) bond market

InstrumentIssuerKey features
Treasury Bills (T-Bills)Government of IndiaMoney-market, zero-coupon: issued at a discount and redeemed at face value. Three tenors: 91-day, 182-day, 364-day. Auctioned weekly by RBI (usually Wednesdays). Yield computed on a 365-day basis.
Cash Management Bills (CMBs)Government of IndiaVery short-term (less than 91 days) T-Bill-like instruments used to meet temporary cash-flow mismatches of the government.
Dated G-SecsGovernment of IndiaFixed coupon, paid half-yearly, tenors from about 2 years to very long (30–40 years and beyond). Named like "7.10% GS 2034" (coupon, then maturity year).
Floating Rate Bonds (FRBs)Government of IndiaCoupon reset periodically at a benchmark (e.g. recent T-Bill cut-off yields) plus a fixed spread; low interest-rate risk.
Inflation-Indexed Bonds (IIBs) / Capital-Indexed BondsGovernment of IndiaPrincipal and/or coupon linked to an inflation index, protecting real returns.
STRIPSCreated from G-SecsSeparate Trading of Registered Interest and Principal of Securities: each coupon and the principal of a G-Sec is split into separate zero-coupon securities. Useful for immunisation and for building zero curves.
State Development Loans (SDLs) / State Government SecuritiesState governmentsDated securities auctioned by RBI on behalf of states; usually yield a spread over G-Secs of similar maturity.
Sovereign Gold Bonds (SGBs)RBI on behalf of GoIDenominated in grams of gold; paid 2.5% p.a. on initial investment plus redemption at gold price; 8-year tenor with early exit after the 5th year. No new tranches have been issued since early 2024.
Floating Rate Savings Bonds 2020 (Taxable)GoI (via RBI and agency banks)Retail, non-tradable; coupon linked to the National Savings Certificate rate plus 0.35%, reset half-yearly.
Sovereign Green Bonds (SGrBs)GoIProceeds used only for eligible green projects under the Sovereign Green Bond Framework (Unit 5).
Special securitiesGoINon-SLR securities issued to specific entities (e.g. oil marketing companies, fertiliser companies, public-sector bank recapitalisation bonds) instead of cash.
PSU bondsPublic-sector undertakings (NTPC, REC, PFC, NABARD, IRFC...)Quasi-sovereign; trade at a small spread over G-Secs; includes past tax-free bonds and Section 54EC bonds.
Corporate bonds / Non-Convertible Debentures (NCDs)Companies, NBFCs, HFCsSecured or unsecured, rated, listed or unlisted; issued by public issue or private placement; coupon fixed or floating; often annual coupons.
Commercial Paper (CP)Companies, NBFCs, PDsUnsecured short-term promissory note, 7 days to 1 year, issued at a discount; rated.
Certificates of Deposit (CDs)Banks (7 days – 1 year), select financial institutions (1–3 years)Negotiable, discounted money-market instrument.
Bank capital bonds (Tier 2, AT1 perpetuals)BanksSubordinated; AT1 bonds are perpetual with issuer call options and loss-absorption features (coupon can be skipped, principal can be written down) under Basel III.
Municipal bondsUrban local bodiesFund urban infrastructure; issued under SEBI's municipal debt regulations; examples include Pune (2017) and Ghaziabad (green municipal bond, 2021).
Securitised debt (PTCs)SPVs/trustsPass-through certificates backed by pools of loans.
Masala bonds / ECBs / FCCBsIndian entities borrowing abroadRupee-denominated offshore (masala, currency risk on investor) vs foreign-currency borrowing (ECBs, FCCBs – currency risk on issuer).
Green, social, sustainability, sustainability-linked bondsGoI, PSUs, corporates, banksLabelled bonds (Unit 5).

A typical week: RBI auctions 91-, 182- and 364-day T-Bills on Wednesday; dated G-Secs on Friday; and State Government Securities on Tuesday, according to calendars published in advance. The published calendar tells investors how much supply of each maturity is coming, which itself affects yields.

T-Bills pay no coupon. The investor's return is the discount: buy at ₹98.40, receive ₹100. Do not add coupons when pricing a T-Bill or a CP/CD.

T-Bill yield (RBI convention, 365-day year)

y = [(F − P) / P] × (365 / d)      P = F / [1 + y × d / 365]

F = face value (₹100), P = price, d = days to maturity. Use for T-Bills, CMBs, CPs and CDs (money-market discount instruments).

  1. Find the yield. A 91-day T-Bill is bought at ₹98.40. Discount = 100 − 98.40 = ₹1.60.
  2. y = (1.60 / 98.40) × (365 / 91) = 0.016260 × 4.010989 = 0.06522 ⇒ 6.52% p.a.
  3. Reverse: find the price. A 364-day T-Bill must yield 6.50%. P = 100 / (1 + 0.065 × 364/365) = 100 / 1.064822 = ₹93.91.
  4. A 182-day T-Bill at 6.20%: P = 100 / (1 + 0.062 × 182/365) = 100 / 1.030915 = ₹97.00.
1. Name the three T-Bill tenors.

91-day, 182-day and 364-day.

2. What are STRIPS and why are they useful?

Zero-coupon securities created by separating each coupon and the principal of a G-Sec. They let investors buy exact-date cash flows (liability matching) and help build the zero-coupon yield curve.

3. Why do SDLs yield more than G-Secs of the same maturity?

Lower liquidity and a (perceived) slightly higher credit and fiscal risk of states, so investors demand a spread.

4. What makes an AT1 bond riskier than a senior bond of the same bank?

It is perpetual, subordinated, coupons can be cancelled and principal can be written down or converted when capital falls below triggers or on regulatory action.

1.4 Primary market: issuance processes

The primary market is the market for new issues, where an issuer sells newly created debt securities to investors and receives the funds. It is where borrowing takes place.

A. How the Government of India borrows (G-Secs and T-Bills)

  1. Borrowing programme – the Union Budget fixes the gross market borrowing. The government, in consultation with RBI, publishes a half-yearly issuance calendar for dated securities (amounts, tenor buckets, weeks) and a quarterly calendar for T-Bills.
  2. Auction notification – a few days before each auction RBI issues a press release naming the securities, notified amount, auction type (yield-based or price-based) and method (uniform or multiple price), and any greenshoe option.
  3. Bidding – bids are submitted electronically on RBI's E-Kuber platform within the bidding window. Two kinds of bids:
    • Competitive bids: large investors (banks, PDs, insurers, MFs, FPIs) state a price (or yield) and an amount.
    • Non-competitive bids: small investors bid only an amount and accept the price discovered in the auction. Up to 5% of the notified amount of dated securities is reserved for them (minimum ₹10,000, maximum ₹2 crore per bidder), allotted at the weighted-average price/yield of accepted competitive bids.
  4. Cut-off decision – RBI ranks competitive bids and sets the cut-off price/yield that raises the notified amount; it may reject bids that are out of line with the market, accept more via greenshoe, or devolve the shortfall on PDs.
  5. Result and settlement – results are announced the same day; settlement is usually T+1: funds move to the government's account and securities are credited to the bidders' SGL/CSGL or gilt accounts.
Auction typeUsed forWhat bidders quoteOutcome
Yield-basedNew security (coupon not yet known)YieldsCut-off yield becomes the coupon of the new security
Price-basedRe-issue of an existing security (coupon fixed)Prices per ₹100Cut-off price; yield implied from price

Other issuance tools: re-issues/reopenings (adding to an existing security to build liquidity), tap sales, switches and buybacks (exchanging or retiring old securities to smooth redemptions), greenshoe option (accepting more than notified), when-issued trading (trading a security between announcement and issue, which aids price discovery), and underwriting by Primary Dealers.

B. Primary Dealers and underwriting

A Primary Dealer (PD) is an RBI-authorised entity (standalone PD or bank-PD) that commits to support government borrowing by underwriting and bidding in primary auctions and to act as a market maker in the secondary market.

  • Minimum Underwriting Commitment (MUC) – each PD underwrites a minimum share of every dated-security auction.
  • Additional Competitive Underwriting (ACU) – the remainder is underwritten through a competitive auction among PDs, who bid underwriting commission rates.
  • Devolvement – if an auction is under-subscribed (or RBI rejects expensive bids), the unsold amount devolves on PDs in proportion to their underwriting commitments.
  • PDs also bid in T-Bill auctions, provide two-way quotes, and get access to RBI liquidity facilities.

C. RBI Retail Direct

Launched on 12 November 2021, the RBI Retail Direct scheme lets individuals open a Retail Direct Gilt (RDG) account directly with RBI through an online portal and (i) place non-competitive bids in primary auctions of T-Bills, dated G-Secs, SDLs and SGrBs and (ii) buy/sell in the secondary market through NDS-OM, without any fee.

D. How companies issue bonds

PointPublic issuePrivate placement
InvestorsGeneral public incl. retailSelected investors (mainly institutions, QIBs)
Offer documentProspectus (shelf prospectus possible)General information document / key information document
Time and costLonger, costlier (marketing, intermediaries)Faster, cheaper
MechanismApplication through exchanges; basis of allotmentElectronic Book Provider (EBP) platform of BSE/NSE for issues above SEBI's threshold
Share of Indian corporate issuanceSmallThe large majority
Common featuresCredit rating, debenture trustee, listing on an exchange, demat form, SEBI (NCS) Regulations 2021 disclosure norms

Private placement via EBP (steps): (1) board and shareholder approvals, appoint arranger, rating agency, trustee, registrar; (2) file placement documents with the exchange; (3) issuer announces the issue on the EBP platform with size, tenor and whether bids are on coupon/yield or price, and whether bidding is open or closed; (4) eligible investors bid electronically during the bidding window; (5) allotment on yield-time priority (lowest yield first, earlier bid first on ties); (6) pay-in through the clearing corporation; (7) credit of bonds in demat form and listing.

Short-term paper: CPs are issued through an Issuing and Paying Agent (IPA) bank; CDs are issued by banks directly to investors. Both are discounted instruments.

SEBI has made corporate bonds more accessible by registering Online Bond Platform Providers (OBPPs) and progressively reducing the minimum face value of privately placed debt securities (to ₹10,000 in 2024), so that individuals can buy corporate bonds in small ticket sizes.

The issuance process determines the borrowing cost of the government and companies. A well-designed auction and a liquid primary market lower the "new-issue premium" and therefore reduce the interest burden on the Budget.

1. Distinguish competitive and non-competitive bids.

Competitive bidders quote price/yield and amount and may get partial or no allotment; non-competitive bidders quote only the amount, are always allotted (within the reserved share) at the weighted-average price of accepted competitive bids.

2. When is a yield-based auction used?

For a new security whose coupon is not yet fixed; the cut-off yield becomes the coupon.

3. What is devolvement?

Allocation of the unsubscribed portion of an auction to Primary Dealers under their underwriting commitments.

1.5 Auction mechanisms: uniform vs discriminatory (multiple-price)

Picture a queue of buyers each shouting a price. RBI sells to the highest bidders first until the whole issue is sold. The last accepted price is the cut-off. The only question left is: what does each winner pay? Under a uniform-price auction everybody pays the cut-off price (one price for all). Under a discriminatory or multiple-price auction every winner pays the price he bid.

Uniform price auction: all successful bidders are allotted securities at the single cut-off price (or yield) at which the notified amount is fully sold.
Discriminatory (multiple price) auction: each successful bidder pays the price (or receives the yield) that he himself bid; bids above the cut-off are therefore filled at different prices.

Allotment procedure (both methods)

  1. Deduct the non-competitive portion from the notified amount to get the competitive amount.
  2. Arrange competitive bids from the highest price (lowest yield) downwards.
  3. Compute the cumulative amount bid.
  4. The cut-off is the price at which cumulative bids first reach or exceed the competitive amount.
  5. Bids above the cut-off are allotted in full; bids at the cut-off are allotted partially, on a pro-rata basis; bids below the cut-off are rejected.
  6. Price paid: cut-off for all (uniform) or own bid (multiple).
  7. Non-competitive bidders are allotted at the weighted-average price of accepted competitive bids (multiple-price) or at the cut-off (uniform-price).

Notified amount ₹1,000 crore; non-competitive bids ₹50 crore. Competitive bids: P1 ₹99.80 (₹300 cr), P2 ₹99.70 (₹400 cr), P3 ₹99.60 (₹500 cr), P4 ₹99.50 (₹200 cr).

  1. Competitive amount = 1,000 − 50 = ₹950 crore.
  2. Cumulative bids from the top: 300 → 700 → 1,200. The 950 mark is reached inside P3's bid, so cut-off price = ₹99.60.
  3. Allotment: P1 300, P2 400, P3 gets 950 − 700 = 250 of 500 (pro-rata 50%), P4 rejected.
  4. Weighted-average price (WAP) of accepted competitive bids = (300 × 99.80 + 400 × 99.70 + 250 × 99.60) / 950 = 94,720 / 950 = ₹99.7053.
  5. Multiple-price proceeds: P1 299.40 + P2 398.80 + P3 249.00 = ₹947.20 cr; non-competitive 50 × 0.997053 = ₹49.85 cr; total ₹997.05 crore.
  6. Uniform-price proceeds: everyone pays ₹99.60 ⇒ 1,000 × 0.9960 = ₹996.00 crore.

A new 10-year G-Sec (semi-annual coupons) of ₹10,000 crore is auctioned on yield basis; non-competitive portion ₹500 crore. Competitive bids: 7.02% (₹2,000 cr), 7.04% (₹3,000 cr), 7.05% (₹2,500 cr), 7.07% (₹3,000 cr), 7.10% (₹2,000 cr).

  1. Competitive amount = 10,000 − 500 = ₹9,500 crore. In a yield auction the lowest yields (highest prices) are accepted first.
  2. Cumulative: 2,000 → 5,000 → 7,500 → 10,500. The 9,500 mark falls in the 7.07% bid: cut-off yield = 7.07%, which becomes the coupon (the bond is named 7.07% GS 2036, say).
  3. Bid at 7.07% gets 9,500 − 7,500 = 2,000 of 3,000 = 66.67% pro-rata; the 7.10% bid is rejected.
  4. Uniform price: all winners pay ₹100.00 (coupon = cut-off yield).
  5. Multiple price: each winner pays the price of a 7.07% coupon bond at his own bid yield: 7.02% → ₹100.35; 7.04% → ₹100.21; 7.05% → ₹100.14; 7.07% → ₹100.00 (computed with the semi-annual pricing formula of Unit 2). Aggressive bidders pay more.

Uniform vs discriminatory: comparison and implications

BasisUniform priceDiscriminatory / multiple price
Price paidSame cut-off price for all winnersEach winner pays own bid
Winner's curseLow: bidding high only improves the chance of allotment, not the price paidHigh: winners who bid highest overpay relative to others
Bidding behaviourBidders bid closer to their true valuationBidders "shade" bids down to avoid overpaying
Government revenue (for given bids)Lower (everyone pays the lowest accepted price)Higher (captures each bidder's surplus)
Revenue after behaviour adjustsCan be as high or higher because of less bid shading and wider participationBid shading may erase the advantage
Transparency and fairnessMore transparent: one published price; small and less-informed bidders are not penalisedLess transparent; favours informed, large bidders
ParticipationEncourages wider participationMay discourage smaller bidders
Risk of collusion / squeezeSomewhat higher (bidders can bid high knowing they pay cut-off)Lower
Typical useSituations where price discovery is uncertain, e.g. new or long-tenor securities, some FRBsCommon for regular re-issues and T-Bills

Your syllabus says "uniform vs discriminatory"; RBI's press releases say "uniform price" and "multiple price". They are the same pair. Some textbooks call the uniform method a "Dutch auction". The auction notification specifies which method applies; RBI has used both.

RBI conducts G-Sec auctions on E-Kuber, announces results the same day (cut-off price/yield, weighted-average yield, amount accepted, and any devolvement on PDs) and has at times cancelled or partly accepted auctions when bids were too expensive, signalling its view on yields to the market.

In a yield-based auction, rank bids from the lowest yield upward (lowest yield = highest price). Ranking from the highest yield is the most common error. Also remember that pro-rata allotment applies only at the cut-off.

1. In Practice Q5, why does bidder E get only ₹1,000 crore?

Cumulative bids of A–D total ₹14,000 crore; only ₹1,000 crore of the ₹15,000 crore issue remains for E, who bid at the cut-off price.

2. Which method gives more revenue for a given set of bids, and why?

Discriminatory, because each winner pays his own (higher) bid rather than the lowest accepted price.

3. What is the winner's curse?

The tendency of the winning bidder in a common-value auction to overpay because winning means he had the most optimistic valuation; it is strongest in discriminatory auctions.

1.6 Secondary market: trading mechanisms and market dynamics

The secondary market is the market in which already-issued bonds are bought and sold among investors. It provides liquidity (the ability to exit before maturity) and price discovery (the market yield curve).

How G-Secs are traded

  • NDS-OM (Negotiated Dealing System – Order Matching): RBI-owned, anonymous, screen-based electronic order-matching platform for G-Secs, operated by CCIL; introduced in 2005. Buy and sell orders are matched on price-time priority. It has segments for standard lots (the market lot is ₹5 crore face value), odd lots (smaller amounts, used by retail and small investors) and request-for-quote. Most G-Sec trading volume takes place here.
  • OTC (telephone / broker) market: bilaterally negotiated deals, which must be reported on the NDS-OM reporting platform for transparency and settlement.
  • Stock exchanges: G-Secs can also be bought through NSE/BSE by retail investors, and via the Retail Direct portal (which connects to NDS-OM).

Clearing and settlement

  1. Trade is executed (NDS-OM) or reported (OTC).
  2. CCIL novation: CCIL becomes the buyer to every seller and the seller to every buyer (central counterparty), guaranteeing settlement.
  3. Multilateral netting: each participant's trades are netted to one fund and one security obligation per security.
  4. Delivery versus Payment (DvP): securities (in RBI's SGL) and funds (in RBI current accounts) move simultaneously, removing principal risk. India uses DvP-III (net funds and net securities).
  5. Standard settlement cycle for outright secondary G-Sec trades: T+1.

How corporate bonds are traded

  • Mostly OTC; trades must be reported to an exchange reporting platform.
  • Request for Quote (RFQ) platforms on NSE and BSE allow participants to seek and give quotes; trades settle through the exchange clearing corporations (NSE Clearing, ICCL), typically on T+1, on a DvP basis.
  • Retail investors can buy listed bonds on the exchange cash segment or through SEBI-registered online bond platforms.

Market dynamics: what moves bond prices and liquidity

DriverEffect on yields (and prices)
RBI policy repo rate and stanceRate hikes push short yields up (prices down); cuts pull them down
Inflation and inflation expectationsHigher expected inflation raises yields, especially long-term
Fiscal deficit and government borrowingHigher supply of G-Secs raises yields
System liquidity (LAF surplus/deficit)Surplus liquidity lowers short-term yields; deficit raises them
Global yields (e.g. US Treasuries) and FPI flowsRising US yields or FPI outflows raise Indian yields; index inclusion inflows lower them
Crude oil prices and the rupeeHigher oil → inflation and deficit worries → higher yields
Credit eventsWidening credit spreads for corporates (e.g. after the IL&FS default in 2018)
Demand from regulated investorsSLR changes, insurance and pension inflows affect particular maturities

Liquidity measures: trading volume/turnover, bid-ask spread (narrow = liquid), market depth (size available at the best quotes), and price impact. Liquidity is concentrated in benchmark (on-the-run) securities, especially the current 10-year G-Sec; older (off-the-run) securities trade less and carry a small liquidity premium.

In September 2018 the default by IL&FS group entities froze parts of the corporate bond and CP market, widened spreads for NBFCs and showed how a credit event spreads through the secondary market. In April 2020, Franklin Templeton India wound up six debt schemes after it could not sell lower-rated bonds in an illiquid market — an example of liquidity risk.

1. What is the standard market lot on NDS-OM?

₹5 crore face value; smaller trades use the odd-lot segment.

2. How does CCIL remove counterparty risk?

By novation: it becomes the legal counterparty to both sides and guarantees settlement, backed by margins and a settlement guarantee fund.

3. Why is the 10-year G-Sec more liquid than a 9-year-old off-the-run security?

It is the current benchmark: most trading, quotes and hedging concentrate there, so bid-ask spreads are narrow.

1.7 Repo markets and their role in liquidity management

A repo is a pawnshop loan with a government bond as the jewellery. A bank that needs cash tonight "sells" its G-Sec to a lender and agrees to buy it back tomorrow at a slightly higher price. The difference is the interest. The lender is safe because it holds the bond; the borrower gets cheap cash without really giving up the bond.

A repurchase agreement (repo) is a collateralised short-term borrowing in which one party sells securities to another with a simultaneous agreement to repurchase them on a specified future date at a pre-determined price. For the counterparty who buys first and sells back later it is a reverse repo (collateralised lending). The implicit interest rate is the repo rate.

Key features

  • Collateral: usually G-Secs, T-Bills, SDLs; corporate bonds in corporate bond repo.
  • Haircut: cash lent is less than the market value of collateral (margin protects the lender if the collateral falls in price).
  • Tenor: overnight (most common), term (few days to months).
  • General collateral (GC) vs special repo: GC accepts any eligible security; "special" repo is for a specific security in demand (often by short sellers), which can trade at a lower repo rate.
  • Ownership legally passes in the first leg; coupons during the repo belong economically to the original owner.

Types of repo in India

TypeParticipants / platformPurpose
RBI policy repo under the Liquidity Adjustment Facility (LAF)Banks borrow from RBI against G-SecsRBI's key instrument; the policy repo rate signals monetary policy
Variable Rate Repo / Reverse Repo (VRR / VRRR)RBI auctions for fixed amounts and tenorsFine-tunes system liquidity
Standing Deposit Facility (SDF)Banks park surplus funds with RBI without collateralFloor of the LAF corridor (introduced April 2022)
Marginal Standing Facility (MSF)Banks borrow overnight from RBI, may dip into SLR securitiesCeiling of the corridor; emergency funds at a penal rate
Market repo (bilateral)Banks, PDs, MFs, insurers on CCIL's CROMS platformInter-market funding against G-Secs
Tri-party repo – TREPSCCIL acts as tri-party agent (collateral management, settlement)Largest overnight money-market segment; replaced CBLO in November 2018
Corporate bond repoAgainst rated corporate bondsImproves corporate bond liquidity

Repo arithmetic (money-market convention, Actual/365 in India)

Cash lent = Market value of collateral × (1 − haircut)
Repo interest = Cash lent × repo rate × days / 365
Repurchase price (second-leg amount) = Cash lent + Repo interest
  1. Overnight repo: A bank borrows ₹100 crore overnight at 6.50%. Interest = 100,00,00,000 × 0.065 × 1/365 = ₹1,78,082. Repurchase amount = ₹100,01,78,082.
  2. Term repo with haircut: Collateral worth ₹105 crore, haircut 5%, 7 days at 6.25%. Cash lent = 105 × 0.95 = ₹99.75 crore. Interest = 99,75,00,000 × 0.0625 × 7/365 = ₹11,95,634. Second-leg amount = ₹99,86,95,634 (≈ ₹99.87 crore).
  3. TREPS: A mutual fund lends ₹50 crore for 3 days at 6.40%: interest = 50,00,00,000 × 0.064 × 3/365 = ₹2,63,014.

Role of repo in liquidity management

  1. Monetary policy transmission: RBI sets the policy repo rate; the LAF corridor (SDF floor – repo – MSF ceiling) keeps the overnight rate (the Weighted Average Call Rate, RBI's operating target) close to the repo rate.
  2. Injecting / absorbing liquidity: repo and VRR inject cash into banks; SDF and VRRR absorb surplus.
  3. Funding bond positions: dealers and PDs finance their inventory of G-Secs cheaply through repo, which lets them make markets.
  4. Safe short-term investment: MFs and corporates park surplus cash in TREPS/reverse repo with collateral protection.
  5. Enabling short sales: a short seller borrows the security through a reverse repo to deliver it.
  6. Boosting demand for G-Secs: because G-Secs can be "repoed", they are more liquid and attractive; this lowers government borrowing costs.
  7. Crisis support: special operations such as Long-Term Repo Operations (LTROs) in 2020 provided term funding to banks during COVID-19.

"Repo" and "reverse repo" describe the same transaction from two sides. When RBI "conducts a repo" it lends to banks (banks do repo, RBI does reverse repo in the economic sense). In exam answers, say clearly who is borrowing cash.

1. What is a haircut and who does it protect?

The percentage by which cash lent is less than collateral value; it protects the cash lender against a fall in collateral price or borrower default.

2. What replaced CBLO?

Tri-party repo (TREPS) run by CCIL, from November 2018.

3. Which rate is the floor of the LAF corridor today?

The Standing Deposit Facility (SDF) rate, since April 2022.

Unit 1 exam kit

Introduction. RBI sells G-Secs and T-Bills through auctions on its E-Kuber platform. After ranking bids and fixing a cut-off, two pricing rules are possible.

Uniform price auction: every successful bidder pays the single cut-off price (or receives the cut-off yield). Discriminatory (multiple price) auction: every successful bidder pays the price he bid.

Implications. (i) Revenue: for the same bids the discriminatory method raises more money, but bidders shade their bids, so the advantage may disappear. (ii) Winner's curse: severe under discriminatory, mild under uniform. (iii) Transparency and participation: uniform pricing is simpler and fairer for small bidders, encouraging participation. (iv) Price discovery: uniform bids reflect true valuations more closely. (v) Collusion: uniform auctions are somewhat more exposed to strategic bidding.

Example. In Practice Q5 (₹15,000 crore), proceeds are ₹15,015 crore under uniform and ₹15,053.5 crore under discriminatory pricing.

Conclusion. RBI uses both and specifies the method in each notification, generally preferring uniform pricing where price discovery is uncertain.

Meaning. A repo is a sale of securities with an agreement to repurchase at a higher price on a future date – a collateralised short-term loan; the reverse repo is the lender's side.

Roles. (1) Policy transmission via the LAF repo rate and corridor (SDF–repo–MSF); (2) liquidity injection/absorption through repo/VRR and SDF/VRRR; (3) funding of dealers' and PDs' bond inventories; (4) safe parking of surplus cash via TREPS; (5) enabling short sales and market-making; (6) raising the attractiveness (and lowering the yield) of G-Secs because they can be financed; (7) crisis support such as the 2020 LTROs.

Indian example. TREPS, run by CCIL, is the largest overnight segment and replaced CBLO in 2018.

Conclusion. Repo is the plumbing that links RBI's policy rate to bond yields and keeps the bond market liquid.

Introduction. The bond market is where debt securities are issued (primary) and traded (secondary). India's bond market is dominated by government securities, with a growing corporate segment.

1. Structure. (a) G-Sec market: dated securities, T-Bills (91/182/364-day), CMBs, SDLs – regulated by RBI. (b) Corporate bond market: PSU, bank, NBFC and corporate bonds/NCDs – regulated by SEBI. (c) Money market: call money, CPs, CDs, repo, TREPS.

2. Primary vs secondary. G-Secs are auctioned on E-Kuber with PD underwriting; corporates use public issues or private placement on EBP. Secondary trading happens on NDS-OM (G-Secs), OTC with reporting, and NSE/BSE RFQ platforms; settlement through CCIL or exchange clearing corporations on T+1, DvP.

3. Participants. Issuers (GoI, states, PSUs, corporates); investors (banks due to SLR, insurers, PF/pension funds, MFs, FPIs, retail via RBI Retail Direct); intermediaries (PDs, brokers, arrangers, rating agencies, trustees); infrastructure (CCIL, NSDL/CDSL, FBIL, FIMMDA); regulators (RBI, SEBI, IRDAI, PFRDA).

4. Instruments. T-Bills, CMBs, dated G-Secs, FRBs, IIBs, STRIPS, SDLs, SGBs, FRSB 2020, SGrBs, PSU bonds, NCDs, CPs, CDs, Tier-2/AT1 bonds, municipal bonds, masala bonds.

5. Features/issues. Liquidity concentrated in benchmarks; corporate market shallow and AAA-heavy; low retail participation, improving with Retail Direct and OBPPs; index inclusion (J.P. Morgan GBI-EM from June 2024) widening the investor base.

Conclusion. A deep bond market lowers the cost of funding the government and infrastructure; reforms in auctions, trading and retail access are steadily deepening it.

Introduction. RBI, as debt manager, issues G-Secs on behalf of the Government through auctions.

Steps. (1) Borrowing programme and half-yearly issuance calendar; (2) auction notification specifying amount, security, yield/price-based and uniform/multiple method, greenshoe; (3) bidding on E-Kuber: competitive bids (price/yield + amount) and non-competitive bids (up to 5% of notified amount, ₹10,000–₹2 crore, allotted at weighted-average price); (4) underwriting by PDs through MUC and ACU; (5) cut-off determination and pro-rata allotment at the cut-off; devolvement on PDs if needed; (6) results announced the same day; (7) settlement on T+1, securities credited to SGL/CSGL/RDG accounts; (8) securities then trade on NDS-OM.

Example. A new 10-year security is auctioned on yield basis; the cut-off yield (e.g. 7.07%) becomes the coupon.

Conclusion. The process balances cost minimisation for the Government with fair access for all investors.

Structure your answer as: (a) ranking table with cumulative amounts; (b) cut-off price with reason; (c) allotment table with pro-rata; (d) proceeds under uniform and discriminatory methods; (e) comparison and recommendation on transparency; (f) conclusion on implications for RBI. The full solution is in Practice Q5.

Unit 2 — The Mathematics of Bond Valuation

Syllabus: TVM; valuation of coupon and zero-coupon bonds; price-yield conventions; YTM, YTC; pull-to-par; duration, modified duration, convexity; Malkiel's theorems and the duration theorem; credit, interest-rate and reinvestment risk. (10 teaching hours, CO2) — the most numerical unit.

Notation used throughout (same as your Malkiel handout): P = bond price; C = coupon per period (annual coupon unless stated); F = face value; y = yield to maturity (decimal); n = number of periods to maturity; t = time of a cash flow; CFt = cash flow at t; m = coupon payments per year; DM = Macaulay duration; DMod = modified duration; C (in convexity formulas) = convexity; Δy = change in yield (decimal: 1% = 0.01; 1 basis point = 0.0001).

2.1 Time value of money (TVM)

₹100 today is worth more than ₹100 next year, because today's ₹100 can be invested to earn interest. So money received in the future must be discounted (shrunk) to compare it with money today. Bond valuation is nothing but TVM applied to a list of promised payments.

The time value of money is the principle that a sum of money has a greater value now than the same sum in the future, because of its earning capacity; future cash flows are converted into present value by discounting at the required rate of return.

FV = PV × (1 + r)n      PV = FV / (1 + r)n
PV of annuity = C × [1 − (1 + r)−n] / r    (PVIFA)
With m compounding periods a year: FV = PV × (1 + r/m)m·n;   EAR = (1 + r/m)m − 1

PV = present value; FV = future value; r = rate per year; n = years; C = equal periodic payment; m = compounding frequency; EAR = effective annual rate. The coupons of a bond are an annuity; the face value is a single lump sum.

  1. Future value: ₹1,00,000 at 8% for 5 years: 1,00,000 × 1.085 = 1,00,000 × 1.469328 = ₹1,46,932.81.
  2. Present value: ₹1,00,000 receivable in 5 years at 8%: 1,00,000 / 1.469328 = ₹68,058.32.
  3. Semi-annual compounding: ₹1,00,000 at 8% compounded half-yearly for 5 years: 1,00,000 × 1.0410 = ₹1,48,024.43; EAR = 1.042 − 1 = 8.16%.
  4. Annuity: PV of ₹5,000 a year for 5 years at 8%: PVIFA = (1 − 1.08−5)/0.08 = 3.992710; PV = 5,000 × 3.992710 = ₹19,963.55.
1. Why is EAR higher than the nominal rate when compounding is semi-annual?

Interest earned in the first half-year itself earns interest in the second half (interest on interest).

2. What is PVIFA at 10% for 6 years?

(1 − 1.1−6)/0.10 = 4.355261.

2.2 Valuing coupon bonds and zero-coupon bonds

The value (intrinsic value) of a bond is the present value of all its expected future cash flows – coupons and redemption value – discounted at the investor's required rate of return (the market yield for bonds of similar risk and maturity).

Coupon bond (annual coupons)

P = C/(1+y) + C/(1+y)2 + ... + (C + F)/(1+y)n  =  C × [1 − (1+y)−n]/y + F/(1+y)n

Semi-annual coupons (Indian G-Secs): use C/2 per period, y/2 per period and 2n periods:

P = (C/2) × [1 − (1+y/2)−2n]/(y/2) + F/(1+y/2)2n

Zero-coupon bond (T-Bills, STRIPS, deep-discount bonds):

P = F / (1+y)n    ⇒   y = (F/P)1/n − 1

Decision rule: intrinsic value > market price ⇒ bond is undervalued (buy); value < price ⇒ overvalued (avoid/sell).

RelationshipPrice vs face valueCalledAlso true
Coupon rate = yieldP = FPar bondCoupon rate = current yield = YTM
Coupon rate > yieldP > FPremium bondCoupon rate > current yield > YTM
Coupon rate < yieldP < FDiscount bondCoupon rate < current yield < YTM

10-year bond, face ₹1,000, coupon 7.5% (₹75 a year), required return 8%.

  1. PVIFA(8%, 10) = (1 − 1.08−10)/0.08 = 6.710081.
  2. PV of coupons = 75 × 6.710081 = ₹503.26.
  3. PV of face value = 1,000 / 1.0810 = 1,000 / 2.158925 = ₹463.19.
  4. P = 503.26 + 463.19 = ₹966.45 – a discount bond because coupon (7.5%) < yield (8%).
  1. Per period: coupon = ₹37.50, rate = 4%, periods = 20.
  2. PVIFA(4%, 20) = (1 − 1.04−20)/0.04 = 13.590326; PV of coupons = 37.50 × 13.590326 = ₹509.64.
  3. PV of face value = 1,000 / 1.0420 = ₹456.39.
  4. P = 509.64 + 456.39 = ₹966.02 (slightly lower than annual because the discount rate also compounds semi-annually).
  1. 5-year zero, face ₹1,000, yield 7.5%: P = 1,000/1.0755 = ₹696.56. With semi-annual compounding: 1,000/1.037510 = ₹692.02.
  2. 5-year bond, 9% annual coupon, yield 7%: P = 90 × PVIFA(7%,5) + 1,000/1.075 = ₹1,082.00 – a premium bond because coupon > yield.

A 10-year zero-coupon bond of face ₹1,000 costs ₹500. y = (1,000/500)1/10 − 1 = 20.1 − 1 = 7.18% (the "rule of 72" check: money doubles in 10 years at about 7.2%).

Course numericals of this type: Practice Q1, Q2, Q3, Q6, Q8.

1. A 5-year zero of face ₹1,000 at 9%: price?

1,000/1.095 = 1,000/1.538624 = ₹649.93.

2. If a bond sells above face value, is its YTM above or below its coupon rate?

Below. A premium price means the yield is less than the coupon rate.

3. A bond's intrinsic value is ₹1,040 and its price ₹1,010. Buy or avoid?

Buy – it is undervalued by ₹30.

2.3 Price-yield conventions (how prices and yields are quoted)

Bonds pay coupons only on coupon dates, but they trade on every day in between. If you buy a bond two months after a coupon date, the seller has "earned" two months of coupon that you will collect in full on the next coupon date. So you must compensate the seller: that compensation is accrued interest. The market quotes a clean price and adds accrued interest at settlement.

Clean price (flat price): quoted price excluding accrued interest. Dirty price (full or invoice price): the amount paid in full = clean price + accrued interest. Accrued interest: the portion of the next coupon that belongs to the seller for the days since the last coupon date up to (but not including) the settlement date.

Accrued interest = Coupon per period × (Days from last coupon date to settlement / Days in coupon period)
Dirty price = Clean price + Accrued interest
Dirty price between coupon dates = Σk=1..N CFk / (1 + y/m)k − 1 + w,   w = days to next coupon / days in period

Conventions in the Indian market

ItemConvention
Price quotationPer ₹100 of face value, clean price, to 2–4 decimals (e.g. ₹101.2550)
Yield quotationAnnualised YTM in % (for semi-annual bonds, 2 × the half-yearly rate – the "bond-equivalent" or semi-annual yield); changes in basis points (1 bp = 0.01%)
Dated G-Secs and SDLsSemi-annual coupons; 30/360 day count for accrued interest
Corporate bonds (SEBI-mandated)Usually annual coupons; Actual/Actual day count
T-Bills, CPs, CDs, repo, call moneyActual/365
SettlementT+1 for secondary G-Sec outright trades; money-market trades often T+0
Coupon holidaysIf a coupon date is a holiday, payment is made on the previous working day for G-Secs

30/360 day count (each month = 30 days, year = 360): Days = 360(Y2−Y1) + 30(M2−M1) + (D2−D1), with day 31 treated as 30.
Actual/Actual: actual days elapsed / actual days in the coupon period (or year).
Actual/365: actual days / 365.

A 7.30% G-Sec pays coupons on 15 January and 15 July. It is bought for settlement on 10 October at a clean price of ₹101.25.

  1. Days from 15 July to 10 October on 30/360 = 30 × (10 − 7) + (10 − 15) = 90 − 5 = 85 days.
  2. Accrued interest per ₹100 = 7.30 × 85/360 = ₹1.7236 (equivalently 3.65 × 85/180).
  3. Dirty price = 101.25 + 1.7236 = ₹102.9736 per ₹100. On a ₹5 crore trade the buyer pays 5,00,00,000 × 1.029736 = ₹5,14,86,806.

An 8.50% NCD (annual coupon on 1 April) settles on 15 August 2026.

  1. Actual days from 1 April 2026 to 15 August 2026 = 136; days in the coupon year (1 Apr 2026 – 1 Apr 2027) = 365.
  2. Accrued interest = 8.50 × 136/365 = ₹3.1671 per ₹100, i.e. ₹3,167.12 per ₹1 lakh face value.

An 8% semi-annual bond (face ₹100) has 4 coupons left; the next coupon is 60 days away in a 180-day period (30/360). Yield 7% (3.5% per half-year). Find dirty and clean price.

  1. w = 60/180 = 0.3333. Exponents are 0.3333, 1.3333, 2.3333, 3.3333.
  2. PVs: 4/1.0350.3333 = 3.9544; 4/1.0351.3333 = 3.8207; 4/1.0352.3333 = 3.6915; 104/1.0353.3333 = 92.7325.
  3. Dirty price = 3.9544 + 3.8207 + 3.6915 + 92.7325 = ₹104.1991.
  4. Accrued interest = 4 × 120/180 = ₹2.6667 (120 days have passed since the last coupon).
  5. Clean (quoted) price = 104.1991 − 2.6667 = ₹101.5324.

Class numericals usually assume annual coupons and valuation on a coupon date, so there is no accrued interest. Real Indian G-Secs pay semi-annually and trade between coupon dates; exams may ask for semi-annual pricing – read the question. In Excel, the basis argument sets the day count: 0 = US 30/360, 1 = Actual/Actual, 2 = Actual/360, 3 = Actual/365, 4 = European 30/360.

The clean price does not jump on a coupon date; the dirty price does (it drops by the coupon). That is why the market quotes clean prices: they move only because of yield changes and pull-to-par, not because a coupon has just been paid.

1. Which day count applies to Indian G-Secs, T-Bills and corporate bonds?

G-Secs 30/360; T-Bills (money market) Actual/365; corporate bonds Actual/Actual.

2. Clean price ₹99.40, accrued interest ₹1.15. What does the buyer pay per ₹100?

Dirty price = ₹100.55.

2.4 Yield measures: current yield, YTM, YTC, realised yield

(a) Coupon (nominal) yield and current yield

Coupon rate = Annual coupon / Face value     Current yield = (Annual coupon / Market price) × 100

Current yield measures only the income return; it ignores capital gain/loss to maturity and the time value of money.

  1. (Handout) Coupon ₹80, price ₹950: CY = 80/950 = 8.42%.
  2. Coupon ₹75, price ₹950: CY = 75/950 = 7.89%.
  3. (Handout) Coupon ₹80 at prices ₹1,000 / ₹900 / ₹1,100: CY = 8.00% / 8.89% / 7.27% – price down, yield up.

(b) Yield to maturity (YTM)

Yield to maturity is the single discount rate that equates the present value of all promised cash flows of a bond (coupons and redemption value) to its current market price. It is the bond's internal rate of return if bought today and held to maturity.

Assumptions of YTM: (1) the bond is held to maturity; (2) all coupons and principal are paid in full and on time (no default); (3) every coupon is reinvested at the YTM itself. If assumption (3) fails, the investor's actual return differs – this is reinvestment risk.

Exact YTM: solve for y in P = Σ C/(1+y)t + F/(1+y)n (trial and error, interpolation, Excel RATE/YIELD/IRR).

Approximation formula (use in exams unless "exact" is asked):

YTM ≈ [C + (F − P)/n] / [(F + P)/2]

C = annual coupon; F = face/redemption value; P = price; n = years to maturity. Numerator = average annual return (coupon + amortised gain/loss); denominator = average investment.

Linear interpolation between two trial rates rL (price PL above market) and rH (price PH below market):

YTM ≈ rL + [(PL − P)/(PL − PH)] × (rH − rL)

8-year bond, face ₹1,000, coupon 8% (₹80), price ₹950.

  1. Approximation: [80 + (1,000 − 950)/8] / [(1,000 + 950)/2] = (80 + 6.25)/975 = 86.25/975 = 8.85%.
  2. Trial rates: at 8% the price is ₹1,000.00 (par, because coupon = yield); at 9% the price is 80 × PVIFA(9%,8) + 1,000/1.098 = ₹944.65. Market price ₹950 lies between, so YTM is between 8% and 9%.
  3. Interpolation: 8% + [(1,000 − 950)/(1,000 − 944.65)] × 1% = 8% + (50/55.35) × 1% = 8% + 0.90% = 8.90%.
  4. Exact (Excel RATE): =RATE(8,80,-950,1000) = 8.90%. Check: price at 8.90% = ₹950.00.
  5. Conclusion: the approximation is within 0.05 percentage points; interpolation is almost exact.

6-year 8% bond paying semi-annually, price ₹1,040. Solve 1,040 = 40 × PVIFA(r,12) + 1,000/(1+r)12 for the half-yearly rate r: r = 3.584%. Annualised (bond-equivalent) YTM = 2 × 3.584% = 7.17%. Excel: =RATE(12,40,-1040,1000)*2.

(c) Yield to call (YTC) and yield to worst

Yield to call is the discount rate that equates the present value of the cash flows up to the first call date – coupons plus the call price – to the bond's current price. Yield to worst is the lowest of YTM and all possible YTCs; conservative investors use it for callable bonds.

P = Σt=1..nc C/(1+YTC)t + Call price/(1+YTC)nc    Approx: YTC ≈ [C + (CP − P)/nc] / [(CP + P)/2]

nc = years to first call; CP = call price (often face value plus a call premium).

10-year 9% bond, price ₹1,080, callable after 5 years at ₹1,030.

  1. YTM (exact): solve 1,080 = 90 × PVIFA(y,10) + 1,000/(1+y)10 ⇒ 7.82%.
  2. YTC approximation: [90 + (1,030 − 1,080)/5] / [(1,030 + 1,080)/2] = (90 − 10)/1,055 = 7.58%.
  3. YTC exact: solve 1,080 = 90 × PVIFA(y,5) + 1,030/(1+y)5 ⇒ 7.54%.
  4. Yield to worst = min(7.82%, 7.54%) = 7.54%. Because the bond trades at a premium and rates have fallen, the issuer is likely to call; the investor should expect about 7.54%, not 7.82%.

(d) Realised (compound) yield and horizon return

Realised compound yield (RCY) is the actual annual return earned over the holding period, given the actual reinvestment rate of coupons and the actual sale/redemption value: RCY = (Total terminal value / Price paid)1/n − 1.

A 3-year 8% bond bought at par (₹1,000), held to maturity. Terminal value = 80(1+r)2 + 80(1+r) + 1,080.

Reinvestment rate rTerminal value (₹)Interest-on-interest (₹)Realised yield
6%1,254.6914.697.86%
8% (= YTM)1,259.7119.718.00%
10%1,264.8024.808.15%

RCY at 6% = (1,254.69/1,000)1/3 − 1 = 7.86%. Only if coupons are reinvested at the YTM does the investor truly earn the YTM.

Buy a 5-year 8% bond at par (₹1,000). After 2 years the yield falls to 7%; coupons were reinvested at 7%; the bond is sold.

  1. Sale price (3 years left, 7%): 80 × PVIFA(7%,3) + 1,000/1.073 = ₹1,026.24.
  2. Coupons with reinvestment: 80 × 1.07 + 80 = ₹165.60.
  3. Total terminal value = 1,026.24 + 165.60 = ₹1,191.84.
  4. Annualised horizon return = (1,191.84/1,000)1/2 − 1 = 9.17% – above the 8% YTM because of the capital gain from falling yields.
Yield measureCaptures coupon?Captures capital gain/loss?Time value / reinvestment?Best used for
Coupon rateYes (on face)NoNoDescribing the contract
Current yieldYes (on price)NoNoIncome investors; quick comparison
YTMYesYes, to maturityYes; assumes reinvestment at YTMStandard comparison of bonds
YTC / yield to worstYesYes, to callYesCallable bonds, AT1/perpetuals
Realised / horizon yieldYesYes, at actual sale priceYes, at actual reinvestment ratePerformance measurement; scenario planning

Bank AT1 perpetual bonds in India are usually traded and quoted on a yield-to-call basis, because investors expected the bank to call them at the first call date. When a call is uncertain, investors must also consider the yield if the bond is not called – the logic of yield to worst.

1. Give the three assumptions behind YTM.

Held to maturity; no default (all payments on time); coupons reinvested at the YTM.

2. Approximate YTM: 5-year, 10% coupon, price ₹960.

[100 + 40/5]/[(1,000+960)/2] = 108/980 = 11.02%.

3. For a premium callable bond, which is usually lower, YTM or YTC?

YTC, because the call cuts short the stream of above-market coupons and the premium is amortised over fewer years.

2.5 Pull-to-par effect

The pull-to-par effect is the tendency of a bond's price to converge towards its face value as it approaches maturity, assuming the yield stays unchanged. Premium bonds fall towards par; discount bonds rise towards par; at maturity the price equals face value.

At maturity the bond is worth exactly its face value – no more, no less. A discount bond gives its extra return through this slow climb to par; a premium bond gives back its extra coupons through the slow slide to par. The closer the maturity, the less time there is for the coupon difference to matter.

Practice Q3 bond (face ₹1,000, 9% coupon, 6 years, yield 10%). Price with n years left, yield constant:

Years left6543210
Price (₹)956.45962.09968.30975.13982.64990.911,000
Capital gain in year (₹)–5.646.216.837.518.269.09

Each year: coupon ₹90 + capital gain = 10% of the opening price (e.g. year 1: 90 + 5.64 = 95.64 = 10% of 956.45). This proves that a discount bond's return comes partly from pull-to-par. The rise accelerates near maturity.

Pull-to-par explains why bond portfolios show accounting gains or losses even when yields do not move, why target-maturity debt funds reduce interest-rate risk as they age, and why held-to-maturity accounting amortises premiums and discounts.

1. Does pull-to-par happen if yields change?

The price still ends at par at maturity, but the path in between also reflects yield changes; the pure pull-to-par effect assumes a constant yield.

2. A premium bond with a constant yield: price rises or falls each year?

Falls, towards par.

2.6 Malkiel's bond price theorems

A bond's coupons and face value never change. Only the discount rate (yield) changes. Malkiel's theorems answer two questions about that: which way does the price move when yields move, and which bonds move the most? Your handout's memory aid is I – M – C – Y – C: Inverse, Maturity, Coupon, Yield level, Convexity.

Malkiel's bond price theorems (Burton Malkiel, 1962) are a set of propositions describing how the prices of option-free bonds respond to changes in market yields, and how maturity, coupon and the level of yields determine the size of that response.

Theorem 1 – Inverse relationship

Statement: Bond prices move inversely to yields: y ↑ ⇒ P ↓; y ↓ ⇒ P ↑ (dP/dy < 0).

(Handout Worked Example 1) 3-year, 8% coupon, face ₹1,000.

YearCash flowDF at 8%PV at 8%PV at 10%PV at 6%
1800.925974.0772.7375.47
2800.857368.5966.1271.20
31,0800.7938857.34811.42906.79
Price1,000.00950.261,053.46

Yield 8% → par; yield 10% → price falls to ₹950.26; yield 6% → price rises to ₹1,053.46.

Theorem 2 – Longer maturity, greater sensitivity

Statement: For the same coupon and yield, a given change in yield causes a larger percentage price change for a longer-maturity bond.

(Handout) Two 8% bonds at 8% yield; yield rises to 9%. 3-year: ₹1,000 → ₹974.69 (−2.53%). 15-year: ₹1,000 → ₹919.39 (−8.06%). The 15-year bond falls more than three times as much.

Corollary (Malkiel's original third theorem): sensitivity increases with maturity at a decreasing rate. For an 8% bond when yield rises 8% → 9%: 1 year −0.92%, 5 years −3.89%, 10 years −6.42%, 20 years −9.13%, 30 years −10.27%. Going from 1 to 10 years adds 5.5 percentage points of loss; going from 20 to 30 years adds only 1.1.

Theorem 3 – Lower coupon, greater sensitivity

Statement: For the same maturity and yield, a lower-coupon bond has a larger percentage price change for a given yield change.

(Handout) Two 10-year bonds at 8%; yield rises to 9%. 4% coupon: ₹731.60 → ₹679.12 (−7.17%). 10% coupon: ₹1,134.20 → ₹1,064.18 (−6.17%). The low-coupon bond has more of its value in the distant face-value payment, so it behaves like a longer bond.

Theorem 4 – Lower starting yield, greater sensitivity

Statement: For a given bond, an equal change in yield causes a larger percentage price change when the initial yield is lower.

10-year 8% coupon bond. Case A: 5% → 6%: ₹1,231.65 → ₹1,147.20 (−6.86%). Case B: 10% → 11%: ₹877.11 → ₹823.32 (−6.13%). Same 1 percentage-point rise, bigger fall from the low-yield start.

(Handout caution) "5% → 6%" and "10% → 11%" are both 1 percentage-point (100 bp) changes, even though they are 20% and 10% relative changes. Theorem 4 compares equal percentage-point changes.

Theorem 5 – Convexity: gains exceed losses

Statement: For a given bond, a decrease in yield produces a larger percentage price increase than the percentage price decrease caused by an equal increase in yield.

20-year 8% bond at 8% (price ₹1,000). Scenario A: yield 8% → 9%: price ₹908.71 (−9.13%). Scenario B: yield 8% → 7%: price ₹1,105.94 (+10.59%). Same 1-point move; the gain (10.59%) beats the loss (9.13%). See the curved price-yield graph in 2.2.

No.Theorem (handout order)RuleIllustration above
1Inverse relationshipy ↑ ⇒ P ↓₹1,000 → ₹950.26 when y 8% → 10%
2MaturityMaturity ↑ ⇒ sensitivity ↑ (at a decreasing rate)−2.53% (3y) vs −8.06% (15y)
3CouponCoupon ↓ ⇒ sensitivity ↑−7.17% (4%) vs −6.17% (10%)
4Yield levelInitial yield ↓ ⇒ sensitivity ↑−6.86% (from 5%) vs −6.13% (from 10%)
5ConvexityGain from y↓ > loss from equal y↑+10.59% vs −9.13%

Malkiel's original 1962 paper states five theorems in a different order: (1) inverse relationship; (2) longer maturity ⇒ larger price change; (3) that sensitivity increases at a decreasing rate as maturity lengthens; (4) price rise from a yield fall exceeds the price fall from an equal yield rise; (5) higher coupon ⇒ smaller percentage change. The "lower initial yield ⇒ higher sensitivity" rule is usually credited to Homer and Liebowitz and is often listed as a sixth property. In your exam, use your handout's I-M-C-Y-C order, and mention the "decreasing rate" point as an extra for full marks.

Portfolio managers use these theorems qualitatively: expecting rates to fall, buy long-maturity, low-coupon bonds (maximum price gain); expecting rates to rise, shift to short-maturity, high-coupon bonds (minimum loss).

1. (Handout) Bond A: 3-year 8%; Bond B: 15-year 8%. Which has more interest-rate risk?

Bond B (theorem 2).

2. (Handout) Bond A: 10-year 4%; Bond B: 10-year 10%. Which has more interest-rate risk?

Bond A (theorem 3).

3. If yield falls by 1%, will the price rise by exactly the same percentage as it falls when yield rises by 1%?

No – the rise is larger, because of convexity (theorem 5).

4. To gain the most from an expected fall in rates, which bond: 5-year 9% or 25-year zero?

25-year zero: longest maturity and lowest (zero) coupon.

2.7 Duration: Macaulay, modified, effective, dollar duration and DV01

Malkiel tells you which bond is more sensitive; duration tells you how much. Picture the bond's cash flows as weights on a see-saw, each placed at its payment date and as heavy as its present value. The point where the see-saw balances is the Macaulay duration: the "centre of gravity" of the bond's cash flows in time.

Macaulay duration is the present-value-weighted average time (in years) at which an investor receives a bond's cash flows; each time t is weighted by the share of the bond's price contributed by the cash flow at t.
Modified duration is Macaulay duration divided by (1 + y/m); it measures the approximate percentage change in bond price for a 1 percentage-point (100 bp) change in yield.

DM = [ Σ t × CFt/(1+y)t ] / P    (years)
DMod = DM / (1 + y)    (annual)      DMod = DM / (1 + y/m)    (m payments a year)
Dollar (rupee) duration = DMod × P      DV01 = PVBP = DMod × P × 0.0001
Effective duration = (P− − P+) / (2 × P0 × Δy)
Zero-coupon bond: DM = n      Perpetuity: DM = (1 + y)/y      Portfolio: Dp = Σ wi Di (wi = market-value weights)

t = time of cash flow in years (for semi-annual bonds compute in half-years and divide the result by 2); CFt = cash flow; y = YTM; P = price; P−/P+ = prices after the yield falls/rises by Δy. DV01 (dollar value of 01) or PVBP (price value of a basis point) = rupee change in price for a 1 bp change in yield. Effective duration is used for bonds with embedded options, where cash flows change with yields.

Year tCash flowPV at 10%t × PV
110090.9190.91
210082.64165.28
31,100826.452,479.35
Total1,000.002,735.54

DM = 2,735.54 / 1,000 = 2.74 years (less than the 3-year maturity because coupons return cash earlier). DMod = 2.7355/1.10 = 2.49.

tCFDF = 1/1.1tPVt × PVt(t+1)CF/1.1t+2
1800.90909172.727372.7273120.2104
2800.82644666.1157132.2314327.8465
3800.75131560.1052180.3156596.0845
4800.68301354.6411218.5643903.1583
51,0800.620921670.59503,352.975116,626.3230
Total924.18433,956.813718,573.6226
  1. Price P = ₹924.18.
  2. DM = 3,956.8137 / 924.1843 = 4.2814 years.
  3. DMod = 4.2814 / 1.10 = 3.8922 ⇒ price changes about 3.89% for a 1-point yield change.
  4. Rupee duration = 3.8922 × 924.18 = ₹3,597.10; DV01 = 3,597.10 × 0.0001 = ₹0.3597 per bond (a 1 bp rise cuts the price by about 36 paise).
  5. Effective duration check with ±50 bp: P at 9.5% = ₹942.40, P at 10.5% = ₹906.43; (942.40 − 906.43)/(2 × 924.18 × 0.005) = 3.89 – the same as modified duration, as expected for an option-free bond.
  6. (The last column is used for convexity in 2.9.)
  1. 4-year 8% bond paying semi-annually at an 8% yield (priced at par): computing in half-years gives 7.0021 periods; DM = 7.0021/2 = 3.50 years; DMod = 3.5010/1.04 = 3.37.
  2. A 7-year zero-coupon bond: DM = 7 years exactly.
  3. A perpetual bond at 8%: DM = 1.08/0.08 = 13.5 years – finite even though the bond never matures.
  4. Portfolio: ₹40 lakh in bonds of duration 2.5, ₹35 lakh of duration 6.0, ₹25 lakh of duration 9.0. Dp = 0.40(2.5) + 0.35(6.0) + 0.25(9.0) = 1.00 + 2.10 + 2.25 = 5.35 years.
  5. (Handout) DM = 6.2 years, y = 8%: DMod = 6.2/1.08 = 5.74.

SEBI requires debt mutual funds to disclose the Macaulay duration of each scheme's portfolio, and categorises schemes by duration: e.g. a "short duration fund" must keep Macaulay duration between 1 and 3 years, and a "medium duration fund" between 3 and 4 years. Investors use the disclosed duration to judge how much the fund's NAV will move when RBI changes rates.

Your handout writes DMod = DM/(1+y) and applies Δy in decimals. Industry analytics (Bloomberg's YAS screen) report "modified duration" and "risk" (= rupee duration per 100 × 0.01) and DV01 in currency per million face value; the ideas are identical.

2.8 The duration theorem and the properties (rules) of duration

The duration theorem states that, for small changes in yield, the percentage change in a bond's price is approximately equal to minus its modified duration multiplied by the change in yield.

ΔP/P ≈ −DMod × Δy      ΔP ≈ −DMod × P × Δy      equivalently ΔP/P ≈ −DM × Δy/(1+y)

Δy in decimals (0.50% = 0.005). The minus sign reflects Malkiel's theorem 1.

  1. A: P = ₹1,000, DMod = 6, yield rises 0.50%: ΔP/P ≈ −6 × 0.005 = −3% ⇒ −₹30 ⇒ new price ≈ ₹970.
  2. B: P = ₹2,000, DMod = 8, yield falls 0.25%: ΔP/P ≈ −8 × (−0.0025) = +2% ⇒ +₹40 ⇒ new price ≈ ₹2,040.
  3. DMod = 6: yield +1% ⇒ price ≈ −6%; yield −1% ⇒ price ≈ +6% (duration alone predicts symmetric moves).
  4. Accuracy test (5-year 8% bond at 10%, DMod = 3.8922, P = ₹924.18): yield +1% ⇒ duration estimate −3.89% (₹888.21); actual price at 11% = ₹889.12 (−3.79%). Yield −1% ⇒ estimate +3.89% (₹960.16); actual at 9% = ₹961.10 (+3.99%). Duration overstates the loss and understates the gain – the gap is convexity.

Properties (rules) of duration

  1. The duration of a zero-coupon bond equals its maturity.
  2. The duration of a coupon bond is always less than its maturity (cash comes back before maturity).
  3. Holding maturity and yield constant, the lower the coupon, the higher the duration (Malkiel 3).
  4. Holding coupon and yield constant, duration generally increases with maturity (Malkiel 2); for par and premium bonds always, while very deep-discount long bonds can show a slight decline at extreme maturities.
  5. Holding coupon and maturity constant, the lower the yield, the higher the duration (Malkiel 4).
  6. The duration of a perpetuity is (1 + y)/y.
  7. Duration falls as time passes (but not one-for-one for coupon bonds) and jumps up slightly just after each coupon payment.
  8. Portfolio duration is the market-value-weighted average of individual durations.
  9. Higher coupon frequency (semi-annual vs annual) slightly reduces duration.

(Handout misconception 1) "If yield rises 1%, price falls 1%" is wrong: a bond with DMod = 7 falls about 7%. (Misconception 3) Macaulay duration is in years; modified duration is a percentage sensitivity – don't mix them in ΔP/P ≈ −DModΔy.

1. (Handout Problem 3) DMod = 5, yield +0.80%. % price change?

−5 × 0.008 = −4%.

2. (Handout Problem 4) MV ₹5,00,000, DMod = 7, rates +0.50%. Change in value?

−7 × 0.005 = −3.5% ⇒ −₹17,500; new value ≈ ₹4,82,500.

3. Portfolio worth ₹50 crore with DMod 5.2: DV01?

50,00,00,000 × 5.2 × 0.0001 = ₹2,60,000 per basis point.

2.9 Convexity

Convexity measures the curvature of the price-yield relationship – the rate at which duration (the slope) itself changes as yield changes. It is the second-order term that corrects the duration estimate for large yield changes.

C = (1/P) × d2P/dy2 = [1 / (P(1+y)2)] × Σ t(t+1) CFt/(1+y)t    (annual payments)
ΔP/P ≈ −DModΔy + ½ C (Δy)2
Effective convexity = (P− + P+ − 2P0) / (P0 (Δy)2)      Zero: C = n(n+1)/(1+y)2

The convexity term ½C(Δy)2 is always positive (squared Δy), whichever way yields move: it adds to gains and reduces losses.

P = ₹1,000, DMod = 7, C = 80, yield +1%.

  1. Duration effect: −7 × 0.01 = −7%.
  2. Convexity effect: ½ × 80 × 0.012 = +0.4%.
  3. Total: −7% + 0.4% = −6.6% ⇒ P1 = 1,000 × (1 − 0.066) = ₹934 (duration alone would give ₹930).

Same 5-year 8% bond at 10% (table in 2.7): Σ t(t+1)CF/(1.1)t+2 = 18,573.62; C = 18,573.62/924.18 = 20.10. DMod = 3.8922.

ΔyActual % changeDuration onlyDuration + convexity
+1%−3.79%−3.89%−3.79%
−1%+3.99%+3.89%+3.99%
+2%−7.40%−7.78%−7.38%
−2%+8.20%+7.78%+8.19%

Example row +2%: −3.8922 × 0.02 + ½ × 20.10 × 0.022 = −0.07784 + 0.00402 = −7.38% (actual −7.40%). Effective convexity with ±50 bp: (942.4044 + 906.4285 − 2 × 924.1843)/(924.1843 × 0.0052) = 0.4643/0.023105 = 20.10 (prices must be carried to 4 decimals here, because the numerator is a small difference).

P = ₹1,000, DMod = 6, C = 75, yield +2%. Duration: −6 × 0.02 = −12%. Convexity: ½ × 75 × 0.0004 = +1.5%. Total −10.5% ⇒ price ≈ ₹895.

Properties of convexity

  • Option-free bonds always have positive convexity (handout: "positive convexity benefits the bondholder in both directions").
  • Convexity increases with maturity, decreases with coupon and decreases as yield rises – the same directions as duration.
  • For the same duration, a barbell portfolio has more convexity than a bullet (Unit 4).
  • Callable bonds and mortgage-backed securities can show negative convexity at low yields: as yields fall, the call caps the price near the call price, so gains are smaller than losses.
  • Higher convexity is valuable, so, other things equal, investors accept a slightly lower yield for it.
DurationConvexity
MeasuresSlope of price-yield curveCurvature of price-yield curve
Question answeredHow much will price change for a small yield change?How does that sensitivity change as yield changes?
OrderFirst-order (linear)Second-order (curvature correction)
UnitYears (Macaulay) / % per 100 bp (modified)Years squared

Your handout uses ΔP/P ≈ −DModΔy + ½C(Δy)2. Some textbooks and Bloomberg quote a "convexity" that already includes the ½ or is divided by 100 (e.g. Bloomberg may show 0.20 where the handout's formula gives 20.10). Always check which scaling a question uses; in your exam apply the handout form.

1. (Handout) Bond A: DMod 4, C 35; Bond B: DMod 9, C 100; yields rise 1%. Which is more sensitive?

Bond B: −9% vs −4% by duration. With convexity: A −4% + 0.175% = −3.83%; B −9% + 0.5% = −8.5%. B is more sensitive but its higher convexity cushions it more.

2. 10-year zero at 7%: modified duration and convexity?

DMod = 10/1.07 = 9.35; C = 10 × 11/1.072 = 96.08.

2.10 Risk metrics: interest-rate, reinvestment and credit risk

(a) Interest-rate risk

Interest-rate risk is the risk that a change in market interest rates will change the value of a bond (price risk) and the income from reinvesting its cash flows (reinvestment risk). It is measured by duration, DV01 and convexity.

It has two opposite components:

If rates riseIf rates fall
Price risk: bond price falls (loss if sold before maturity)Bond price rises (gain)
Reinvestment: coupons earn more (gain)Coupons earn less (loss)

Because the two offset, there is a holding period – equal to the Macaulay duration – at which they approximately cancel. This is the basis of immunisation (Unit 4).

(b) Reinvestment risk

Reinvestment risk is the risk that coupons and principal received before the end of the investor's horizon will have to be reinvested at a rate lower than the bond's YTM, so that the realised return falls short of the YTM.

It is greater for high-coupon bonds, long horizons, callable/amortising bonds, and in falling-rate environments; it is zero for a zero-coupon bond held to maturity. Illustration: the 3-year 8% bond realised 7.86% when coupons were reinvested at 6% (section 2.4).

(c) Credit (default) risk

Credit risk is the risk that the issuer fails to pay coupons or principal in full and on time (default risk), together with the risk that its credit quality deteriorates, leading to a rating downgrade (downgrade risk) or a widening of its yield spread over government bonds (spread risk), reducing the bond's price.

Credit spread = Yield on corporate bond − Yield on G-Sec of the same maturity
Corporate bond yield ≈ Government yield + Credit spread   (handout: 7% + 2% = 9%)
Expected loss = PD × LGD × EAD

PD = probability of default; LGD = loss given default (1 − recovery rate); EAD = exposure at default. Price effect of a spread change: ΔP/P ≈ −DMod × Δspread.

  1. A ₹50 crore exposure to a BBB issuer with a 2% annual PD and 60% LGD: EL = 0.02 × 0.60 × 50 crore = ₹60 lakh a year.
  2. A corporate bond with DMod = 4.2 whose spread widens by 75 bp: ΔP/P ≈ −4.2 × 0.0075 = −3.15% even though G-Sec yields did not move.

Credit rating scales (SEBI-standardised symbols used by CRISIL, ICRA, CARE, India Ratings)

Long-term symbolMeaningShort-term symbolMeaning
AAAHighest degree of safetyA1Very strong degree of safety
AAHigh degree of safetyA2Strong degree of safety
AAdequate degree of safetyA3Moderate degree of safety
BBBModerate degree of safety (lowest investment grade)A4Minimal degree of safety
BBModerate risk of default (speculative)DDefault
BHigh risk of default
CVery high risk of default
DIn default or expected to default

Modifiers "+" and "−" show relative standing within AA to C. Agencies prefix their name: CRISIL AAA, [ICRA]AAA, CARE AAA, IND AAA. Ratings from AAA to BBB− are investment grade; below that is speculative/high-yield. Outlooks (positive, stable, negative) and "rating watch" signal likely direction.

(d) Other risks in bonds

RiskMeaningExample
Liquidity riskCannot sell quickly without a large price concessionFranklin Templeton India debt schemes, 2020
Inflation (purchasing-power) riskFixed coupons lose real valueMitigated by inflation-indexed bonds
Call / prepayment riskIssuer redeems early when rates fallCallable NCDs, AT1 bonds
Currency riskExchange-rate moves change home-currency valueFPIs in Indian G-Secs; masala bond investors
Event / regulatory riskSpecial events hurt bondholdersYes Bank AT1 bonds written down in 2020 under its reconstruction scheme
Sovereign / political riskGovernment policy affects repayment or taxationCapital controls, tax changes

The IL&FS default (2018) and DHFL default (2019) showed that even highly rated issuers can be downgraded to default quickly; rating agencies were criticised for late downgrades, and SEBI tightened disclosure norms for rating agencies afterwards.

1. Which risk is zero for a zero-coupon bond held to maturity?

Reinvestment risk (and price risk, if held to maturity).

2. What is the lowest investment-grade rating?

BBB−.

3. A 10-year G-Sec yields 7%; a AA corporate bond of the same maturity yields 8.35%. Credit spread?

1.35% = 135 basis points.

Unit 2 exam kit

Introduction. Malkiel (1962) described how bond prices react to yield changes. With coupons fixed, only the discount rate changes.

  1. Inverse: y↑ ⇒ P↓. 3-year 8% bond: ₹1,000 at 8%, ₹950.26 at 10%.
  2. Maturity: longer maturity ⇒ greater % change (3-year −2.53% vs 15-year −8.06% for +1%), at a decreasing rate.
  3. Coupon: lower coupon ⇒ greater % change (4% coupon −7.17% vs 10% coupon −6.17%).
  4. Yield level: lower initial yield ⇒ greater % change (−6.86% from 5% vs −6.13% from 10%).
  5. Convexity: gain from a fall > loss from an equal rise (+10.59% vs −9.13% for a 20-year bond).

Conclusion. Memory aid I-M-C-Y-C; duration quantifies theorems 2–4 and convexity quantifies theorem 5.

Coupon rate = coupon/face value, fixed at issue. Current yield = coupon/market price – income only. YTM = IRR of all cash flows to maturity, including capital gain/loss and time value, assuming reinvestment at YTM. Example: 8-year 8% bond at ₹950: coupon rate 8%, current yield 8.42%, YTM 8.90%. For a discount bond coupon < CY < YTM; for a premium bond the order reverses; for a par bond all three are equal. YTM is the preferred basis for comparing bonds; CY suits income-focused investors.

Introduction. Investors need a number for interest-rate sensitivity; Malkiel's theorems are only qualitative.

Macaulay duration: PV-weighted average time to receive cash flows, DM = Σt·PV(CFt)/P. Modified duration: DM/(1+y), the % price change per 1-point change in yield; duration theorem ΔP/P ≈ −DModΔy. Convexity: curvature, C = (1/P)d2P/dy2; improved estimate ΔP/P ≈ −DModΔy + ½C(Δy)2.

Illustration. 5-year 8% bond at 10%: P = ₹924.18; DM = 3,956.81/924.18 = 4.28 years; DMod = 3.89; C = 20.10. If yields rise 2%: duration predicts −7.78%, duration + convexity −7.38%, actual −7.40%.
