Properties. Zero-coupon duration = maturity; coupon bond duration < maturity; duration rises with maturity, falls with coupon and yield; perpetuity (1+y)/y; portfolio duration is a weighted average; positive convexity benefits investors.

Uses. Measuring and hedging risk (DV01), immunisation, SEBI duration-based debt fund categories, stress tests.

Limitations. Duration assumes a small parallel shift and a straight-line relation; convexity is needed for large moves; effective measures are needed for bonds with options.

Conclusion. Duration gives the first-order sensitivity; convexity refines it.

Introduction. Bonds are "fixed income", not "fixed value".

1. Interest-rate (price) risk – prices fall when yields rise; measured by duration/DV01 (e.g. DMod 7 ⇒ −7% for +1%). 2. Reinvestment risk – coupons reinvested at lower rates reduce realised yield (8% YTM bond realised 7.86% at 6% reinvestment). 3. Credit risk – default, downgrade and spread risk; ratings AAA–D; EL = PD × LGD × EAD; IL&FS 2018. 4. Liquidity risk – Franklin Templeton 2020. 5. Inflation risk. 6. Call risk – YTC < YTM for premium callable bonds. 7. Currency risk. 8. Event risk – Yes Bank AT1 write-down 2020.

Managing the risks. Duration matching/immunisation, diversification across issuers and ratings, holding liquid benchmarks, inflation-indexed bonds, stress tests.

Conclusion. Price and reinvestment risk offset each other at the duration horizon, which is the basis of immunisation.

Question pattern: "A 5-year 8% bond (face ₹1,000) trades at a 10% yield. (a) Find its price. (b) Find Macaulay and modified duration. (c) Estimate the price if yields rise by 2% using duration and duration + convexity (convexity 20.10) and compare with the actual price. (d) Advise an investor who expects rates to fall."

(a) P = ₹924.18 (table in 2.7). (b) DM = 4.28 years; DMod = 3.89. (c) Duration: 924.18 × (1 − 0.0778) = ₹852.24; with convexity: 924.18 × (1 − 0.0738) = ₹855.96; actual at 12% = ₹855.81. (d) If rates are expected to fall, lengthen duration: prefer longer-maturity, lower-coupon bonds (Malkiel 2, 3), since price gains will be larger and convexity adds further gain. Conclude with the risk that the forecast may be wrong, and suggest a duration target consistent with risk appetite.

Unit 3 — Riding the Yield Curve: Analytics and Applications

Syllabus: types of yield curves (normal, inverted, flat); constructing yield curves (bootstrapping for zero and spot rates); Excel for yield-curve analysis; introduction to Bloomberg analytics. (10 teaching hours, CO3). Follows your Yield Curve Student Handout, sections 1–23.

3.1 What is a yield curve?

Should a bank charge the same rate for a 1-year loan and a 10-year loan? Usually not: lending for longer means more uncertainty, more inflation risk, more interest-rate risk, more credit risk and a longer opportunity cost. The yield curve simply answers: "What yield is available in the market right now, for each different length of time?"

A yield curve is a graphical representation of the relationship between the yield (interest rate) on bonds and their remaining time to maturity, generally for bonds of similar credit quality. The term structure of interest rates is that relationship itself; the yield curve is its graph.

Reading the graph: X-axis = maturity (3 months, 6 months, 1, 2, 5, 10, 20, 30 years); Y-axis = yield (%). The government curve is the standard reference because government securities are free of credit risk in their own currency, so maturity is the main thing that drives differences in yield.

Maturity (handout Figure 1)3M6M1Y2Y5Y10Y
Yield6.0%6.2%6.4%6.7%7.0%7.3%

What the curve tells us: current rates at every maturity; the market's expectations of future interest rates; inflation expectations; signals about economic conditions; and the relative attractiveness of maturities. Who uses it: investors, banks, corporates, central banks, governments and portfolio managers.

Yield is not coupon. The coupon rate is fixed in the contract (8% on ₹1,000 = ₹80 forever). The yield depends on the market price: at ₹950 the current yield is 80/950 = 8.42%. Yield curves plot yields, never coupons.

Every bond, loan and deposit in India is priced off the G-Sec curve plus a spread; its shape shows what the market expects RBI and inflation to do, and it tells an investor what extra yield he gets for extending maturity.

FBIL and CCIL publish daily Indian government yield curves (par and zero-coupon) that banks, mutual funds and insurers use to value their G-Sec and SDL holdings. The 10-year benchmark G-Sec yield is the headline point on the curve.

3.2 Types (shapes) of yield curves

ShapeHandout data (1Y, 2Y, 5Y, 10Y, 20Y)PatternPossible interpretation
Normal / upward-sloping5.5, 5.8, 6.3, 6.8, 7.2Short < LongMost common; market expects growth, higher future rates or inflation, or demands a term premium
Flat6.0, 6.1, 6.1, 6.2, 6.2Short ≈ LongUncertainty; transition between economic phases (e.g. late in a rate-hike cycle)
Inverted / downward-sloping7.5, 7.2, 6.7, 6.2, 6.0Short > LongRates expected to fall; slowdown or lower inflation expected. Investors lock in long yields, pushing long prices up and long yields down
Humped5.5, 6.2, 6.8, 6.3, 6.0Medium highestMixed signals; uncertainty about the rate path; supply-demand imbalances at particular maturities

Movements of the curve (useful vocabulary)

  • Parallel shift: all yields move by the same amount.
  • Steepening: long-short spread (e.g. 10Y − 2Y) widens; flattening: spread narrows.
  • Bull steepener: short yields fall more than long (typical when a central bank starts cutting). Bear flattener: short yields rise more than long (typical in a hiking cycle). Bear steepener: long yields rise more (inflation or fiscal worries). Bull flattener: long yields fall more (flight to safety, falling inflation expectations).
  • Twist / butterfly: short and long ends move differently from the middle.

Between May 2022 and February 2023 RBI raised the policy repo rate from 4.00% to 6.50%. Short-term yields rose sharply while the 10-year yield rose much less, so the Indian G-Sec curve flattened considerably – a classic bear flattener. In the US, the 2-year Treasury yield stayed above the 10-year yield for a long stretch from 2022, one of the longest inversions on record.

(Handout Q7) An inverted curve does not guarantee a recession. It is a market signal to be read with GDP growth, inflation, unemployment, central-bank policy, credit spreads, money supply and commodity prices.

1. Curve: 1Y 7%, 5Y 6%, 10Y 5%. Shape?

Inverted.

2. What is a bear flattener?

Yields rise, short-term more than long-term, so the curve flattens; typical of a central-bank hiking cycle.

3. Which shape is most common historically?

Normal (upward-sloping).

3.3 Theories of the term structure

Four classic theories explain why the curve has the shape it has.

(1) Pure (unbiased) expectations theory

The expectations theory holds that long-term yields are determined solely by the market's expectations of future short-term rates; the long rate is the geometric average of current and expected future short rates, so forward rates are unbiased forecasts of future spot rates, and investors are indifferent between a long bond and rolling over short bonds.

(1 + Rn)n = (1 + r1)(1 + E[r2])...(1 + E[rn])

Implications: upward curve ⇒ rates expected to rise; inverted ⇒ expected to fall; flat ⇒ no change expected. Limitation: ignores risk; cannot explain why the curve is upward-sloping most of the time.

(Handout) 1-year yield 6%, 2-year yield 7%. Under pure expectations the expected 1-year rate one year from now = 1.072/1.06 − 1 = 1.1449/1.06 − 1 = 8.01%.

(2) Liquidity preference (liquidity premium) theory

The liquidity preference theory (Hicks) holds that investors prefer short-term, more liquid, less price-volatile securities and demand a liquidity (term) premium that increases with maturity; long-term yields therefore equal the average of expected short rates plus a premium.

Rn ≈ average of expected short rates + Ln   (Ln rises with n)

Explains why the curve is usually upward-sloping even when rates are expected to stay flat. Forward rates overstate expected future rates by the premium.

Current and expected 1-year rates are both 6%; the 2-year liquidity premium is 0.5%. 2-year yield ≈ (6% + 6%)/2 + 0.5% = 6.5% – an upward curve with no expected change in rates.

(3) Market segmentation theory

The market segmentation theory holds that the bond market is divided into separate maturity segments, each served by investors and borrowers who stay in their own segment (because of regulation, liabilities or habit); the yield in each segment is set by supply and demand in that segment alone, independent of others.

Example: banks concentrate in short and medium maturities (SLR, asset-liability management); insurers and pension funds in very long maturities. A surge in long-dated insurance demand can push down 30-year yields regardless of short rates. Limitation: ignores arbitrage between segments.

(4) Preferred habitat theory

The preferred habitat theory (Modigliani and Sutch) holds that investors have preferred maturity ranges but will leave them if offered a sufficient yield premium; the yield curve therefore reflects both expected future rates and maturity-specific premiums that can be positive or negative.

It combines expectations with segmentation, and can explain every shape including humps.

TheoryKey assumptionUpward curve meansCan it explain a usually upward curve?Can it explain humps/inversions?
Pure expectationsMaturities are perfect substitutes; risk-neutral investorsRates expected to riseNoYes (via expected rate path)
Liquidity preferenceInvestors prefer short maturities; premium rises with maturityExpected rise and/or premiumYesInversion only if rates expected to fall a lot
Market segmentationMaturities are not substitutes at allRelative supply/demand across segmentsYes, if supply pattern fitsYes
Preferred habitatImperfect substitutes; investors move for a premiumExpectations + habitat premiumsYesYes

India shows preferred-habitat effects clearly: life insurers and pension funds (LIC, EPFO, NPS funds) have strong demand for 30–40-year G-Secs and SDLs to match long liabilities, which is why the government can issue very long bonds; banks dominate the 5–10-year segment.

1. Which theory says forward rates are unbiased predictors of future spot rates?

Pure expectations theory.

2. Which theory best explains why the curve is usually upward-sloping?

Liquidity preference (term premium).

3. Difference between segmentation and preferred habitat?

Segmentation: investors never leave their segment. Preferred habitat: they prefer a segment but will move if paid enough premium.

3.4 Spot rates, forward rates and par yields

Spot rate (zero rate) sn: the yield on a zero-coupon bond maturing at n; the rate for discounting a single cash flow at time n.
Forward rate f: the interest rate, agreed or implied today, for a period that starts in the future (e.g. the 1-year rate one year from now, f1,1).
Par yield: the coupon rate at which a bond of a given maturity would price exactly at par.
Discount factor dn = 1/(1+sn)n: the present value of ₹1 receivable at n.

(1 + sn)n = (1 + sm)m × (1 + fm,n−m)n−m   ⇒   fm,n−m = [(1+sn)n/(1+sm)m]1/(n−m) − 1
Bond price with spot rates: P = Σ CFt/(1+st)t = Σ CFt × dt

fm,k = the k-year rate starting at year m. No-arbitrage: investing for n years directly must equal investing for m years and rolling at the forward rate.

  1. 1-year yield 6%, 2-year yield 7% (annual compounding).
  2. (1.07)2 = (1.06)(1 + f) ⇒ 1 + f = 1.1449/1.06 = 1.080094.
  3. f = 8.01%: the market-implied 1-year rate one year from now. It is not a promise; the actual rate may differ.

2-year spot 7.0%, 3-year spot 7.4%: f2,1 = 1.0743/1.072 − 1 = 1.238833/1.1449 − 1 = 8.20%. When the spot curve rises, forwards lie above spots.

CurveBuilt fromUsed for
Yield (YTM) curveYTMs of coupon bondsQuick market view; quoting
Par curveCoupons that price bonds at parSetting coupons of new issues
Spot (zero) curveZero-coupon yields (bootstrapped or STRIPS)Correct discounting of each cash flow; arbitrage-free pricing
Forward curveDerived from spot curveMarket expectations; pricing FRAs, swaps, FRBs; riding strategies

Ordering rule for an upward-sloping curve: forward > spot > par/YTM. For a downward-sloping curve the order reverses. A coupon bond's YTM is a weighted blend of spot rates, closer to the spot rate of its final (largest) cash flow.

3.5 Constructing the yield curve: bootstrapping zero and spot rates

We rarely see zero-coupon bonds at every maturity, but we see coupon bonds. Bootstrapping peels off the spot rates one maturity at a time: the 1-year rate prices the 1-year bond; knowing it, the 2-year bond has only one unknown (the 2-year spot rate), and so on – each step "pulls itself up by its bootstraps" using the previous step.

Bootstrapping is a step-by-step method of deriving the zero-coupon (spot) yield curve from the prices or par yields of coupon-bearing bonds, by discounting the known earlier cash flows at previously derived spot rates and solving for the one unknown spot rate at each successive maturity.

sn = [ (Cn + F) / (Pn − Σt=1..n−1 Cn/(1+st)t) ]1/n − 1

For par bonds (P = F = 1, coupon = par yield cn): sn = [(1 + cn) / (1 − cn Σ dt)]1/n − 1.

Steps: (1) collect bonds of increasing maturities (same issuer/credit quality, annual coupons here); (2) the shortest is a zero (or one cash flow) ⇒ its yield is s1; (3) for the next bond, discount its earlier coupons at known spot rates, subtract from price, and solve for the last spot rate; (4) repeat; (5) compute discount factors and forward rates; (6) plot the zero curve.

Par yields: 1-year 6.0%, 2-year 6.5%, 3-year 7.0% (annual coupons, face 1).

  1. s1 = 6.00% (a 1-year par bond pays one cash flow 1.06 for price 1). d1 = 1/1.06 = 0.943396.
  2. s2: 1 = 0.065 × 0.943396 + 1.065/(1+s2)2. PV of first coupon = 0.061321; 1 − 0.061321 = 0.938679; (1+s2)2 = 1.065/0.938679 = 1.134573 ⇒ s2 = 6.516%. d2 = 0.881389.
  3. s3: PV of first two coupons = 0.07 × (0.943396 + 0.881389) = 0.127735; 1 − 0.127735 = 0.872265; (1+s3)3 = 1.07/0.872265 = 1.226691 ⇒ s3 = 7.048%. d3 = 0.815201.
  4. Forwards: f1,1 = 1.134573/1.06 − 1 = 7.035%; f2,1 = 1.226691/1.134573 − 1 = 8.119%.
  5. Use: price a 3-year 8% bond (face ₹1,000) with the spot rates: 80 × 0.943396 + 80 × 0.881389 + 1,080 × 0.815201 = 75.47 + 70.51 + 880.42 = ₹1,026.40; its YTM is 6.99% – slightly below the 7.0% par yield because a higher coupon puts more weight on the earlier, lower spot rates (the "coupon effect").

Bonds (face ₹100, annual coupons): 1-year zero at ₹94.34; 2-year 7% coupon at ₹101.50; 3-year 8% coupon at ₹102.00.

  1. d1 = 94.34/100 = 0.9434 ⇒ s1 = 100/94.34 − 1 = 6.00%.
  2. 2-year: 101.50 = 7 × 0.9434 + 107 × d2 ⇒ d2 = (101.50 − 6.6038)/107 = 0.886880 ⇒ s2 = 0.886880−1/2 − 1 = 6.19%.
  3. 3-year: 102.00 = 8 × 0.9434 + 8 × 0.886880 + 108 × d3 ⇒ d3 = (102.00 − 7.5472 − 7.0950)/108 = 0.808868 ⇒ s3 = 0.808868−1/3 − 1 = 7.33%.
  4. Forwards from discount factors (f = dearlier/dlater − 1): f1,1 = 0.9434/0.886880 − 1 = 6.37%; f2,1 = 0.886880/0.808868 − 1 = 9.64%. (Using discount factors avoids rounding errors that creep in when rounded spot rates are raised to powers.)

Indian STRIPS give directly observable zero-coupon prices for G-Sec cash-flow dates, and CCIL's published zero-coupon yield curve is estimated from traded G-Sec prices with a smooth parametric model, which is a more sophisticated version of the same bootstrapping idea.

1. Why can't we just use YTMs to discount every cash flow?

A YTM is an average rate for one bond; cash flows at different dates should be discounted at the spot rate for their own date. Using spot rates prevents arbitrage between bonds.

2. 1-year spot 5%, 2-year spot 6%: find f1,1.

1.062/1.05 − 1 = 1.1236/1.05 − 1 = 7.01%.

3.6 Using the yield curve: investment decisions and riding the curve

Decisions from the curve (handout sections 8–17)

  • Expecting rates to rise (handout Example 1: 1Y 6%, 10Y 7%): prefer short maturities, T-Bills, short-duration funds; reinvest later at higher rates. A long bond may suffer a large price fall.
  • Expecting rates to fall (Example 2: 10Y 7%, expected to fall to 5.5%): buy the 10-year now; gain twice – higher coupon than new issues and capital appreciation (yield ↓ ⇒ price ↑).
  • Highest yield ≠ best investment (section 12: 2Y 6.0%, 5Y 6.5%, 10Y 7.0%): weigh horizon, rate expectations, inflation, liquidity, duration and risk tolerance. Ask "is an extra 0.5% worth 5 more years of maturity risk?" – the 10-year par bond at 7% has DMod ≈ 7.02 and would lose about 6.7% if its yield rose 1%, versus about 4.0% for the 5-year (DMod ≈ 4.16).
  • Portfolio construction (section 16.3): short-duration, long-duration, barbell or ladder portfolios (Unit 4).
  • Banks (17.1): borrow short at 5%, lend long at 8% ⇒ spread about 3%; a flattening curve squeezes margins.
  • Corporate borrowing (17.2): corporate yield ≈ G-Sec yield + credit spread = 7% + 2% = 9%.
  • Government debt management (17.3): choose maturity mix, refinancing and timing of issues to minimise cost and rollover risk.

Riding the yield curve

Riding the yield curve is an active strategy in which an investor buys a bond with a maturity longer than his investment horizon, on an upward-sloping curve, and sells it at the end of the horizon; if the curve does not shift, the bond "rolls down" to a lower yield as its maturity shortens, producing a capital gain in addition to its yield (roll-down return).

Horizon 1 year. Zero-coupon yields: 1-year 6.0%, 2-year 6.5% (face ₹100).

  1. Buy-and-hold alternative: buy a 1-year zero, return = 6.00%.
  2. Ride: buy the 2-year zero at 100/1.0652 = ₹88.1659.
  3. After one year it is a 1-year bond. If the curve is unchanged, its yield is 6.0% and it sells for 100/1.06 = ₹94.3396.
  4. Return = 94.3396/88.1659 − 1 = 7.00% – one full percentage point more than the 1-year bond.
  5. Risk: if the 1-year yield has risen to 7.0% by then, the sale price is 100/1.07 = ₹93.4579 and the return is 6.00% – no better than the safe choice. The break-even future 1-year rate is 1.0652/1.06 − 1 = 7.00%, i.e. exactly the forward rate: riding beats buy-and-hold only if the future rate turns out below the forward rate.

Riding works when the curve is steep and stable (and it is popular with money-market and short-term debt funds in India); it fails when rates rise faster than the forward curve implied. Under pure expectations theory it earns nothing extra on average; under liquidity preference it earns the term premium.

3.7 Using Excel for yield-curve analysis

A step-by-step Excel workflow (details and more functions in the Analytics angle section):

  1. Enter data: column A maturities in years (0.25, 0.5, 1, 2, 5, 10, 20, 30); column B yields (%) from FBIL/CCIL or the handout.
  2. Plot: select A:B → Insert → Scatter with Smooth Lines and Markers. Title "G-Sec yield curve"; X-axis "Maturity (years)", Y-axis "Yield (%)". Add several dates as extra series to compare shapes.
  3. Slope and spreads: =B7-B4 (10Y − 2Y) for steepness; =2*B6-B4-B7 (butterfly/curvature: 2 × 5Y − 2Y − 10Y).
  4. Interpolate a missing maturity (e.g. 7 years between 5 and 10): =FORECAST.LINEAR(7, B6:B7, A6:A7).
  5. Forward rates from spot rates in C: =((1+C5)^A5/(1+C4)^A4)^(1/(A5-A4))-1.
  6. Bootstrapping (annual par yields in B, discount factors in D): D2 =1/(1+B2); for row n: =(1-B3*SUM($D$2:D2))/(1+B3) copied down; spot =D3^(-1/A3)-1.
  7. Fit a smooth curve: add a polynomial trendline (order 2–3) with equation displayed, or fit a Nelson-Siegel curve y(t) = β0 + β1[(1−e−t/λ)/(t/λ)] + β2[(1−e−t/λ)/(t/λ) − e−t/λ] by minimising squared errors with Solver (β0 = long-run level, β1 = slope, β2 = curvature).
  8. Scenario analysis: add a shift cell (e.g. +0.50%) and a "shocked curve" column =B2+$F$1; revalue bonds with PRICE to see the impact; use Data → What-If Analysis → Data Table for a grid of shifts.
  9. Classify the shape automatically: =IF(B7-B2>0.25%,"Normal",IF(B2-B7>0.25%,"Inverted","Flat")).

Excel sheets for the handout's Try-It-Yourself curves (A, B, C) take two minutes to build and make excellent CIA assignment content: plot all three on one chart and label the shapes.

3.8 Introduction to Bloomberg analytics

The Bloomberg Terminal is a professional platform that provides real-time and historical market data, news, analytics and trading tools. For bonds, it offers security descriptions, pricing and yield calculators, curve construction, relative-value and portfolio-risk analysis.

Navigating

  • Load a security by typing its ticker/identifier then pressing a yellow market-sector key: Govt (government bonds), Corp (corporate bonds), Mtge, M-Mkt, Index, Curncy, Equity, Comdty; then type a function mnemonic and press <GO>.
  • HELP (pressed twice) connects to a Bloomberg help-desk representative; typing a keyword in the command line searches functions.

Key bond functions

FunctionWhat it showsWhat to interpret
DESSecurity description: issuer, coupon, maturity, day count, coupon frequency, ratings, call schedule, amount outstandingConfirm cash-flow terms before valuing
YASYield and spread analysis: price ↔ yield calculator, spreads to benchmark, risk measuresYTM/YTW, G-spread, I-spread, Z-spread, OAS, modified duration, convexity, DV01 ("risk")
ALLQAll dealer quotes for the bondBid-ask spread, depth – liquidity
GP / HPPrice or yield history graph / tableTrends, volatility
GCGraph curves: sovereign yield curves, compare dates, spreads between curvesShape, steepening/flattening, forward curves
WBWorld bond markets monitorCompare 2Y/10Y yields across countries
SRCHBond search/screening by issuer, rating, maturity, currency, couponBuild a universe of candidate bonds
CRPRCredit ratings history from agenciesRating changes and outlooks
BTMMTreasury and money-market monitorPolicy rates, money-market and short yields
ECOEconomic calendar and releasesInflation, GDP, policy dates that move yields
PORTPortfolio and risk analyticsPortfolio duration, key-rate exposures, scenario tests, attribution

Interpreting YAS spreads

  • G-spread: bond yield minus the interpolated government yield of the same maturity.
  • I-spread: bond yield minus the interpolated swap rate.
  • Z-spread: constant spread added to every point of the government spot curve that makes discounted cash flows equal the price.
  • OAS (option-adjusted spread): Z-spread after removing the value of embedded options; used for callable/puttable bonds.

Bloomberg's Excel add-in lets you pull data into spreadsheets with =BDP(security, field) (current value), =BDH(security, field, start, end) (history) and =BDS(security, field) (bulk data such as cash-flow schedules).

In a written exam you cannot use a terminal; questions test whether you know which function answers which question and how to interpret its output (e.g. "a bond's G-spread widened from 90 to 140 bp; what does it mean?" – the market sees higher credit risk or lower liquidity, and the bond's price has fallen relative to G-Secs).

1. Which Bloomberg function would you use to find a bond's modified duration and Z-spread?

YAS.

2. Which function compares yield curves on two dates?

GC.

Unit 3 exam kit

Introduction. A yield curve plots yields of bonds of similar credit quality against maturity.

Normal – short < long (5.5% → 7.2%): growth, rising rates or term premium. Flat – short ≈ long (6.0% → 6.2%): uncertainty or transition. Inverted – short > long (7.5% → 6.0%): rate cuts/slowdown expected. Humped – medium highest (peak 6.8% at 5 years): mixed signals. Draw the four curves.

Indian example. Flattening of the G-Sec curve during RBI's 2022–23 hiking cycle.

Conclusion. The shape is a signal, not a guaranteed forecast; read it with other indicators.

Introduction. The term structure is the relationship between yield and maturity; theories explain its shape.

1. Pure expectations – long rate = geometric average of expected short rates; (1.07)2 = 1.06(1+f) ⇒ f = 8.01%; upward curve means rising expected rates; limitation: ignores risk. 2. Liquidity preference – investors demand a premium for longer maturities; explains the usual upward slope; forward = expected rate + premium. 3. Market segmentation – separate maturity markets, yields by segment supply-demand; banks short, insurers long; limitation: ignores arbitrage. 4. Preferred habitat – investors prefer segments but move for a premium; explains any shape.

Comparison table (assumption, meaning of upward slope, explains humps?).

Indian application. Strong long-end demand from insurers and pension funds (habitat) and RBI policy expectations (expectations) both shape the G-Sec curve.

Conclusion. No single theory suffices; the curve reflects expectations plus maturity-specific premiums.

Define spot rate and bootstrapping; list the steps; solve the par-yield example (6.0%, 6.5%, 7.0% ⇒ spots 6.00%, 6.516%, 7.048%; forwards 7.035%, 8.119%); show pricing of a 3-year 8% bond at ₹1,026.40 using spot rates; explain why spot discounting is arbitrage-free; mention Indian STRIPS and CCIL's zero curve; conclude with uses (valuation, forwards, immunisation).

Curve: 3M 6.5%, 1Y 6.7%, 2Y 6.9%, 5Y 7.2%, 10Y 7.6%. (a) Shape: normal/upward. (b) Highest yield: 10Y 7.6%. (c) Signal: term premium and/or expected higher rates or inflation. (d) Forward 1-year rate one year ahead from the 1Y and 2Y points: 1.0692/1.067 − 1 = 7.10%. (e) Advice: an investor expecting a sharp rise in rates should not buy the 10-year just because it yields most – prefer short maturities or a ladder; an investor expecting falling rates or with long liabilities may buy long bonds; mention riding the curve on the steep 3M–2Y segment and the risk if rates rise above forwards. (f) Conclusion: combine curve, duration and risk appetite.

Unit 4 — Strategic Bond Portfolio Management

Syllabus: active vs passive approaches; laddering, barbell and buy-and-hold strategies; stress testing (scenario analysis); performance metrics – Sharpe ratio, alpha and beta. (8 teaching hours, CO4)

4.1 Active vs passive bond portfolio management

A passive manager is like a driver who sets cruise control on a fixed route: low cost, predictable, no attempt to beat traffic. An active manager keeps changing lanes and routes based on forecasts (of interest rates, spreads, the curve) hoping to arrive sooner – but may also get stuck.

Passive bond management aims to earn the return of the market (or to meet a pre-set liability) with minimal trading, without forecasting interest rates – e.g. buy-and-hold, indexing, target-maturity funds.
Active bond management seeks to outperform a benchmark by using forecasts of interest rates, yield-curve shape, credit spreads or mispricing, and adjusting duration, maturity mix, sector and security holdings accordingly.

BasisPassiveActive
ObjectiveMatch an index or meet known liabilitiesBeat a benchmark (generate alpha)
Belief about marketsMarkets are efficient; forecasting doesn't pay after costsForecasts and mispricing can be exploited
Trading and turnoverLowHigh
CostsLow expense ratio and transaction costsHigher fees and costs
Risk vs benchmarkLow tracking errorHigher tracking error
DurationKept equal to benchmark/liabilityLengthened or shortened on rate views
ExamplesBuy-and-hold, indexing, target-maturity ETFs, immunisation, cash-flow matchingRate anticipation, curve trades, credit rotation, bond swaps, riding the curve

Passive strategies

  • Buy-and-hold: buy bonds whose maturity matches the investment horizon and hold to maturity; returns known in advance (if no default), apart from reinvestment of coupons.
  • Indexing: replicate a bond index (full replication is hard because indices contain thousands of illiquid bonds, so managers use stratified sampling – matching duration, sector, rating and maturity cells). Performance judged by tracking error.
  • Target-maturity funds: hold bonds maturing near a fixed date; risk falls as the fund ages (pull-to-par).

Active strategies

  • Interest-rate anticipation (duration management): lengthen duration before expected rate cuts; shorten before hikes.
  • Yield-curve strategies: bullet, barbell, ladder positioning; steepener/flattener trades; riding the curve (Unit 3).
  • Credit/sector strategies: overweight sectors (PSU, NBFC, corporate) whose spreads are expected to narrow; avoid those likely to widen; credit upgrade plays.
  • Security selection: buy bonds whose intrinsic value exceeds price (Unit 2 valuation).
  • Bond swaps: substitution swap (exchange for an identical bond at a higher yield); inter-market spread swap (move between sectors when the spread is abnormal); rate anticipation swap (change duration); pure yield pick-up swap (move to higher-yield bonds for more income); tax swap (realise losses for tax benefit).

Structured (semi-active) strategies

Immunisation, cash-flow matching (dedication) and contingent immunisation – covered in 4.3. These manage the portfolio against a liability rather than an index.

Bharat Bond ETF (launched in December 2019, managed by Edelweiss AMC) is a passive, target-maturity ETF that tracks indices of AAA-rated bonds of central public-sector enterprises; each series matures on a fixed date. SEBI's mutual fund categories also separate duration-based active funds (e.g. dynamic bond funds that change duration freely) from passive index funds and ETFs.

1. What is tracking error?

The standard deviation of the difference between the portfolio's and the benchmark's returns.

2. A manager expects RBI to cut rates. What should an active manager do with duration?

Lengthen it (buy longer, lower-coupon bonds) to benefit more from the price rise.

3. Name the swap in which a bond is exchanged for an identical one offering a higher yield.

Substitution swap.

4.2 Laddering, barbell, bullet and buy-and-hold

Laddered portfolio: funds are divided roughly equally among bonds maturing at regular intervals; as each bond matures, the proceeds are reinvested at the long end.
Barbell portfolio: funds are concentrated in short-term and long-term bonds, with little or nothing in intermediate maturities.
Bullet portfolio: maturities are concentrated around a single target date/maturity.
Buy-and-hold: bonds are bought and held to maturity with little or no trading, regardless of interim price changes.

FeatureLadderBarbellBulletBuy-and-hold
Management stylePassiveActive (curve/convexity view)Active or liability-drivenPassive
LiquidityRegular maturities provide cashShort leg gives liquidityLow until target dateOnly at maturities
Reinvestment riskSpread over time (averaging)High on short legLow before targetDepends on coupons
Convexity (same duration)MediumHighestLowest–
Performs best whenRates uncertain; steady needsLarge parallel moves, curve flattens/volatility highCurve steepens; known date liabilityInvestor needs certainty of cash flows
WeaknessDoesn't exploit viewsYield often lower than bullet; loses if curve steepensLess convexityIgnores opportunities; reinvestment risk

All yields 7% (flat curve). Bullet: a 5-year zero. Barbell: 1-year and 10-year zeros. Find barbell weights to match duration, then compare convexity and value after ±2% parallel shifts (₹100 invested).

  1. Duration match: w × 1 + (1 − w) × 10 = 5 ⇒ 9w = 5 ⇒ w = 5/9 = 0.5556 in the 1-year, 0.4444 in the 10-year. Both portfolios have Macaulay duration 5 and DMod = 5/1.07 = 4.67.
  2. Convexity of a zero = n(n+1)/(1+y)2. Bullet: 5 × 6/1.1449 = 26.20. Barbell: 0.5556 × (2/1.1449) + 0.4444 × (110/1.1449) = 0.97 + 42.70 = 43.67.
  3. Yields +2%: bullet worth ₹91.16, barbell ₹91.47. Yields −2%: bullet ₹109.89, barbell ₹110.29. The barbell does better in both directions – that is the value of extra convexity.
  4. Catch: in real markets a barbell usually has a lower yield than a bullet of the same duration (investors pay for convexity), and it loses if the curve steepens (long end falls in price) while short rates stay put.

₹10 lakh: ₹2 lakh each in 1-, 3-, 5-, 7- and 10-year bonds. Treating each as a zero at 7%, portfolio Macaulay duration = (1 + 3 + 5 + 7 + 10)/5 = 5.2 years; convexity = average of n(n+1)/1.072 = 36.68. Every year (or two) one rung matures and is reinvested at the prevailing long rate – spreading reinvestment and maturity risk across time.

1. Which structure has the highest convexity for a given duration?

Barbell.

2. A company must repay ₹50 crore in exactly 5 years. Which structure fits?

Bullet (or a 5-year zero/cash-flow matched portfolio) around the 5-year date.

4.3 Immunisation, cash-flow matching and contingent immunisation

If rates rise, your bond price falls but you reinvest coupons at higher rates; if rates fall, the price rises but reinvestment earns less. At one particular horizon – the Macaulay duration – these two effects cancel. Immunisation sets the portfolio's duration equal to the investment horizon (or liability date) so that the target amount is achieved whichever way rates move.

Immunisation is a strategy of structuring a bond portfolio so that its value at a given horizon (or its surplus over a liability) is protected against changes in interest rates, by making the price risk and reinvestment risk offset each other.

Conditions for immunising a single liability (Redington):

  1. PV of assets = PV of liability.
  2. Macaulay duration of assets = duration (horizon) of the liability.
  3. Convexity (dispersion) of assets ≥ convexity of liability.

Assumptions: flat curve, small parallel shifts, no default. Because duration drifts as time passes and yields change, the portfolio must be rebalanced periodically.

5-year 8% bond bought at par (₹1,000), yield 8%. Its Macaulay duration = 4.31 years. Target value at 4.31 years = 1,000 × 1.084.3121 = ₹1,393.57.

  1. Suppose the yield changes immediately to 6% and stays there. Value at the horizon = price at 6% × 1.064.3121 = ₹1,393.96.
  2. If instead it changes to 10%: value = price at 10% × 1.104.3121 = ₹1,393.96.
  3. At 8%: ₹1,393.57. In every case the target is met (slightly exceeded, thanks to convexity) – price and reinvestment effects cancel at the duration.

An insurer owes ₹10,00,000 in 4 years. Yield 8%. Available: 2-year and 6-year zero-coupon bonds.

  1. PV of liability = 10,00,000/1.084 = ₹7,35,029.85.
  2. Duration match: w × 2 + (1 − w) × 6 = 4 ⇒ w = 0.5. Invest ₹3,67,514.93 in each.
  3. Face values: 2-year = 3,67,514.93 × 1.082 = ₹4,28,669.41; 6-year = 3,67,514.93 × 1.086 = ₹5,83,200.00.
  4. Test: if yields move to 6%, PV assets = ₹7,92,647.24 vs PV liability ₹7,92,093.66 ⇒ surplus ₹553.57. At 10%: assets ₹6,83,473.44 vs liability ₹6,83,013.46 ⇒ surplus ₹459.98. Immunised: the surplus stays ≥ 0 because the barbell of zeros has more convexity than the single liability.

Cash-flow matching (dedication)

Cash-flow matching constructs a portfolio whose coupon and principal receipts exactly (or almost exactly) match the amounts and timing of a stream of liabilities, starting from the last liability and working backwards, so that no reinvestment or price risk remains.

More certain than immunisation and needs no rebalancing, but usually costlier (exact matches are hard to find). STRIPS make it easier in India.

Contingent immunisation

Contingent immunisation (Leibowitz and Weinberger) allows active management as long as the portfolio's value stays above the amount needed to immunise a minimum acceptable (floor) return; if the "safety cushion" is exhausted, the manager must switch to full immunisation.

₹100 crore portfolio, 5-year horizon, floor return 6%, current yields 8%.

  1. Required terminal value = 100 × 1.065 = ₹133.82 crore.
  2. Assets needed now to immunise at 8% = 133.82/1.085 = ₹91.08 crore. Safety cushion = 100 − 91.08 = ₹8.92 crore; the manager may manage actively.
  3. Rates jump to 9% and the active portfolio (DMod 7) loses about 7% ⇒ worth ₹93.00 crore. Required assets at 9% = 133.82/1.095 = ₹86.98 crore. Cushion = ₹6.02 crore – still positive, active management continues.
  4. If the portfolio value ever falls to the required amount (cushion = 0), the manager must immunise immediately to lock in 6%.

Indian life insurers and pension funds run asset-liability management (ALM) programmes that match the duration of their G-Sec and SDL holdings to the long duration of policy liabilities; IRDAI's investment rules require a large share of life funds in government securities, which makes long G-Secs natural immunising assets.

Immunisation uses Macaulay duration (a time measure) to match the horizon; price-sensitivity calculations use modified duration. Also: immunisation fails for non-parallel shifts and requires rebalancing.

1. State the three conditions of immunisation.

PV assets = PV liabilities; D(assets) = D(liabilities); convexity of assets ≥ convexity of liabilities.

2. Why must an immunised portfolio be rebalanced?

Duration changes with time and with yield changes (it does not fall one-for-one with time), so the match breaks down.

4.4 Stress testing: scenario analysis for portfolio risk assessment

Stress testing evaluates how a portfolio's value would change under extreme but plausible adverse conditions (large rate moves, curve twists, spread widening, liquidity shocks). Scenario analysis revalues the portfolio under a set of specified, internally consistent scenarios (base, bull, bear) to understand the range of outcomes.

Types

  • Sensitivity analysis – change one factor (e.g. all yields +100 bp).
  • Historical scenario – replay a past event (2013 taper tantrum, 2018 IL&FS spread shock, March 2020 COVID sell-off).
  • Hypothetical scenario – designed "what-if" (e.g. oil at a very high level causing inflation and a bear steepener).
  • Reverse stress test – find the scenario that would cause an unacceptable loss.

Steps

  1. Identify risk factors (level, slope, curvature of the curve; credit spreads; liquidity).
  2. Design scenarios and shock sizes.
  3. Revalue each bond (full repricing, or ΔP ≈ MV × [−DModΔy + ½C(Δy)2]).
  4. Aggregate losses; compare with capital or risk limits.
  5. Decide actions (reduce duration, hedge, diversify credit, hold liquid assets).
  6. Report and repeat regularly.
HoldingMV (₹ cr)DModConvexity
10-year G-Sec506.960
5-year AAA PSU bond304.222
3-year AA corporate bond202.69

Portfolio DMod = (50 × 6.9 + 30 × 4.2 + 20 × 2.6)/100 = 5.23. Change for each bond = MV × [−DModΔy + ½C(Δy)2]. E.g. G-Sec, +100 bp: 50 × (−0.069 + 0.003) = −3.30.

ScenarioG-SecAAA PSUAA corpTotal (₹ cr)
Parallel +100 bp−3.30−1.23−0.51−5.04
Parallel +200 bp−6.30−2.39−1.00−9.69
Parallel −100 bp+3.60+1.29+0.53+5.42
Bear steepener (10Y +100, 5Y +50, 3Y +25 bp)−3.30−0.62−0.13−4.05
Credit shock (AAA +75 bp, AA +150 bp; G-Sec unchanged)0.00−0.93−0.76−1.69

Reading: a 200 bp parallel rise wipes out almost ₹9.7 crore (9.7%). Most of the risk sits in the 10-year G-Sec. If the risk limit is, say, a 6% loss under +200 bp, the manager must cut duration (sell some 10-year G-Sec) or hedge. Note the asymmetry (+5.42 vs −5.04) caused by convexity.

RBI requires banks to run stress tests and publishes system-wide stress-test results, including interest-rate risk in banks' bond portfolios, in its half-yearly Financial Stability Report. SEBI requires debt mutual funds to disclose a Potential Risk Class (PRC) matrix based on interest-rate risk (Macaulay duration) and credit risk.

1. What is a reverse stress test?

Starting from an unacceptable loss and working backwards to find which scenarios would cause it.

2. In the table, why is the steepener loss smaller than the parallel +100 bp loss?

Short and medium bonds are shocked by only 25–50 bp in the steepener, and they carry smaller durations.

4.4A Duration-based scenario analysis: yield-curve, credit-spread and liquidity shocks (Lecture 5)

Follows your professor's Lecture 5 exercise "Bond Portfolio Scenario Analysis" (Mr. Sharma's ₹25 lakh portfolio). The exercise tests four risks separately: interest-rate (parallel) risk, yield-curve risk, credit-spread risk and liquidity risk.

Section 4.4 revalued a portfolio with duration and convexity. In the classroom version you drop convexity and use duration alone: every bond loses roughly "its duration × the rise in its own yield" per cent of its value. The trick is that each holding may get a different shock. If all yields rise equally, it is a pure interest-rate shock. If short, medium and long yields rise by different amounts, the curve has changed shape (yield-curve risk). If only corporate yields rise because investors demand a bigger premium over G-Secs, it is a credit-spread shock. If nobody wants to buy at the quoted price, you take a flat cut on the whole value (liquidity haircut).

Scenario analysis (duration approach) estimates the change in a bond portfolio's value under a specified market situation by applying, to each holding, the approximation ΔP/P ≈ −Duration × ΔYield, and adding the results.
Yield-curve risk is the risk of loss from a non-parallel change in the yield curve (short, medium and long yields moving by different amounts, e.g. steepening or flattening).
Credit-spread risk is the risk that the spread of a corporate bond over the government yield widens, lowering its price even when G-Sec yields are unchanged. Spread duration is the approximate percentage price change of a bond for a 1 percentage-point (100 bp) change in its credit spread.
Liquidity risk is the risk that a bond cannot be sold quickly without accepting a price below its fair value; a liquidity haircut is the percentage deduction applied to market value to reflect this.

Estimated value change of a holding = Investment × (−Duration × Yield shock)
Credit shock: change = Investment × (−Spread duration × Spread change)
Liquidity shock: change = −Haircut % × Portfolio market value
Portfolio duration = Σ (wi × Di), where wi = investment in bond i / total portfolio

Shocks are in decimals: +2% = 0.02; +200 bp = 0.02; +0.5% = 0.005. Final portfolio value = Initial value + Σ changes. Loss is reported as a positive rupee amount. Use this approach exactly as the lecture does: the course treats the given durations as the price-sensitivity measure (a modified-duration-style figure), so no division by (1 + y) is needed.

Course vs industry: the lecture's durations are "simplified classroom assumptions" and ignore convexity, so gains and losses are symmetric. In practice a desk would use modified (or effective) duration plus a convexity term (section 4.4) and would reprice bonds fully for large shocks. For the exam, use the lecture's formula unless a question gives convexity.

A portfolio can pass a parallel-rate test and still fail a curve, credit or liquidity test. Naming the primary risk of each situation and matching it to the right action is exactly what the lecture's scoring rewards (value 12, loss 4, risk 2, decision 2 marks per situation).

From your course materialLecture 5 exercise – Mr. Sharma's ₹25 lakh portfolio, all five situations solved

Capital ₹25,00,000; objective regular income; risk tolerance low–moderate; horizon 5 years. Holdings: Government Bond ₹12,50,000 (duration 2.7 years); Corporate Bond A ₹7,50,000 (4.2 years); Corporate Bond B ₹5,00,000 (6.5 years). For each situation find the final portfolio value, total loss, primary risk and portfolio decision.

Portfolio duration (useful for checking Situation 1) = (12,50,000 × 2.7 + 7,50,000 × 4.2 + 5,00,000 × 6.5) / 25,00,000 = (33,75,000 + 31,50,000 + 32,50,000) / 25,00,000 = 3.91 years.

Situation 1 – all yields +2 percentage points
  1. Government Bond: 12,50,000 × (−2.7 × 0.02) = 12,50,000 × −0.054 = −₹67,500.
  2. Corporate Bond A: 7,50,000 × (−4.2 × 0.02) = 7,50,000 × −0.084 = −₹63,000.
  3. Corporate Bond B: 5,00,000 × (−6.5 × 0.02) = 5,00,000 × −0.13 = −₹65,000.
  4. Total loss = 67,500 + 63,000 + 65,000 = ₹1,95,500. Check: 25,00,000 × 3.91 × 0.02 = ₹1,95,500.
  5. Final value = 25,00,000 − 1,95,500 = ₹23,04,500.
  6. Primary risk: Interest-rate risk (equal, parallel rise in all yields). Decision: Reduce duration exposure by shifting toward shorter-maturity bonds.
Situation 2 – short +0.5%, medium +1.0%, long +1.5% (curve steepens)
  1. Map each holding to the curve by duration: Government Bond (2.7) = short; Bond A (4.2) = medium; Bond B (6.5) = long.
  2. Government Bond: 12,50,000 × (−2.7 × 0.005) = −₹16,875.
  3. Bond A: 7,50,000 × (−4.2 × 0.01) = −₹31,500.
  4. Bond B: 5,00,000 × (−6.5 × 0.015) = −₹48,750.
  5. Total loss = 16,875 + 31,500 + 48,750 = ₹97,125; final value = ₹24,02,875.
  6. Primary risk: Yield-curve risk (non-parallel shift). Decision: Increase diversification across maturities and issuers.
Situation 3 – short +1.5%, medium +1.0%, long +0.5% (curve flattens)
  1. Government Bond: 12,50,000 × (−2.7 × 0.015) = −₹50,625.
  2. Bond A: 7,50,000 × (−4.2 × 0.01) = −₹31,500.
  3. Bond B: 5,00,000 × (−6.5 × 0.005) = −₹16,250.
  4. Total loss = 50,625 + 31,500 + 16,250 = ₹98,375; final value = ₹24,01,625.
  5. Primary risk: Yield-curve risk. Decision: Increase diversification across maturities and issuers.
  6. Insight: Situations 2 and 3 have almost the same total loss for opposite curve moves. The big short-end holding is hit in Situation 3; the long-duration Bond B is hit in Situation 2.
Situation 4 – G-Sec yield unchanged, corporate spreads +200 bp (spread durations 4 and 6)
  1. Government Bond: no shock, change = ₹0.
  2. Bond A: 7,50,000 × (−4 × 0.02) = −₹60,000 (use the spread duration 4, not 4.2).
  3. Bond B: 5,00,000 × (−6 × 0.02) = −₹60,000 (spread duration 6, not 6.5).
  4. Total loss = ₹1,20,000; final value = ₹23,80,000.
  5. Primary risk: Credit spread risk. Decision: Reduce corporate bond exposure and increase government securities.
Situation 5 – 2% liquidity haircut on the portfolio
  1. Haircut = 2% × 25,00,000 = ₹50,000 (no duration needed; the whole market value is cut).
  2. Final value = 25,00,000 − 50,000 = ₹24,50,000.
  3. Primary risk: Liquidity risk. Decision: Maintain liquidity by keeping more readily saleable securities.
