Reading for Mr. Sharma (regular income, low–moderate risk): the worst case is the parallel +2% rise (7.82% of capital), then the credit shock (4.8%). Both point in the same direction: keep a large G-Sec core and limit long-duration corporate exposure. All losses are mark-to-market; if he holds each bond to maturity and no issuer defaults, the coupons and principal are still received.

Holdings: Treasury bond ₹20,00,000 (duration 1.8); PSU bond ₹18,00,000 (duration 4.5, spread duration 4.3); private corporate bond ₹12,00,000 (duration 7.0, spread duration 6.8). Total ₹50,00,000.

  1. Portfolio duration = (20,00,000 × 1.8 + 18,00,000 × 4.5 + 12,00,000 × 7.0)/50,00,000 = (36,00,000 + 81,00,000 + 84,00,000)/50,00,000 = 4.02.
  2. Steepener (short +25 bp, medium +75 bp, long +125 bp): 20,00,000 × 1.8 × 0.0025 = 9,000; 18,00,000 × 4.5 × 0.0075 = 60,750; 12,00,000 × 7.0 × 0.0125 = 1,05,000. Loss = ₹1,74,750 ⇒ value ₹48,25,250. Primary risk: yield-curve risk.
  3. Credit shock (PSU spread +100 bp, private corporate spread +250 bp, Treasury unchanged): 18,00,000 × 4.3 × 0.01 = 77,400; 12,00,000 × 6.8 × 0.025 = 2,04,000. Loss = ₹2,81,400 ⇒ value ₹47,18,600. Primary risk: credit-spread risk (the lower-rated bond causes 72% of the loss).
  4. Combined rate and liquidity shock: parallel +1% first: 36,000 + 81,000 + 84,000 = ₹2,01,000 loss ⇒ value ₹47,99,000; then a 1.5% liquidity haircut on the stressed value: 47,99,000 × 0.015 = ₹71,985 ⇒ final value ₹47,27,015, total loss ₹2,72,985.

Rule for combined shocks: apply the haircut to the value after the rate shock unless the question says to apply it to the original value.

India saw each of these shocks: a broad rise in G-Sec yields during the 2013 taper tantrum (interest-rate risk); sharp widening of spreads on NBFC and lower-rated corporate paper after the IL&FS defaults in 2018 (credit-spread risk); and, in April 2020, the winding up of six Franklin Templeton India debt schemes after redemptions could not be met from a market where their lower-rated bonds had become hard to sell (liquidity risk).

Traps: (1) using the ordinary duration instead of the given spread duration in a credit shock; (2) applying a shock to the G-Sec when the question says "government yield unchanged"; (3) writing the loss as a negative number in the "loss" box; (4) mapping holdings to the wrong part of the curve – use duration/maturity order (shortest = short end); (5) multiplying by 2 instead of 0.02.

1. In Mr. Sharma's portfolio, what loss would a 1% parallel rise cause?

25,00,000 × 3.91 × 0.01 = ₹97,750.

2. Why does Situation 4 leave the Government Bond unchanged?

A credit-spread shock changes only the premium of corporate yields over G-Sec yields; the G-Sec yield itself did not move.

3. Which primary risk and decision fit a 2% liquidity haircut?

Liquidity risk; keep more readily saleable securities (e.g. benchmark G-Secs).

4.5 Performance metrics: Sharpe ratio, alpha and beta

Sharpe ratio = (Rp − Rf) / σp
Beta βp = Cov(Rp, Rm) / Var(Rm)
Jensen's alpha αp = Rp − [Rf + βp(Rm − Rf)]
Treynor ratio = (Rp − Rf) / βp     Information ratio = (Rp − Rb) / Tracking error

Rp = portfolio return; Rf = risk-free rate (e.g. 91-day T-Bill yield); σp = standard deviation of portfolio returns; Rm = market (benchmark bond index) return; Rb = benchmark return; tracking error = standard deviation of (Rp − Rb).

Sharpe ratio: excess return per unit of total risk (standard deviation). Beta: sensitivity of the portfolio's return to the benchmark's return (systematic risk; for bond funds, similar in spirit to relative duration). Alpha (Jensen's): return earned above what CAPM predicts for the portfolio's beta – a measure of manager skill.

Rf = 6.5%, benchmark return Rm = 10%. Fund X: return 9.5%, σ 4%, β 0.80. Fund Y: return 11.0%, σ 6%, β 1.10.

MeasureFund XFund Y
Sharpe = (Rp − 6.5)/σ3.0/4 = 0.754.5/6 = 0.75
Treynor = (Rp − 6.5)/β3.0/0.8 = 3.75%4.5/1.1 = 4.09%
CAPM required return = 6.5 + β(3.5)9.30%10.35%
Jensen's alpha+0.20%+0.65%

Interpretation: both funds give the same reward per unit of total risk (Sharpe 0.75), so for an investor holding only one fund they are equally good. Per unit of systematic risk, and after adjusting for beta, Fund Y is better (higher Treynor and alpha). Use Sharpe for a stand-alone portfolio, Treynor/alpha for a portfolio that is part of a diversified whole.

Monthly returns (%): fund 1.2, 0.8, −0.5, 1.5, 0.3, 0.9; index 1.0, 0.6, −0.8, 1.4, 0.2, 0.7.

  1. Means: fund 4.2/6 = 0.70; index 3.1/6 = 0.5167.
  2. Deviation products (fund dev × index dev): 0.2417, 0.0083, 1.5800, 0.7067, 0.1267, 0.0367; sum = 2.7000.
  3. Squared index deviations: 0.2336, 0.0069, 1.7336, 0.7803, 0.1003, 0.0336; sum = 2.8883.
  4. β = 2.7000/2.8883 = 0.935 (same as Cov/Var = 0.5400/0.5777 with n − 1 = 5).
  5. Regression alpha (intercept) = 0.70 − 0.935 × 0.5167 = 0.217% per month.
  6. Excel: =SLOPE(fund,index) = 0.935; =INTERCEPT(fund,index) = 0.217.
  1. A fund returns 9.5% vs benchmark 8.8% with tracking error 2%: IR = 0.7/2 = 0.35.
  2. Attribution of a bond's annual return (10-year bond, YTM 7%, DMod 7, convexity 60; yield falls 0.50% in the year; roll-down adds 0.10%): income ≈ 7.00%; rate effect = −7 × (−0.005) + ½ × 60 × 0.0052 = 3.50% + 0.075% = 3.575%; roll-down 0.10%; total ≈ 10.675%. This decomposition (income, curve/rate, spread, selection) is the basis of fixed-income performance attribution.
MeasureRisk usedBest forHigher is
SharpeTotal (σ)Whole/undiversified portfolioBetter
TreynorSystematic (β)Part of a diversified portfolioBetter
Jensen's alphaβ via CAPMManager skill vs risk-adjusted benchmarkBetter (positive = outperformance)
BetaRelative to benchmarkMeasuring market exposure>1 aggressive, <1 defensive
Information ratioTracking errorActive manager vs benchmarkBetter

Indian mutual fund factsheets report standard deviation, Sharpe ratio, beta and (for debt funds) YTM, Macaulay duration and modified duration of each scheme; AMFI/SEBI rules make funds disclose benchmark returns alongside their own, which is the starting point for alpha.

1. A portfolio returned 8% with σ 2.5%; Rf 6.5%. Sharpe?

(8 − 6.5)/2.5 = 0.60.

2. What does a negative alpha mean?

The manager earned less than the CAPM-required return for the portfolio's beta – underperformance after adjusting for systematic risk.

Unit 4 exam kit

Define each; compare objective, belief about efficiency, turnover, cost, tracking error, duration policy; give examples (passive: buy-and-hold, indexing, Bharat Bond ETF, immunisation; active: rate anticipation, curve strategies, credit rotation, bond swaps, riding the curve); conclude that many managers combine them (core-satellite; contingent immunisation).

Ladder: equal amounts across maturities (handout: ₹2 lakh each in 1, 3, 5, 7, 10 years); regular liquidity, averages reinvestment rates, passive. Barbell: short + long, avoids middle; higher convexity for same duration (43.67 vs 26.20 for a 5-year bullet), performs well in large parallel moves and flattening, but yields less and suffers in steepening. Conclude with investor suitability.

Introduction. Interest-rate risk has two offsetting parts: price and reinvestment risk. Definition of immunisation. Conditions: PV equality, duration match, convexity of assets ≥ liabilities. Example: ₹10 lakh liability in 4 years at 8%: invest ₹7,35,029.85 split 50:50 in 2-year and 6-year zeros; surplus stays positive at 6% (₹553.57) and 10% (₹459.98). Limitations: parallel-shift assumption, rebalancing costs, default risk, single-factor model. Variants: cash-flow matching, contingent immunisation (₹8.92 crore cushion example). Indian use: insurers' ALM. Conclusion.

Define stress testing and scenario analysis; types (sensitivity, historical, hypothetical, reverse); steps; formula ΔV ≈ MV[−DModΔy + ½C(Δy)2]; the ₹100 crore table (−5.04 crore for +100 bp, −9.69 crore for +200 bp, −4.05 crore bear steepener, −1.69 crore credit shock); interpretation and actions; RBI FSR and SEBI PRC matrix; limitations (model risk, correlations change in crises, liquidity); conclusion.

Compute portfolio duration; for each situation apply Investment × (−Duration × shock) holding by holding (spread duration for credit shocks, a flat haircut for liquidity); total the loss and final value; name the primary risk (interest-rate, yield-curve, credit-spread, liquidity) and the matching action. Use the Mr. Sharma table in 4.4A (e.g. +2% parallel: loss ₹1,95,500, value ₹23,04,500) and conclude with a recommendation for the client's objective.

Use Worked Example 1 in 4.5: compute Sharpe, Treynor, CAPM return and Jensen's alpha for both funds; note equal Sharpe (0.75) but Fund Y's higher Treynor (4.09% vs 3.75%) and alpha (0.65% vs 0.20%); recommend Y for an investor who holds a diversified portfolio and X or Y equally for a single-fund investor; discuss limitations (benchmark choice, CAPM assumptions, short history, non-normal bond returns) and suggest information ratio and attribution analysis as complements.

Unit 5 — Future of Bond Markets: Sustainability and Innovation

Syllabus: sustainability-linked bonds; green bonds and their alignment with SDGs; digital and tokenised bonds – blockchain applications; Excel and Bloomberg for valuation and portfolio analysis. (8 teaching hours, CO5; SDG focus: Industry, Innovation and Infrastructure). This unit also covers every pointer of the 15 Unit 5 presentation topics.

5.1 Green bonds: concept, characteristics and market development

A green bond is an ordinary bond with a promise attached to the use of the money: every rupee raised goes only to environment-friendly projects (solar parks, metro rail, green buildings), and the issuer reports on where the money went and what impact it had. The investor's credit risk is still the issuer's normal credit risk.

A green bond is a fixed-income instrument whose proceeds are exclusively applied to finance or re-finance, in part or in full, new and/or existing eligible green projects with clear environmental benefits, and which is aligned with recognised standards such as the ICMA Green Bond Principles (use of proceeds, project selection, management of proceeds and reporting).

Climate transition needs enormous long-term capital; green bonds channel bond-market money into it and give investors verifiable environmental impact alongside a normal bond return. The syllabus links this to SDG 9 (Industry, Innovation and Infrastructure).

Evolution

  • 2007 – European Investment Bank's "Climate Awareness Bond", widely regarded as the first green bond.
  • 2008 – World Bank's first green bond.
  • 2014 – ICMA publishes the Green Bond Principles; corporate issuance begins to grow rapidly.
  • 2015 – First green bonds from India: Yes Bank (rupee) and EXIM Bank (US dollar).
  • 2016 onwards – sovereign green bonds (Poland was the first sovereign issuer, December 2016); SEBI issues green bond disclosure requirements (2017).
  • 2021 – Ghaziabad Nagar Nigam issues India's first green municipal bond.
  • 2023 – India's first Sovereign Green Bonds (5.6).

Characteristics

  • Use-of-proceeds restricted to eligible green projects.
  • Same seniority, coupon structure and credit risk as the issuer's conventional bonds.
  • A published green bond framework and usually an external review.
  • Tracking/ring-fencing of proceeds.
  • Annual allocation and impact reporting until full allocation (and often to maturity).
  • Often priced at a small "greenium" (slightly lower yield than a comparable conventional bond) because of strong investor demand.
PointGreen bondConventional bond
Use of proceedsOnly eligible green projectsAny purpose
DisclosureFramework, allocation and impact reportsStandard financial disclosure
External reviewRecommended (SPO, verification, certification)Not needed
Investor baseConventional + ESG/impact investorsConventional
PricingSame credit risk; possible greeniumMarket yield
Cost for issuerExtra reporting/verification costLower issuance overhead

Types of green bonds (ICMA)

  1. Standard green use-of-proceeds bond – recourse to the issuer's full balance sheet.
  2. Green revenue bond – recourse only to revenues/fees of a project (e.g. municipal water charges).
  3. Green project bond – recourse only to the specific project's assets and cash flows.
  4. Green securitised bond – backed by a pool of green assets (e.g. solar loans, green mortgages).

Related labels: social bonds (social projects), sustainability bonds (mix of green and social), blue bonds (oceans/water), transition bonds (decarbonising hard-to-abate sectors).

Issuers, investors, benefits, limitations

Issuers: supranationals (EIB, World Bank, ADB), sovereigns, municipalities, banks (SBI, Yes Bank), PSUs (NTPC, IREDA, PFC, REC), renewable-energy and infrastructure companies.

Investors: ESG funds, pension and insurance funds, central banks, sovereign wealth funds, banks, retail via funds.

Benefits: diversified investor base, possible greenium, reputational benefit, transparency, channels capital to climate goals, helps investors meet ESG mandates.

Limitations: greenwashing risk, reporting cost, lack of a single global taxonomy, small greenium, limited pipeline of eligible projects, impact hard to measure.

Greenium = Yield on comparable conventional bond − Yield on green bond     Annual interest saving ≈ Issue size × Greenium

Illustration: a sovereign sells ₹8,000 crore of 10-year green bonds at 7.29% when an otherwise identical conventional bond yields 7.35%. Greenium = 6 bp; interest saving = 8,000 × 0.0006 = ₹4.8 crore per year.

1. Does a green bond have lower credit risk than the issuer's normal bond?

No (for a standard use-of-proceeds bond) – it ranks the same; only the use of funds differs.

2. What is a greenium?

The yield discount at which a green bond trades compared with an equivalent conventional bond of the same issuer.

5.2 Green Bond Principles and the issuance framework

The Green Bond Principles (GBP), published by the International Capital Market Association (ICMA), are voluntary process guidelines that promote transparency, disclosure and integrity in the green bond market through four core components and two key recommendations.

Core componentWhat the issuer must do
1. Use of proceedsUse funds only for eligible green projects, described in the legal documentation, with clear environmental benefits; disclose share of refinancing.
2. Process for project evaluation and selectionExplain environmental objectives, how projects are chosen against eligibility criteria, exclusion criteria and how environmental/social risks are managed.
3. Management of proceedsCredit net proceeds to a sub-account/sub-portfolio or track them internally; disclose treatment of unallocated funds.
4. ReportingAnnual allocation report (projects financed, amounts) and impact report with quantitative indicators, until full allocation.

Key recommendations: (a) publish a Green Bond Framework explaining alignment with the four components and the issuer's sustainability strategy; (b) obtain external review – second-party opinion (SPO) before issue, verification (e.g. of allocation) after issue, certification against a standard (e.g. the Climate Bonds Standard), or a green bond rating/score.

Issuance process (step by step)

  1. Identify eligible projects and set the green objectives.
  2. Draft the Green Bond Framework (four components).
  3. Obtain a pre-issuance external review (SPO).
  4. Obtain credit rating, board approvals; structure the bond (tenor, coupon).
  5. Market to investors, including ESG investors; price and issue.
  6. Track and allocate proceeds; hold unallocated money in approved instruments.
  7. Post-issuance verification of allocation.
  8. Publish annual allocation and impact reports.

Transparency and disclosure are what make a bond "green" in investors' eyes. Without credible reporting and review, the label has no value and the bond may be accused of greenwashing.

5.3 Eligible projects, environmental impact and the SDGs

Eligible categoryExample projectsImpact indicatorMain SDG
Renewable energySolar, wind, small hydro, green hydrogenMW installed; MWh generated; tCO2e avoidedSDG 7, 13
Energy efficiencyLED street lighting, efficient motors, smart gridsMWh savedSDG 7, 12
Clean transportationMetro rail, electric buses, railway electrificationPassenger-km; tCO2e avoidedSDG 9, 11
Sustainable water and wastewaterSewage treatment, water recyclingLitres treated/savedSDG 6, 11
Pollution prevention and circular economyWaste-to-energy, recyclingTonnes of waste divertedSDG 12
Climate adaptationFlood defences, climate-resilient agriculturePeople/hectares protectedSDG 13
Green buildingsCertified energy-efficient buildingsEnergy use per m2SDG 11
Natural resources and biodiversityAfforestation, sustainable forestryHectares restoredSDG 15

The UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) are 17 global goals adopted by UN member states in 2015 as part of the 2030 Agenda. Green bonds map most directly to SDG 7 (Affordable and Clean Energy), SDG 9 (Industry, Innovation and Infrastructure), SDG 11 (Sustainable Cities and Communities), SDG 12 (Responsible Consumption and Production) and SDG 13 (Climate Action).

Challenges in measuring SDG contribution: no standard metrics across issuers; attribution when projects are co-financed; baselines and counterfactuals ("what would have happened anyway"); double counting; long time lags before impact; data quality and cost of verification.

Indian green bond proceeds have gone largely into renewable energy (solar and wind parks financed by IREDA, NTPC, SBI and private renewable developers), metro rail and electric mobility – mapping to SDG 7, 9, 11 and 13.

5.4 Sustainability-linked bonds (SLBs): structure, KPIs/SPTs and pricing

A green bond says "we will spend this money on green projects". An SLB says "spend the money however we like, but we promise to hit a sustainability target by a date – and if we miss it, we pay you a higher coupon". The penalty makes the promise credible.

A sustainability-linked bond is a bond whose financial or structural characteristics (typically the coupon) vary depending on whether the issuer achieves predefined Sustainability Performance Targets (SPTs), measured by Key Performance Indicators (KPIs), within a set timeline. The proceeds may be used for general corporate purposes.

Evolution

Enel (Italy) issued the first SLB in 2019, with its coupon linked to its share of renewable capacity. ICMA published the Sustainability-Linked Bond Principles (SLBP) in June 2020. Since then corporates in carbon-intensive sectors (cement, steel, utilities) and some sovereigns (Chile, Uruguay) have issued SLBs. Indian companies such as UltraTech Cement have issued US-dollar SLBs linked to carbon-emission-intensity targets.

Five core components of the SLB Principles

  1. Selection of KPIs – relevant, core and material to the issuer's business; measurable on a consistent basis; externally verifiable; benchmarkable.
  2. Calibration of SPTs – ambitious (beyond business-as-usual), consistent with the issuer's strategy, set against a baseline, with a timeline (target observation date).
  3. Bond characteristics – the financial consequence of hitting/missing the SPT: coupon step-up (most common), step-down, higher redemption premium.
  4. Reporting – at least annual updates on KPI performance.
  5. Verification – independent external verification of KPI performance against SPTs at least annually and at each observation date (mandatory under the SLBP).

KPIs, SPTs, baselines and target setting

TermMeaningExample
KPIThe sustainability metric being measuredScope 1 + 2 CO2 emissions per tonne of cement
BaselineStarting value of the KPI in a reference year0.60 tCO2/t in base year
SPTThe target value of the KPI by a dateReduce intensity by 20% by year 3
Observation dateDate when achievement is testedEnd of year 3
Trigger eventMissing the SPT (or failing to report/verify)Coupon steps up

Common KPIs: greenhouse-gas emission intensity, share of renewable energy, energy/water intensity, waste recycled, women in leadership, safety (lost-time injury rate). Design challenges: unambitious or already-nearly-achieved targets; small step-ups (often 12.5–25 bp) that barely penalise; observation dates near maturity; call options that let issuers redeem before the step-up; KPI methodology changes.

Extra coupon cost = Issue size × step-up × number of years affected
Investor IRR: solve Price = Σ (coupont incl. step-up)/(1+IRR)t + F/(1+IRR)n

₹500 crore 5-year SLB, 7.50% annual coupon, issued at par. If the issuer misses its SPT at the end of year 3, the coupon steps up by 25 bp for years 4 and 5.

  1. Cash flows per ₹100 if missed: 7.50, 7.50, 7.50, 7.75, 107.75.
  2. Extra cost to issuer = 500 × 0.0025 × 2 = ₹2.5 crore.
  3. Investor's IRR if the target is missed: solve 100 = 7.5/(1+r) + 7.5/(1+r)2 + 7.5/(1+r)3 + 7.75/(1+r)4 + 107.75/(1+r)5 ⇒ r = 7.59% (vs 7.50% if achieved).
  4. Value of the stepped-up cash flows at a 7.50% market yield = ₹100.36 per ₹100 – the step-up is worth about 36 paise per ₹100 to investors, which is why SLBs may be priced slightly tighter when a miss is considered likely.
  5. Investor considerations: probability of missing; credibility of KPIs; size of step-up vs credit spread; call features; verification.

SLB vs green bond (presentation topic 9)

BasisGreen bondSustainability-linked bond
ApproachUse-of-proceedsPerformance-based (outcome at issuer level)
Use of fundsOnly eligible green projectsGeneral corporate purposes
Project eligibilityRequiredNot required
TargetsNo KPIs/SPTs neededKPIs and SPTs central
CouponFixed (or normal floating)Varies with SPT achievement (step-up/down)
ReportingAllocation and impact reportsKPI performance reports with verification
PrinciplesICMA GBP (2014)ICMA SLBP (2020)
SuitsIssuers with identifiable green projectsIssuers transitioning whole business (cement, steel)
Risk/returnNormal credit risk; greeniumCredit risk + variable coupon; possible reward if target missed
CriticismFungibility of money; greenwashingWeak targets; "penalty" too small
1. Name the five SLBP core components.

KPI selection, SPT calibration, bond characteristics, reporting, verification.

2. ₹300 crore SLB, 50 bp step-up for the last 2 years. Extra cost?

300 × 0.005 × 2 = ₹3 crore.

5.5 Greenwashing, transparency and credibility

Greenwashing is the practice of making misleading, unsubstantiated or exaggerated claims about the environmental benefits of an issuer, project or security, so that it appears more sustainable than it really is.

Why it matters in green bonds: investors pay a greenium and accept reporting obligations in exchange for real impact; greenwashing misleads them, misallocates capital, and undermines the whole market's credibility.

Forms: misallocation of proceeds (money spent on non-eligible assets); vague eligibility criteria; refinancing old projects with no new impact; labelling "less brown" activities as green; selective or no impact reporting; issuer-level activities contradicting the green label (e.g. expanding fossil-fuel capacity while issuing green bonds).

Safeguards: (1) clear taxonomy of eligible activities; (2) published framework; (3) pre-issuance second-party opinion; (4) post-issuance verification of allocation; (5) certification (e.g. Climate Bonds Standard); (6) audited impact reporting with quantitative indicators; (7) regulatory disclosure and penalties; (8) investor due diligence and engagement.

SEBI's revised framework for green debt securities (2023) added explicit guidance to avoid greenwashing – for example, issuers should not use misleading labels, should disclose where proceeds are allocated and should not make claims unsupported by evidence. In the EU, the European Green Bond Standard (adopted 2023) creates a voluntary "EuGB" label tied to the EU taxonomy with registered external reviewers.

5.6 India: Sovereign Green Bonds and the regulatory framework

  • Announcement: Union Budget 2022–23 announced Sovereign Green Bonds (SGrBs) as part of government borrowing.
  • Framework: India's Sovereign Green Bond Framework was issued by the Ministry of Finance in November 2022; the Norwegian reviewer CICERO rated it "Medium Green" with a "Good" governance score.
  • First issues: RBI auctioned the first SGrBs in two tranches of ₹8,000 crore each in January and February 2023 (5-year and 10-year), ₹16,000 crore in FY 2022–23, at a small greenium of a few basis points relative to comparable G-Secs.
  • Eligible spending: public-sector projects in renewable energy, energy efficiency, clean transportation (e.g. metro, electric mobility), climate adaptation, sustainable water and waste management, pollution control, green buildings and natural resource conservation.
  • Exclusions: for example, fossil-fuel-based power, nuclear power, hydro projects above 25 MW, landfills, and sectors such as alcohol, weapons, tobacco and gaming.
  • Governance: a Green Finance Working Committee selects and evaluates eligible projects; proceeds go to the Consolidated Fund of India and an equivalent amount is allocated to eligible expenditure; the government publishes allocation and impact reports.
  • Access: retail investors can bid through RBI Retail Direct (non-competitive bids).

SEBI: first issued green bond disclosure requirements in 2017; green debt securities are defined under the NCS Regulations 2021; the 2023 revision widened the definition (including blue bonds for water/oceans, yellow bonds for solar, and transition bonds) and added anti-greenwashing guidance. RBI: issued a framework for green deposits by banks and NBFCs (2023).

Sovereign issuance creates a green benchmark curve that helps price corporate green bonds, signals policy commitment to climate goals, and deepens the domestic ESG investor base.

5.7 Digital bonds

A digital bond is a bond whose issuance, record of ownership, servicing (coupons, redemption) and settlement are handled natively on digital infrastructure – typically a distributed ledger – rather than through paper certificates and multiple intermediary databases reconciled after the fact.

StageTraditional issuanceDigital (DLT) issuance
DocumentationPaper/PDF term sheets, manual book-buildingTerms encoded as data and smart contracts
Ownership recordDepositories/registrars, separate ledgers reconciledSingle shared ledger ("golden record")
SettlementT+1 or longer via clearing corporationNear-instant atomic DvP (T+0) possible
Coupon paymentPaying agent computes and paysAutomated by smart contract
IntermediariesMany (arranger, registrar, custodian, paying agent)Fewer; roles change
TransparencyLimited, periodicReal-time for permitted participants

Digital issuance lifecycle: (1) structuring and on-boarding of investors with KYC; (2) creation ("minting") of the bond tokens on the ledger; (3) primary allocation and atomic settlement against digital cash; (4) automated servicing (coupons, corporate actions); (5) secondary transfers; (6) redemption and "burning" of tokens.

Advantages: faster settlement, less counterparty and operational risk, lower issuance and servicing cost, transparency, programmability, easier smaller issues. Challenges and risks: legal recognition of on-ledger ownership, availability of on-chain cash (CBDC/tokenised deposits), interoperability with legacy systems, cyber and technology risk, low secondary liquidity so far, regulatory uncertainty.

International examples: the World Bank's "bond-i" (2018, on a blockchain, arranged by Commonwealth Bank of Australia); the European Investment Bank's digital bond on Ethereum (2021) settled with a Banque de France central-bank digital currency experiment; Hong Kong government's tokenised green bond (2023); Siemens' digital bond on a public blockchain (2023). India: G-Secs are already fully electronic (SGL accounts, NDS-OM), and RBI's wholesale CBDC (e₹-W) pilot launched in November 2022 used digital rupee for settling secondary-market G-Sec transactions – a step towards DLT-based settlement.

5.8 Tokenised bonds: structure, fractional ownership and trading

Tokenisation is the process of representing ownership rights in an asset as digital tokens on a distributed ledger. A tokenised bond is a bond (or an interest in a conventional bond) whose units are recorded and transferred as tokens, each token carrying the right to a pro-rata share of coupons and principal.

Structure: issuer (or SPV/custodian holding the underlying bond) → token smart contract that records holders → registrar/transfer-agent role performed by the ledger → investor wallets (whitelisted after KYC) → cash leg in CBDC, tokenised bank deposits or stablecoins (where permitted) → trustee/legal wrapper confirming token = legal ownership.

  1. A ₹10,00,000 bond with an 8% annual coupon is split into 1,000 tokens of ₹1,000 each (10,00,000/1,000).
  2. Annual coupon per token = 1,000 × 8% = ₹80; an investor with 5 tokens (₹5,000 invested) receives ₹400 automatically.
  3. A retail investor who could not buy a ₹10 lakh bond can now hold a slice for ₹1,000 and sell it peer-to-peer on a permitted platform.

Trading and transfer: tokens move between whitelisted wallets; transfer rules (lock-ins, investor eligibility, holding limits) are coded in. Settlement: atomic delivery-versus-payment on the ledger removes settlement lag and principal risk. Benefits: fractional ownership and wider access, 24×7 transferability, instant settlement, lower costs, transparent ownership, programmable compliance. Limitations: legal and regulatory recognition, custody and private-key risk, fragmented platforms and thin liquidity, need for on-chain money, cyber risk, and in India the requirement that securities be held in dematerialised form with SEBI-registered depositories.

5.9 Smart contracts and automated bond market operations

A smart contract is self-executing computer code stored on a blockchain that automatically performs pre-agreed actions (such as transferring tokens or cash) when specified conditions are met, without needing a central party to execute them.

Think of a vending machine: insert the right amount, press a button, and the machine delivers – no shopkeeper required. A bond smart contract is a vending machine for bond obligations: on each coupon date it "delivers" the coupon to every holder recorded on the ledger; at maturity it pays principal and cancels the tokens.

Applications across the bond lifecycle

Lifecycle eventWhat the smart contract automates
IssuanceMinting tokens, allocating to subscribers, settling against cash (DvP)
ComplianceWhitelisting KYC'd investors, blocking transfers to ineligible wallets, enforcing lock-ins and holding limits
Coupon paymentComputing each holder's coupon (holding × rate × day count) on the record date and paying on the coupon date
Floating/SLB couponsReading a benchmark rate or verified KPI from an "oracle" and resetting the coupon (e.g. step-up if SPT missed)
Corporate actionsCalls, puts, partial redemptions, covenant tests
Maturity/redemptionPaying principal and "burning" (cancelling) tokens
ReportingImmutable audit trail for regulators and trustees
on CouponDate(d): require(d in schedule) for holder in register.at(RecordDate(d)): amount = holder.tokens * FaceValuePerToken * CouponRate / Frequency pay(holder.wallet, amount) # digital cash leg on MaturityDate: for holder in register: pay(holder.wallet, holder.tokens * FaceValuePerToken) burn(holder.tokens)

Benefits: no manual intervention, fewer errors and reconciliation breaks, faster and cheaper servicing, real-time compliance, transparency. Risks: coding bugs and exploits (code is hard to change once deployed), oracle risk (wrong external data triggers wrong payments), legal enforceability and dispute resolution, key-management and cyber risk, rigidity when unforeseen events occur (e.g. a holiday or a restructuring), and regulatory uncertainty.

A 3-year ₹100 crore tokenised SLB pays 8% annually; if verified emissions intensity at end of year 2 exceeds the SPT, coupon steps up by 25 bp for year 3. The verifier posts the KPI result to the oracle; the contract reads it and, if the target was missed, pays 8.25% (₹8.25 crore instead of ₹8 crore) in year 3 automatically – linking Units 5.4 and 5.9.

5.10 Blockchain and distributed ledger technology (DLT) in bond markets

A distributed ledger is a database shared and synchronised across multiple participants, in which records are updated through a consensus mechanism rather than by a single central authority. A blockchain is a type of distributed ledger in which transactions are grouped into blocks, each cryptographically linked (hashed) to the previous one, making the history tamper-evident.

  • Fundamentals: blocks, cryptographic hashes, digital signatures (public/private keys), consensus (proof-of-work, proof-of-stake, or voting-based consensus in permissioned networks), immutability.
  • Permissioned vs permissionless: financial markets mostly use permissioned DLT (only approved, identified nodes) for privacy, control and regulatory compliance; some experiments use public chains with permissioning at the token level.
  • Architecture relevant to markets: (1) ledger/consensus layer; (2) asset layer (security tokens); (3) smart-contract layer (lifecycle logic); (4) identity and access layer (KYC, permissions); (5) cash/payment layer (CBDC, tokenised deposits); (6) integration layer with legacy systems and oracles.
  • Uses in bond markets: issuance, golden-record ownership registers, atomic clearing and settlement, collateral mobility (e.g. repo), automated servicing, regulatory reporting.
  • Transparency and auditability: every transfer is time-stamped and traceable; regulators can be observer nodes.
  • Reduction of intermediaries: fewer reconciliations between depositories, custodians, registrars and paying agents; roles shift to technology operators and trustees.
  • Security and privacy: strong cryptography, but trades must remain confidential between counterparties (handled with private channels, zero-knowledge techniques); key management is critical.
  • Adoption challenges: legal finality of settlement, scalability, interoperability among ledgers, on-chain cash, standardisation, cost of migration, governance of shared networks, and regulatory approval.

DLT could shrink settlement from T+1 to near real time, free up collateral and lower costs – but India's already-efficient electronic G-Sec infrastructure (SGL, NDS-OM, CCIL) means the gains must be weighed against migration risk, which is why RBI is proceeding through pilots.

1. Why do bond markets prefer permissioned DLT?

Participants must be identified (KYC/AML), trade data must stay confidential, and regulators need control and accountability.

2. What is an oracle?

A trusted data feed that brings off-chain information (benchmark rates, KPI results, holidays) into the smart contract.

3. What is atomic settlement?

Both the security and the cash legs transfer in one indivisible transaction – either both happen or neither does.

Premium/par/discount test: =IF(price>100,"Premium",IF(price<100,"Discount","Par")). Yield sensitivity: list yields 6%–14% in a column, compute prices with PRICE, chart as a scatter – the convex price-yield curve.

(b) Excel-based portfolio analysis (presentation topic 15)

₹1 crore portfolio of semi-annual G-Secs: ₹40 lakh in a 10-year 7% bond (yield 7.5%), ₹35 lakh in a 5-year 6.5% bond (yield 7.0%), ₹25 lakh in a 2-year 6% bond (yield 6.5%).

  1. MDURATION (frequency 2) gives 7.0371, 4.1931 and 1.8536 respectively.
  2. Weights 0.40, 0.35, 0.25. Portfolio DMod =SUMPRODUCT(weights, mdurations) = 0.40 × 7.0371 + 0.35 × 4.1931 + 0.25 × 1.8536 = 2.8148 + 1.4676 + 0.4634 = 4.7458.
  3. Estimated loss for +50 bp: 1,00,00,000 × 4.7458 × 0.005 = ₹2,37,291 (about 2.37%). Portfolio DV01 = 1,00,00,000 × 4.7458 × 0.0001 = ₹4,746.
  4. Use Data → What-If Analysis → Scenario Manager or a Data Table for shifts of −200 to +200 bp, and conditional formatting to flag limit breaches – this is the stress test of Unit 4 in a spreadsheet.

(c) Bloomberg for valuation and portfolio analysis

Load the bond (e.g. an Indian G-Sec with the Govt key) → DES for terms → YAS to convert price ↔ yield, see spreads, modified duration, convexity and risk → GC to place it on the curve → PORT to load the portfolio, view duration and key-rate exposures, run rate and spread scenarios and performance attribution → export with BDP/BDH/BDS to Excel.

Unit 5 exam kit

Definition (use-of-proceeds bond for eligible green projects). GBP (ICMA, 2014): (1) use of proceeds, (2) project evaluation and selection, (3) management of proceeds, (4) reporting; plus framework and external review. Example: India's Sovereign Green Bonds (₹16,000 crore in FY 2022–23, framework rated "Medium Green" by CICERO). Conclusion: principles build credibility and guard against greenwashing.

Define smart contract; list lifecycle applications (issuance, KYC whitelisting, automated coupons, SLB step-ups via oracles, redemption and token burning); benefits (automation, fewer errors, faster settlement, transparency); risks (bugs, oracle, legal enforceability, key management); illustrate with the ₹10 lakh bond split into 1,000 tokens paying ₹80 per token automatically; conclude on India's cautious, pilot-based approach (e₹-W pilot 2022).

Introduction on sustainable finance. Green bonds: definition, GBP components, eligible projects, reporting. SLBs: definition, five SLBP components, KPI/SPT, step-up mechanism with the ₹500 crore example (₹2.5 crore extra cost, investor IRR 7.59%). Comparison table (approach, use of funds, targets, coupon, reporting, principles, suitability, risks). Indian examples: SGrBs; Indian SLB issues by carbon-intensive companies. Critical evaluation: greenwashing and weak-target risks; role of verification. Conclusion: complementary tools – green bonds for identifiable projects, SLBs for whole-company transition.

Introduction: limits of the traditional process (many intermediaries, reconciliations, T+1 settlement, large ticket sizes). DLT fundamentals; digital bonds (lifecycle); tokenisation and fractional ownership; smart contracts; benefits (atomic DvP, lower cost, transparency, retail access, programmability); examples (World Bank bond-i 2018, EIB 2021, Hong Kong tokenised green bond 2023, RBI e₹-W G-Sec settlement pilot 2022); challenges (legal finality, interoperability, on-chain cash, cyber risk, liquidity, regulation); conclusion: evolution rather than revolution in India.

Facts: the company needs ₹500 crore, has a few renewable-power projects but most emissions come from clinker production; investors are ESG-focused. Answer structure: (1) identify objectives (cost, credibility, transition); (2) green bond option – only eligible projects qualify, limited pipeline, greenium; (3) SLB option – general purpose, KPI = CO2 intensity, SPT e.g. 20% reduction by year 3, 25 bp step-up costing ₹2.5 crore if missed; (4) risks (greenwashing accusations; weak SPTs; verification cost); (5) recommendation – SLB for whole-company transition, possibly alongside a green bond for the renewable projects; (6) governance – framework, SPO, annual verified reporting; (7) conclusion.

Power BI

  1. Get Data → import a yield table (Date, Maturity, Yield) from Excel/CSV (e.g. FBIL daily curves you download).
  2. Line chart: Maturity on the X-axis, Yield on the Y-axis, Date in the legend or a slicer ⇒ interactive yield-curve comparison across dates.
  3. What-if parameter (Modeling → New parameter) named "Shift (bp)" from −200 to +200; measure Shifted Yield = SUM(Curve[Yield]) + 'Shift (bp)'[Shift (bp) Value]/100 (yields in %).
  4. Bond valuation measure with a cash-flow table (Bond, t, CF): Price = SUMX(CashFlows, CashFlows[CF] / (1 + [Yield])^CashFlows[t]); MacDur = DIVIDE(SUMX(CashFlows, CashFlows[t]*CashFlows[CF]/(1+[Yield])^CashFlows[t]), [Price]). DAX also offers Excel-style financial functions (PRICE, YIELD, DURATION, MDURATION, XIRR).
  5. Dashboard: KPI cards (portfolio value, duration, DV01), a stress-test bar chart by scenario, a matrix of holdings by rating and maturity bucket (ladder view), and a 10Y–2Y spread line over time.

R (base R, no packages needed)

# 1. Price, YTM, duration, convexity bond_price <- function(F, c, y, n, m = 1) { t <- 1:(n * m) cf <- rep(F * c / m, n * m); cf[n * m] <- cf[n * m] + F sum(cf / (1 + y / m)^t) } bond_price(1000, 0.09, 0.10, 6) # 956.4474 (Practice Q3) ytm <- function(P, F, c, n, m = 1) uniroot(function(y) bond_price(F, c, y, n, m) - P, c(-0.5, 1), tol = 1e-10)$root ytm(1050, 1000, 0.11, 7) # 0.09974 (Practice Q4, exact) risk <- function(F, c, y, n) { t <- 1:n; cf <- rep(F * c, n); cf[n] <- cf[n] + F pv <- cf / (1 + y)^t; P <- sum(pv); mac <- sum(t * pv) / P c(price = P, macaulay = mac, modified = mac / (1 + y), convexity = sum(t * (t + 1) * pv) / (P * (1 + y)^2)) } risk(1000, 0.08, 0.10, 5) # 924.18 4.2814 3.8922 20.097 # 2. Bootstrapping and forward rates from par yields par <- c(0.06, 0.065, 0.07); d <- numeric(3) for (n in 1:3) d[n] <- (1 - par[n] * sum(d[seq_len(n - 1)])) / (1 + par[n]) spot <- d^(-1 / (1:3)) - 1 # 0.06000 0.06516 0.07048 fwd <- d[-3] / d[-1] - 1 # 0.07035 0.08119 # 3. Plot the handout's four curve shapes mat <- c(1, 2, 5, 10, 20) curves <- cbind(Normal = c(5.5, 5.8, 6.3, 6.8, 7.2), Flat = c(6.0, 6.1, 6.1, 6.2, 6.2), Inverted = c(7.5, 7.2, 6.7, 6.2, 6.0), Humped = c(5.5, 6.2, 6.8, 6.3, 6.0)) matplot(mat, curves, type = "b", pch = 1:4, xlab = "Maturity (years)", ylab = "Yield (%)") legend("bottomright", colnames(curves), lty = 1:4, pch = 1:4) # 4. Beta, alpha, Sharpe; stress test fund <- c(1.2, 0.8, -0.5, 1.5, 0.3, 0.9); index <- c(1.0, 0.6, -0.8, 1.4, 0.2, 0.7) coef(lm(fund ~ index)) # (Intercept) 0.217 = alpha, index 0.935 = beta mv <- c(50, 30, 20); dm <- c(6.9, 4.2, 2.6); cx <- c(60, 22, 9) shock <- function(dy) sum(mv * (-dm * dy + 0.5 * cx * dy^2)) sapply(c(-0.01, 0.01, 0.02), shock) # 5.422 -5.038 -9.692 (Rs crore)

Jamovi

  • Beta and alpha: enter fund and index returns in two columns → Analyses → Regression → Linear Regression; Dependent = fund, Covariate = index. The slope estimate is beta (0.935), the intercept is alpha (0.217); R2 shows how much of the fund's variation the benchmark explains.
  • Sharpe inputs: Exploration → Descriptives → mean and standard deviation of returns; then Sharpe = (mean − Rf)/SD.
  • Correlation of yields: Regression → Correlation Matrix across 2Y, 5Y and 10Y yield changes (shows how parallel moves are).
  • Rj module: run the R code above inside jamovi for bond pricing.

Orange3 (visual data mining)

  • File widget (load yields or bond list) → Data Table (inspect).
  • Feature Constructor: create new columns with formulas, e.g. price of a zero 1000/(1+yield)**maturity, or spread corp_yield - gsec_yield.
  • Scatter Plot: maturity vs yield (the curve) or yield vs price (convexity).
  • Linear Regression + Test and Score: regress fund returns on index returns (beta) or model credit spreads on ratings and maturity.
  • Distributions / Box Plot: compare return distributions of bond funds (risk).

Course material — every question solved

All questions from your Practice Numerical Questions sheet, the Malkiel's Theorems & Duration handout, and the Yield Curve Student Handout, solved in full. Try each one first; answers are in the expandable panels.

A. Practice Numerical Questions (Q1–Q8)

From your course materialQuestion 1 – Zero-coupon bond

Face value ₹2,000; maturity 6 years; required return 8%. (1) Present value. (2) If market price is ₹1,180, undervalued or overvalued?

Full solution
  1. Formula: P = F/(1+y)n.
  2. 1.086 = 1.586874.
  3. PV = 2,000/1.586874 = ₹1,260.34.
  4. Market price ₹1,180 < intrinsic value ₹1,260.34 ⇒ the bond is undervalued by ₹80.34 ⇒ buy.
  5. Cross-check: at ₹1,180 the bond yields (2,000/1,180)1/6 − 1 = 9.19%, which is more than the required 8% – the same conclusion.

From your course materialQuestion 2 – Zero-coupon bond (YTM)

Face value ₹1,000; price ₹720; 5 years. (1) YTM. (2) Price if the required return decreases to 7%.

Full solution
  1. YTM = (F/P)1/n − 1 = (1,000/720)1/5 − 1 = 1.3888890.2 − 1 = 6.79%.
  2. Price at 7%: 1.075 = 1.402552; P = 1,000/1.402552 = ₹712.99.
  3. Interpretation: the bond's current yield-to-maturity is 6.79%, so a required return of 7% is in fact 21 bp higher than today's yield; the price therefore falls slightly (₹720 → ₹712.99, −₹7.01), exactly as the inverse price-yield relationship predicts. (If the required return fell below 6.79%, the price would rise above ₹720.)

From your course materialQuestion 3 – Coupon bond valuation

6-year bond, face ₹1,000, coupon 9%, required return 10%. (1) Intrinsic value. (2) Market price ₹930 – premium or discount? (3) Explain pull-to-par.

Full solution
  1. Annual coupon = 9% × 1,000 = ₹90.
  2. PVIFA(10%, 6) = (1 − 1.1−6)/0.10 = 4.355261 ⇒ PV of coupons = 90 × 4.355261 = ₹391.97.
  3. PV of face = 1,000/1.16 = 1,000/1.771561 = ₹564.47.
  4. Intrinsic value = 391.97 + 564.47 = ₹956.45.
  5. Market price ₹930 is below face value ₹1,000 ⇒ the bond is selling at a discount (of ₹70). It is also below intrinsic value (₹956.45), so it is undervalued by ₹26.45 – attractive; its YTM at ₹930 is 10.64% > 10% required.
  6. Pull-to-par: with the yield constant at 10%, the value rises each year towards ₹1,000: 956.45 → 962.09 → 968.30 → 975.13 → 982.64 → 990.91 → 1,000 at maturity (see graph in section 2.5). A discount bond earns part of its return from this price accretion; a premium bond's price declines to par; at maturity every bond is worth its face value.

From your course materialQuestion 4 – Coupon bond YTM

Face ₹1,000, coupon 11%, maturity 7 years, price ₹1,050. (1) Approximate YTM. (2) If price rises to ₹1,120, overpriced or under-priced?

Full solution
  1. C = ₹110; (F − P)/n = (1,000 − 1,050)/7 = −7.1429.
  2. Approximate YTM = (110 − 7.1429)/[(1,000 + 1,050)/2] = 102.8571/1,025 = 10.03%. (Exact YTM by RATE(7,110,-1050,1000) = 9.97%.)
  3. At ₹1,050 investors are pricing the bond to earn about 10%. Value of the bond at that required yield ≈ ₹1,050.
  4. At ₹1,120 the approximate YTM becomes (110 − 120/7)/[(1,000 + 1,120)/2] = 92.8571/1,060 = 8.76% (exact 8.64%).
  5. Since ₹1,120 exceeds the value of about ₹1,050 at the required ~10% yield (equivalently, the yield at ₹1,120 is well below the required return), the bond is overpriced by about ₹70 ⇒ do not buy; a holder could sell.

From your course materialQuestion 5 – Government security auction

RBI auctions ₹15,000 crore. Bids: A 100.60 (₹3,000 cr), B 100.45 (₹4,000 cr), C 100.30 (₹2,500 cr), D 100.20 (₹4,500 cr), E 100.10 (₹3,000 cr). (1) Cut-off price. (2) Allotment. (3) Proceeds under uniform and discriminatory auctions. (4) Which is more transparent?

Full solution
BidderBid priceBid (₹ cr)CumulativeAllottedUniform paysDiscriminatory pays
A100.603,0003,0003,0003,003.03,018.0
B100.454,0007,0004,0004,004.04,018.0
C100.302,5009,5002,5002,502.52,507.5
D100.204,50014,0004,5004,504.54,509.0
E100.103,00017,0001,0001,001.01,001.0
Total17,00015,00015,015.015,053.5
  1. Bids are already ranked from highest to lowest price. Cumulative bids reach ₹14,000 crore after D; the ₹15,000 crore mark falls within E's bid ⇒ cut-off price = ₹100.10.
  2. Allotment: A ₹3,000 cr, B ₹4,000 cr, C ₹2,500 cr, D ₹4,500 cr in full; E gets the remaining 15,000 − 14,000 = ₹1,000 crore of its ₹3,000 crore bid (pro-rata 33.33%); ₹2,000 crore of E's bid is rejected. (Oversubscription: 17,000/15,000 = 1.13 times.)
  3. Uniform price: 15,000 × 100.10/100 = ₹15,015 crore.
  4. Discriminatory: 3,000 × 1.0060 + 4,000 × 1.0045 + 2,500 × 1.0030 + 4,500 × 1.0020 + 1,000 × 1.0010 = 3,018 + 4,018 + 2,507.5 + 4,509 + 1,001 = ₹15,053.5 crore – ₹38.5 crore more.
  5. Transparency: the uniform price auction is more transparent: one publicly announced price applies to all winners; the outcome does not depend on each bidder's private strategy; small and less-informed bidders are not penalised for bidding high (no winner's curse); it encourages bids close to true valuation, improving price discovery. The discriminatory auction raises more for these given bids, but bidders learn to shade bids down, and different winners pay different prices, making it less transparent.

From your course materialQuestion 6 – Bond portfolio decision

₹15,00,000 to invest; required return 9%. A: 8%, 4 years, price ₹965. B: 10%, 6 years, price ₹1,045. C: zero, 5 years, price ₹720. (1) Value of A. (2) Approximate YTM of B. (3) PV of C. (4) Recommend.

Full solution
  1. Bond A: PVIFA(9%,4) = 3.239720; coupons 80 × 3.239720 = ₹259.18; face 1,000/1.094 = ₹708.43; value = ₹967.60. Price ₹965 < ₹967.60 ⇒ undervalued by ₹2.60 (exact YTM 9.08% > 9%).
  2. Bond B: approx YTM = [100 + (1,000 − 1,045)/6]/[(1,000 + 1,045)/2] = (100 − 7.5)/1,022.5 = 9.05% (exact 9.00%). Value at 9% = ₹1,044.86 ≈ price ⇒ fairly priced.
  3. Bond C: PV = 1,000/1.095 = 1,000/1.538624 = ₹649.93. Price ₹720 > ₹649.93 ⇒ overvalued by ₹70.07 (its YTM is only 6.79%).
  4. Comparison:
    BondValue at 9%PriceYTM (exact)Macaulay durationVerdict
    A967.609659.08%3.57 yrsUndervalued
    B1,044.861,0459.00%4.82 yrsFair
    C649.937206.79%5.00 yrsOvervalued
  5. Recommendation: invest in Bond A – the only undervalued bond, the highest YTM (9.08%) and the lowest duration (least interest-rate risk). ₹15,00,000/965 = 1,554 bonds costing ₹14,99,610 (₹390 left over). Avoid C (overpriced; returns only 6.79%). B is acceptable as a fairly priced diversifier if the investor wants higher coupon income, but it offers no margin of safety.

From your course materialQuestion 7 – Duration and investment decision

A bank has ₹40 crore; required return 8%. A: 7%, 5 years, price ₹950. B: 9%, 5 years, price ₹1,025. (1) YTM of A. (2) Macaulay duration of A. (3) How duration measures sensitivity. (4) Recommend for a medium-term investor.

Full solution
  1. YTM of A: approx = [70 + (1,000 − 950)/5]/[(1,000 + 950)/2] = 80/975 = 8.21%; exact (RATE(5,70,-950,1000)) = 8.26%.
  2. Macaulay duration of A at its YTM of 8.26%:
    tCFDFPVt × PV
    1700.92369464.6664.66
    2700.85321159.72119.45
    3700.78810755.17165.50
    4700.72797050.96203.83
    51,0700.672422719.493,597.46
    Total950.004,150.90
    DM = 4,150.90/950.00 = 4.37 years; DMod = 4.3694/1.0826 = 4.04. (Using the 8% required return instead gives 4,198.48/960.07 = 4.37 years – the same to two decimals.)
  3. Duration and sensitivity: duration is the PV-weighted average time to receive cash flows; modified duration converts it into price sensitivity: ΔP/P ≈ −DMod × Δy. Bond A's price would fall about 4.04% if yields rose by 1% (about ₹38 on ₹950) and rise about 4.04% if yields fell by 1%. The higher the duration, the greater the interest-rate risk.
  4. Bond B: YTM approx (90 − 5)/1,012.5 = 8.40%; exact 8.37%; DM = 4.25 years; DMod = 3.92. Values at 8%: A ₹960.07 (price ₹950 ⇒ undervalued by ₹10.07); B ₹1,039.93 (price ₹1,025 ⇒ undervalued by ₹14.93).
  5. Recommendation: Bond B – higher YTM (8.37% vs 8.26%), lower duration (4.25 vs 4.37 years, so less price risk), higher coupon income and a larger margin below intrinsic value. For a medium-term (about 4–5 year) investor its duration is close to the horizon, which also limits reinvestment and price risk. ₹40 crore buys 3,90,243 bonds of B (cost ₹39,99,99,075). Bond A would suit only an investor who specifically wants slightly more price gain if rates fall.

From your course materialQuestion 8 – Interest-rate sensitivity

5-year bond, face ₹1,000, coupon 8%, required return 8%. Value at 8%, 10% and 6%; explain the inverse relationship.

Full solution
  1. At 8%: coupon = yield ⇒ ₹1,000 (80 × 3.992710 + 1,000 × 0.680583 = 319.42 + 680.58).
  2. At 10%: 80 × 3.790787 + 1,000 × 0.620921 = 303.26 + 620.92 = ₹924.18 (−7.58%).
  3. At 6%: 80 × 4.212364 + 1,000 × 0.747258 = 336.99 + 747.26 = ₹1,084.25 (+8.42%).
  4. Inverse relationship: the bond's cash flows are fixed; a higher required return discounts them more heavily, lowering their present value; a lower required return does the opposite. Economically, when new bonds offer 10%, nobody pays ₹1,000 for an 8% bond – its price must fall until its yield reaches 10%. Note the asymmetry: +2% costs ₹75.82 while −2% gains ₹84.25 (convexity, Malkiel theorem 5).

B. Malkiel's Theorems, Duration & Convexity handout – all examples and problems

From your course materialWarm-up: 8% coupon bond, market rates rise to 9% – what happens?

The price falls. The ₹80 coupon is fixed; new bonds pay 9%, so the old bond's price must drop until a buyer earns 9% overall.

From your course materialWorked Example 1: 3-year 8% bond at 8%, 10%, 6%

At 8%: 74.07 + 68.59 + 857.34 = ₹1,000.00 (par). At 10%: 72.73 + 66.12 + 811.42 = ₹950.26 (< ₹1,000). At 6%: 75.47 + 71.20 + 906.79 = ₹1,053.46 (> ₹1,000). Teaching point: nothing about the bond changed – only the discount rate.

From your course materialTheorems 2–5 numerical illustrations

T2: 3-year vs 15-year 8% bonds, 8% → 9%: −2.53% vs −8.06% (Bond B, the 15-year, falls more). Quick check: the 20-year bond is more exposed than the 2-year. T3: 10-year 4% vs 10% coupons, 8% → 9%: −7.17% vs −6.17% (Bond A, low coupon). T4: 10-year 8% bond, 5% → 6% gives −6.86%; 10% → 11% gives −6.13% (Case A larger). T5: 20-year 8% bond at 8%: −9.13% for +1%, +10.59% for −1% (gain larger).

From your course materialConsolidation activity (4 questions)
  1. 3-year 8% vs 15-year 8%: Bond B (15-year) – theorem 2.
  2. 10-year 4% vs 10-year 10%: Bond A (4%) – theorem 3.
  3. Yield increases ⇒ price decreases.
  4. Yield falls 1% – does price rise by exactly the percentage it falls when yield rises 1%? No, the rise is larger (convexity).
From your course materialMacaulay duration example (3-year 10% at 10%)

PVs 90.91, 82.64, 826.45 (sum ₹1,000); t × PV 90.91, 165.28, 2,479.35 (sum 2,735.54); DM = 2,735.54/1,000 ≈ 2.74 years < 3-year maturity. A zero-coupon bond's duration equals its maturity.

From your course materialModified duration example (DM 6.2, y 8%)

DMod = 6.2/1.08 = 5.74: price changes about 5.74% for every 1 percentage-point change in yield, in the opposite direction.

From your course materialDuration theorem: DMod = 6 interpretation; Examples A and B

DMod 6: +1% yield ⇒ −6%; −1% ⇒ +6%. A: ₹1,000, DMod 6, +0.50% ⇒ −3% ⇒ ₹970. B: ₹2,000, DMod 8, −0.25% ⇒ +2% ⇒ ₹2,040.

From your course materialConvexity example (P 1,000, DMod 7, C 80, +1%)

Duration effect −7%; convexity +½ × 80 × 0.0001 = +0.4%; total −6.6% ⇒ ₹934 (duration only: −7% ⇒ ₹930). Convexity makes the estimated decline smaller.

From your course materialIntegrated application: Bond A (DMod 4, C 35) vs Bond B (DMod 9, C 100), yields +1%

A ≈ −4%; B ≈ −9% by duration. Bond B is more sensitive; its higher convexity partly cushions it (with convexity: A −3.83%, B −8.5%).

From your course materialProblem 1 – 4% vs 9% coupon, same maturity and yield

Bond A (4%) has the larger percentage price change – lower coupon, greater sensitivity (theorem 3).

From your course materialProblem 2 – 4-year vs 15-year, same coupon and yield

Bond B (15-year) has greater interest-rate risk (theorem 2).

From your course materialProblem 3 – DMod 5, yield +0.80%

ΔP/P ≈ −5 × 0.008 = −4%.

From your course materialProblem 4 – ₹5,00,000, DMod 7, rates +0.50%

ΔP/P ≈ −7 × 0.005 = −3.5%; ΔP = 5,00,000 × (−0.035) = −₹17,500; new value ≈ ₹4,82,500.

From your course materialProblem 5 – P 1,000, DMod 6, C 75, yield +2%

Duration −6 × 0.02 = −12%; convexity ½ × 75 × 0.0004 = +1.5%; total −10.5% ⇒ ₹895.

From your course materialFive-minute recap (fill in the blanks)
  1. If yield increases, bond price decreases.
  2. Longer maturity means greater price sensitivity.
  3. Lower coupon means greater price sensitivity.
  4. Lower initial yield means greater price sensitivity.
  5. The price-yield relationship is convex.
  6. Modified duration measures approximate percentage price sensitivity to yield changes.
  7. Convexity measures the curvature of the price-yield relationship.
From your course materialFive misconceptions corrected
  1. "1% yield rise ⇒ 1% price fall" – wrong; it is about DMod % (e.g. 7%).
  2. "Longer maturity always means higher duration" – generally, but coupon and yield matter too.
  3. "Macaulay = modified duration" – no: time (years) vs % sensitivity.
  4. "Duration gives the exact change" – no: first-order approximation; add convexity.
  5. "−1% gives exactly the opposite of +1%" – no: gains exceed losses (convexity).

Concept chain from the handout: Yield change → Bond price change → Malkiel's theorems (direction and relative size) → Duration (first-order size) → Convexity (curvature correction).

C. Yield Curve Student Handout – all examples, exercises and quiz

From your course materialSection 4: current yield of an 8% bond at ₹950

80/950 × 100 = 8.42%. The coupon stays 8% forever; the yield moves with price.

From your course materialSection 5: Option A (₹1 lakh for 1 year at 6%) vs Option B (10 years at 6%)

A earns ₹6,000 in one year and frees the money. B locks the money for 10 years at the same 6% – ₹1,00,000 would grow to ₹1,79,085 if coupons were reinvested at 6%, but the investor bears 10 years of interest-rate and inflation risk for no extra yield. A normal curve exists precisely because investors usually demand more than 6% for B.

From your course materialSection 7: forward rate from 1-year 6% and 2-year 7%

(1.07)2 = (1.06)(1 + f) ⇒ 1 + f = 1.1449/1.06 = 1.080094 ⇒ f ≈ 8.01%. Market-implied, not a promise.

From your course materialSection 8: ₹10 lakh – Strategy A (1-year at 6%) vs B (10-year at 7%)

A: ₹10,00,000 × 1.06 = ₹10,60,000 after one year, then reinvest at whatever rate prevails. B: locks 7% for 10 years; with reinvestment at 7% it grows to ₹19,67,151. For A to match B, the ₹10,60,000 must be reinvested at about 7.11% a year for the remaining 9 years. If the investor expects rates to rise above that, A may be better; if rates are expected to fall, B is better. The choice depends on rate expectations, horizon and risk tolerance.

From your course materialSection 9: Examples 1 and 2

Example 1 (expect rates to rise; 1Y 6%, 10Y 7%): prefer short maturities/T-Bills/short-duration funds; avoid long bonds whose prices would fall; reinvest later at higher rates. Example 2 (expect 10Y to fall from 7% to 5.5%): buy the 10-year now – earn a higher coupon than future issues and gain capital appreciation as yields fall.

From your course materialSection 10: coupon ₹80 at prices ₹1,000, ₹900, ₹1,100

8.00%, 8.89%, 7.27%. Price ↓ → yield ↑; price ↑ → yield ↓.

From your course materialSection 12: ₹5 lakh – A 2Y 6.0%, B 5Y 6.5%, C 10Y 7.0%

Highest yield: Bond C (7.0%). It is not automatically best: the investor must weigh horizon, rate expectations, inflation, liquidity, interest-rate risk (duration), and risk tolerance. A 10-year par bond at 7% loses about 6.7% if its yield rises 1%, versus about 1.8% for the 2-year (DMod 1.83). Highest yield ≠ best investment.

From your course materialSection 14: worked example (3M 6.5%, 1Y 6.7%, 2Y 6.9%, 5Y 7.2%, 10Y 7.6%) – Q1 to Q4
  1. Normal/upward-sloping.
  2. 10-year at 7.6%.
  3. Market demands higher returns for longer maturities; may be pricing higher future rates, inflation or term risk.
  4. No – if rates rise sharply the 10-year's price falls, eroding its yield advantage.
From your course materialSection 15 Try It Yourself – Curve A (1Y 5%, 5Y 6%, 10Y 7%)

(a) Normal. (b) Expected growth, rising rates or inflation, or a term premium. (c) Short-term bonds: investors who expect rates to rise, need liquidity soon or are risk-averse (e.g. corporate treasuries, liquid funds). (d) Long-term bonds: long-horizon investors (insurers, pension funds) locking in 7%, or those expecting rates to fall. (e) Risks: long bonds suffer price falls if rates rise and inflation erodes real value; short bonds face reinvestment risk if rates fall.

From your course materialTry It Yourself – Curve B (1Y 7%, 5Y 6%, 10Y 5%)

(a) Inverted. (b) Expected rate cuts, slowdown, falling inflation; currently tight policy. (c) Short-term: investors wanting today's high short yield with little price risk. (d) Long-term: investors expecting rates to fall further (capital gains) or needing to lock in yields for long liabilities before they fall. (e) Risks: short-term holders face reinvestment risk when short rates fall; long bonds earn less income and lose if the expected fall does not happen; rising credit risk if a slowdown comes.

From your course materialTry It Yourself – Curve C (1Y 6%, 5Y 6.1%, 10Y 6.2%)

(a) Flat. (b) Uncertainty or transition. (c) Short-term: most investors – almost no yield is sacrificed and flexibility is kept. (d) Long-term: only those expecting falling rates or matching long liabilities. (e) Risks: the extra 0.2% does not compensate for 9 more years of duration; the curve could steepen (hurting long bonds) or invert.

From your course materialSection 16: ladder of ₹10 lakh; "is 0.8% worth five more years?"

Ladder: ₹2 lakh each in 1-, 3-, 5-, 7- and 10-year bonds; reinvest each maturing rung at prevailing rates – spreads maturity and reinvestment risk (duration about 5.2 years if treated as zeros). For the 0.8% question, compare extra yield with extra duration: five more years typically adds 2.5–3 points of modified duration, so a rise in yields of only about 0.3% would wipe out a year's 0.8% extra income.

From your course materialSection 17: bank spread; corporate borrowing; government debt management

Bank: borrow at 5%, lend at 8% ⇒ spread about 3%; a flattening curve squeezes it. Corporate: 7% G-Sec + 2% spread = 9%. Government: uses the curve to decide maturity mix, refinancing, timing and interest cost.

From your course materialSelf-assessment quiz Q1–Q8
  1. X-axis: maturity/time to maturity.
  2. Y-axis: yield.
  3. Normal: long-term yields higher than short-term.
  4. Inverted: short-term yields higher than long-term.
  5. Market yields up ⇒ bond prices generally down.
  6. Long bonds are more sensitive because they have greater duration (interest-rate risk).
  7. No, an inverted curve does not guarantee a recession; it is one signal among many.
  8. No, the highest-yielding bond is not automatically best; consider risk, maturity, duration, liquidity and objectives.

D. Yield Curve Handout – data tables and illustrations read and interpreted

The handout also contains several data tables that are not phrased as questions, but an examiner can hand you any of them and ask "identify and interpret". Each is solved below in the way a full-marks answer would treat it: name the shape, quote the slope in basis points, interpret, and state the investor implication.

From your course materialSection 1 – "A first look": 3M 6.0%, 6M 6.2%, 1Y 6.4%, 2Y 6.7%, 5Y 7.0%, 10Y 7.3%
  1. Shape: normal (upward-sloping) – every longer maturity yields more than the one before it.
  2. Slope: 10Y − 3M = 7.3% − 6.0% = 1.3 percentage points = 130 basis points.
  3. Why yields rise with maturity (the handout's five reasons): greater uncertainty, inflation risk, interest-rate risk, credit risk (for non-government issuers) and the opportunity cost of locking funds up for longer.
  4. Answer to the core question "what yield is available right now for each length of time?" – read straight off the table: 6.0% for three months up to 7.3% for ten years.
From your course materialSection 5 – Bonds A to E: 1Y 6.0%, 2Y 6.3%, 5Y 6.8%, 10Y 7.2%, 20Y 7.5%
  1. Longer maturity carries higher yield ⇒ normal / upward-sloping curve.
  2. Slope 20Y − 1Y = 7.5% − 6.0% = 150 bp. The increments shrink as maturity lengthens (+30 bp from 1Y to 2Y, +50 bp over the next three years, +40 bp over the next five, +30 bp over the last ten) – the typical "concave" normal curve: the extra reward per extra year falls.
  3. Reasons long bonds pay more (handout): interest-rate risk, inflation risk, uncertainty about growth and policy, and liquidity preference (investors prefer getting money back sooner, so they must be paid to lend long).
From your course materialSection 6 – the four shape tables (normal, flat, inverted, humped)
Table1Y5Y20Y20Y − 1YShapePossible reading
6.15.5%6.3%7.2%+170 bpNormalGrowth, higher future rates or inflation, term premium
6.26.0%6.1%6.2%+20 bpFlatUncertainty; transition between phases of the cycle
6.37.5%6.7%6.0%−150 bpInvertedRates expected to fall; possible slowdown; long-bond demand pushes long yields down
6.45.5%6.8%6.0%+50 bpHumpedMedium maturities highest (5Y is 130 bp above 1Y and 80 bp above 20Y): mixed signals, supply–demand pressure in the middle

Note the humped curve: a positive 20Y − 1Y spread alone would wrongly suggest "normal". Always look at the middle of the curve too.

From your course materialSection 7 – curve 1Y 5.0%, 2Y 5.5%, 3Y 6.0%, 5Y 6.5%, 10Y 7.0%: what does it imply?
  1. Shape: normal. The market prices progressively higher yields for longer maturities.
  2. Treating the 1-, 2- and 3-year yields as spot rates with annual compounding, the implied one-year forward rates are:
    f1,1 = 1.0552/1.05 − 1 = 1.113025/1.05 − 1 = 6.00%;
    f2,1 = 1.063/1.0552 − 1 = 1.191016/1.113025 − 1 = 7.01%.
  3. Interpretation: the curve is consistent with one-year rates rising from 5% today to about 6% next year and about 7% the year after. The handout's caution applies: this blends expectations with risk and liquidity premiums, so it is not a guaranteed forecast.
From your course materialSection 11 – duration table (1Y low, 5Y medium, 10Y high, 20Y very high): put numbers on it

For par bonds with a 7% annual coupon (yield 7%), modified duration equals the annuity factor PVIFA(7%, n):

BondMaturityMacaulay durationModified durationApprox. price change if yield +1%
A1 year1.000.93−0.93%
B5 years4.394.10−4.10%
C10 years7.527.02−7.02%
D20 years11.3410.59−10.59%

So "Low → Very high" means roughly 1% to 11% price sensitivity per 1% yield change. Duration grows with maturity but more slowly than maturity (the 20-year bond's duration is about 11, not 20) – a direct illustration of Malkiel's second theorem ("at a decreasing rate").

From your course materialSection 13 – curve shape and economic conditions (and the caveat)

Normal ⇒ consistent with expansion, rising inflation or rising future rates. Flat ⇒ uncertainty, transition, conflicting expectations. Inverted ⇒ expected lower rates, slower activity or lower inflation. Caveat: never read the curve alone – check GDP growth, inflation, unemployment, central-bank (RBI MPC) policy, credit spreads, money supply and commodity prices.

From your course materialSection 18 – term structure vs yield curve

The term structure of interest rates is the relationship between yield and time to maturity; the yield curve is its graph. Memory line from the handout: "Yield Curve = Maturity on X + Yield on Y + Market Expectations in the Shape."

Past papers & CIA questions — solved

Your Mid-Semester Examination, August 2026 (BBA303F-5, 50 marks, 2 hours) solved question by question. Every answer below is aligned with the official scheme of valuation: the points, the marks split and the method marks follow the scheme, and every number was recomputed by computer. The paper follows exactly the ESE pattern in your course plan (Section A 3 × 5, Section B 2 × 10, Section C one compulsory 15-mark case), so treat it as the best available model of the end-semester paper.

  • Substance, not wording. Marks go to correct, relevant points; you do not need textbook language.
  • Recommendation questions are open. "Recommend / should it / which would you" questions accept a different answer if it is logically justified. Always state one clear recommendation first, then justify it point by point against each criterion named in the question (e.g. participation, cost, transparency).
  • Method marks. In numericals the formula, the substitution and each intermediate value (e.g. (1.09)4, PVIFA, PVIF) carry separate marks, and a small arithmetic slip carried forward consistently is not penalised twice. Always write: formula → substitution → intermediate factors → answer → one-line interpretation.
  • Rounding ranges are accepted (e.g. ₹705–₹712, 8.8%–9.2%, 4.2–4.4 years), and the approximate YTM formula is accepted where the scheme uses it; trial-and-error / IRR is also given full marks.
  • Interpretation earns its own marks. Over/undervalued, premium/discount, "YTM is earned only if held to that bond's own maturity", and the Modified Duration → % price change link each carried separate marks.
  • Named criteria = mark heads. If the question says "compare objectives, participants and pricing mechanisms", each of the three gets 1 mark; the recommendation gets 2. Use one heading per criterion.

Mid-sem August 2026 — Section A (5 marks each)

From your course materialQ1(a) – Three bond-market participants to collaborate with

An investment firm wants to increase its participation in the Indian bond market. Recommend three categories of participants it should collaborate with and justify the role of each in achieving efficient investment and liquidity. (5 marks)

Model answer (aligned with the official scheme: 3 marks for three categories + 2 marks for justification)

Introduction. The Indian bond market works through issuers, intermediaries and investors, with RBI and SEBI as regulators. A firm entering it gains most by partnering with those who supply issues, liquidity and depth.

  1. RBI / Government of India (issuer and regulator). RBI, as the Government's debt manager, conducts G-Sec auctions on a published calendar, regulates the G-Sec and money markets and manages liquidity through repo/reverse repo and open market operations. Working within RBI's framework (auctions, NDS-OM, repo) gives the firm access to the deepest, safest segment and to liquidity when it needs cash.
  2. Primary Dealers (PDs). PDs underwrite and bid in G-Sec auctions and act as market makers, giving two-way (buy and sell) quotes. Dealing through them lets the firm buy and sell with narrow spreads – direct secondary-market liquidity.
  3. Institutional investors – banks, insurance companies, provident and pension funds, mutual funds. They are large, stable, long-term holders (banks for SLR, insurers and pension funds under investment norms), which gives the market depth and steady demand, so the firm can place or exit positions without moving prices much.

Also acceptable: Foreign Portfolio Investors (add capital, liquidity and price discovery; widen the investor base) and corporates (issue and invest in corporate bonds; higher-yield opportunities).

Conclusion. Partnering with the issuer/regulator (supply and liquidity backstop), PDs (market making) and institutional investors (depth) together delivers efficient investment and liquidity.

From your course materialQ1(b) – ₹500 crore expansion: primary or secondary market?

A manufacturing company plans to raise ₹500 crore for expansion. Should it issue bonds through the primary market or purchase existing bonds from the secondary market? Justify by comparing objectives, participants and pricing mechanisms. (5 marks)

Model answer (aligned with the official scheme: 1 + 1 + 1 for the three comparisons, 2 for the recommendation)
BasisPrimary marketSecondary market
Objective (1)Mobilises fresh capital; the issuer receives the issue proceeds.Gives liquidity/exit and price discovery for existing bonds; no new money reaches the issuer.
Participants (1)Issuer (the company), merchant bankers/arrangers, underwriters, anchor/institutional investors (e.g. via the EBP platform for private placements).Existing bondholders, brokers, stock exchanges (NSE/BSE debt segments), OTC dealers, retail and institutional investors.
Pricing mechanism (1)Price/coupon fixed at issue through book-building (bidding) or a fixed-price offer.Price set continuously by demand and supply, moving with interest rates and credit quality.

Recommendation (2). The company should issue new bonds in the primary market. Only a primary issue puts ₹500 crore of fresh money into the company for its expansion. Buying existing bonds in the secondary market would use the company's cash (it becomes an investor) and the proceeds would go to the selling bondholders, not to the company. A ₹500 crore issue can be placed with institutional investors through a private placement on the exchange's electronic bidding platform or through a public issue, with the coupon discovered by bidding.

From your course materialQ2(a) – Auction method in a period of high uncertainty

RBI plans to issue G-Secs during high market uncertainty. Recommend Uniform Price or Discriminatory Price auction, considering bidder participation, government borrowing cost and transparency. (5 marks)

Model answer (aligned with the official scheme: 2 for the recommendation + 1 each for the three criteria)

Recommendation: Uniform Price Auction. (Under uniform pricing, every successful bidder pays the single cut-off (stop-out) price; under discriminatory pricing, each pays his own bid.)

  1. Bidder participation. One cut-off price for all removes the fear of overpaying (the winner's curse). When uncertainty is high, this fear is strongest, so uniform pricing draws wider and more aggressive participation, including from smaller bidders.
  2. Government borrowing cost. Because bidders do not need to shade their bids down, bids are closer to true valuations and competition is broader; this tends to lower the effective cost of borrowing over time, especially in volatile markets.
  3. Transparency. All winners pay the same price, so the outcome is simpler, fairer and easier to understand than a discriminatory auction in which different winners pay different prices.

Also acceptable (with reasons): discriminatory pricing, arguing that in stable conditions it extracts more revenue from aggressive bidders – provided you explain the trade-off (bid shading, winner's curse, lower transparency). See 1.5.

From your course materialQ2(b) – Bank short of funds: repo or reverse repo?

A commercial bank faces a temporary shortage of funds due to heavy withdrawals. Recommend repo or reverse repo and explain how it maintains liquidity. (5 marks)

Model answer (aligned with the official scheme: 2 + 1.5 + 1.5)

Recommendation: Repo (2 marks).

Mechanism (1.5). In a repo the bank sells government securities to a counterparty (RBI under its liquidity window, or another bank/market participant) with an agreement to repurchase them on a future date at a predetermined higher price. Economically this is collateralised borrowing: the securities are the collateral and the price difference is the interest (repo rate).

How it solves the problem (1.5). The bank receives cash immediately to meet withdrawals, without permanently selling its securities (so no loss from a forced sale and the holding returns after the repo). A reverse repo is the opposite – buying securities with an agreement to resell, i.e. lending surplus funds – so it would take cash away from a bank that is already short. It suits a bank with surplus funds, not this bank.

From your course materialQ3(a) – Zero-coupon bond: value and over/undervaluation

Zero-coupon bond: face value ₹1,000; maturity 4 years; required return 9%. (a) Present value (3 marks). (b) If the market price is ₹710, is the bond overvalued or undervalued? (2 marks)

Full solution (aligned with the official scheme: 0.5 + 0.5 + 1 + 1, then 2)
  1. Formula: PV = FV / (1 + r)n. (0.5)
  2. Substitution: PV = 1,000 / (1.09)4. (0.5)
  3. (1.09)4 = 1.4116. (1)
  4. PV = 1,000 / 1.4116 = ₹708.43. (1) (Scheme accepts ₹705–₹712.)
  5. Market price ₹710 > intrinsic value ₹708.43 ⇒ the bond is overvalued (by about ₹1.57) ⇒ do not buy at ₹710. (2)

From your course materialQ3(b) – Retiring investor: which risk matters most?

An investor nearing retirement wants stable income with minimum risk. Among credit risk, interest-rate risk and reinvestment risk, identify the most significant and recommend strategies to minimise each. (5 marks)

Model answer (aligned with the official scheme: 2 for the risk with justification + 1 per strategy)

Most significant risk: reinvestment risk (2). The investor's goal is a stable income. When bonds or coupons fall due and interest rates have fallen, the money has to be reinvested at lower rates, so the income stream shrinks – the very thing a retiree cannot afford. (The scheme equally accepts interest-rate risk if you stress capital preservation or a possible need to sell early, because rising rates cut the market value of existing bonds. Credit risk is lower priority if high-quality bonds are assumed, but a well-argued case is also credited. Pick one and argue it consistently.)

Strategies (1 each):

  • Credit risk: hold sovereign (G-Secs, SDLs, RBI Floating Rate Savings Bonds) or AAA-rated bonds; diversify across issuers.
  • Interest-rate risk: prefer shorter-duration bonds, hold bonds to maturity, or use floating-rate instruments.
  • Reinvestment risk: build a bond ladder with staggered maturities, or immunise by matching portfolio duration to the investment horizon (longer-maturity or zero-coupon bonds also lock in today's rate).

Mid-sem August 2026 — Section B (10 marks each)

From your course materialQ4(a) – Five-year coupon bond: value, premium/discount, pull-to-par

Face value ₹1,000; coupon 8% (annual); required return 9%; 5 years. (a) Intrinsic value (6). (b) Premium or discount if market price is ₹965? (2). (c) Explain the pull-to-par effect (2).

Full solution (aligned with the official scheme: 1 + 1.5 + 1 + 1 + 1 + 0.5; 2; 2)
  1. Formula: Intrinsic value = Coupon × PVIFA(9%, 5) + FV × PVIF(9%, 5). (1)
  2. PVIFA(9%, 5) = [1 − (1.09)−5] / 0.09 = 3.8897. (1.5)
  3. PVIF(9%, 5) = 1/(1.09)5 = 0.6499. (1)
  4. PV of coupons = 80 × 3.8897 = ₹311.17. (1)
  5. PV of face value = 1,000 × 0.6499 = ₹649.93. (1)
  6. Intrinsic value = 311.17 + 649.93 = ₹961.10. (0.5) (Scheme accepts ₹958–₹965.)
  7. (b) The coupon rate (8%) is below the required return (9%), so fair value is below par. Market price ₹965 < face value ₹1,000 ⇒ the bond sells at a discount (of ₹35). (2) Extra insight: ₹965 is slightly above the intrinsic ₹961.10, so the price implies a YTM of 8.90%, a little below the required 9%.
  8. (c) Pull-to-par: as a bond approaches maturity, its price converges to (is "pulled" to) its face value, whether it trades at a premium or a discount, because the premium or discount amortises to zero by the maturity date when the bond is redeemed at par. This bond (₹961.10 today) will rise towards ₹1,000 year by year if the yield stays at 9%. (2) See 2.5.

From your course materialQ4(b) – ₹5,00,000 to invest: which bond for a 3-year and a 5-year horizon?

Bond A: 8% coupon, FV ₹1,000, price ₹980, 3 years, AAA. Bond B: 10% coupon, FV ₹1,000, price ₹1,050, 5 years, AA. Bond C: zero-coupon, FV ₹1,000, price ₹760, 7 years, Government. (a) Horizon 3 years – recommend (5). (b) Horizon 5 years – recommend (5).

Full solution (aligned with the official scheme)

Preliminary step – YTM of each bond (approximate formula for coupon bonds, exact formula for the zero): YTM ≈ [C + (F − P)/n] / [(F + P)/2].

BondWorkingYTMEarned over
A[80 + (1,000 − 980)/3] / [(1,000 + 980)/2] = (80 + 6.67)/990 = 86.67/990≈ 8.75%3 years
B[100 + (1,000 − 1,050)/5] / [(1,000 + 1,050)/2] = (100 − 10)/1,025 = 90/1,025≈ 8.78%5 years
C(1,000/760)1/7 − 1 (exact)≈ 4.00%7 years

Key interpretive point (scheme: 1 mark). A YTM is earned in full only if the bond is held to its own maturity. Selling before maturity exposes the investor to price risk that the YTM does not capture; holding a bond that matures early exposes him to reinvestment risk. So maturity-matching, not the highest YTM alone, drives the choice.
