(a) Horizon 3 years ⇒ Bond A.

  1. YTM of A ≈ 8.75%, earned exactly over 3 years because its maturity equals the horizon: no price risk at exit and no reinvestment of principal. (1.5)
  2. B's 8.78% needs a 5-year hold; selling it at year 3 means accepting whatever the market price is then. C's 4.00% needs 7 years and is much lower anyway. Their YTMs are therefore not comparable with A's for a 3-year investor. (1)
  3. Recommendation: Bond A. (1)
  4. Justification: maturity match eliminates price risk, plus AAA credit quality – ideal for a fixed 3-year goal. (1.5)

(b) Horizon 5 years ⇒ Bond B.

  1. YTM of B ≈ 8.78%, locked in over exactly 5 years; highest return once maturities are matched. (1.5)
  2. A matures after 3 years, so its proceeds must be reinvested for 2 more years at unknown future rates (reinvestment risk). C yields only about 4.00% even to its own maturity and would have to be sold 2 years early (price risk). Both are inferior for a 5-year horizon. (1)
  3. Recommendation: Bond B. (1)
  4. Justification: maturity match, highest locked-in return, a regular 10% coupon income; AA rating is a small trade-off but still investment grade. (1.5)

Exact-YTM note. Solving exactly (IRR) gives A 8.79% and B 8.72%, so the ordering of A and B flips by a few basis points. The recommendations do not change, because they rest on maturity matching – which is precisely the scheme's point. With ₹5,00,000 the investor buys about 510 units of A (₹980 each) or about 476 units of B (₹1,050 each).

From your course materialQ5(a) – RBI's auction process for ₹25,000 crore; role of competitive and non-competitive bidders

The Government of India plans to borrow ₹25,000 crore for infrastructure. Describe the sequence of activities RBI performs from announcing the auction until settlement, and explain how competitive and non-competitive bidders influence the outcome. (10 marks)

Model answer (aligned with the official scheme: 6 for the sequence + 2 + 2)

Introduction. RBI manages the Government's market borrowing and raises the ₹25,000 crore by auctioning dated G-Secs.

Sequence of activities (about 1–1.5 marks each):

  1. Notification / announcement. Within the half-yearly issuance calendar, RBI (on behalf of the Government) issues a press release and notification naming the security (new issue or re-issue), notified amount (₹25,000 crore), auction date, auction type (price- or yield-based) and pricing method (uniform or multiple price).
  2. Bidding. On auction day bids are submitted electronically on RBI's E-Kuber system. Competitive bidders (banks, primary dealers, insurance companies, mutual funds and other institutions) quote a price or yield and an amount. Non-competitive bidders (retail and small investors, including through RBI Retail Direct or via aggregators) quote only the amount they want.
  3. Bid processing. RBI arranges competitive bids in ascending order of yield (descending order of price), accumulates amounts and fixes the cut-off yield/price at which the notified amount is fully raised. RBI may also reject bids it considers too costly.
  4. Allotment. The non-competitive portion is set aside first (up to 5% of the notified amount, i.e. up to ₹1,250 crore here) and allotted at the weighted-average rate; the rest goes to competitive bids at or better than the cut-off, with pro-rata allotment at the cut-off itself. Winners pay the cut-off price (uniform) or their own bid price (discriminatory), as notified.
  5. Result announcement and settlement. RBI announces the cut-off and amounts the same day. Successful bidders pay in funds, and securities are credited to their SGL/CSGL (or demat) accounts, typically on T+1. The security is then tradable on NDS-OM.

Influence of competitive bidders (2): they set the market-clearing yield/price. Their bids form the demand curve; aggressive bids lower the cut-off yield (cheaper borrowing), weak demand pushes it up. They provide institutional price discovery.

Influence of non-competitive bidders (2): they widen participation (retail access) and add assured demand, but do not affect the cut-off because they quote no price; they receive guaranteed allotment within the reserved share at the weighted-average rate.

Conclusion. The process gives the Government its ₹25,000 crore at a market-determined cost while keeping the market transparent and open to small investors. See 1.4 and 1.5.

From your course materialQ5(b) – Effect of interest-rate changes on bond prices (premium, discount, par)

Explain how changes in market interest rates affect bond prices, with numerical examples for premium, discount and par bonds. (10 marks)

Model answer with numbers (aligned with the official scheme: 3 + 2.5 + 2.5 + 2)

Inverse relationship (3). A bond's cash flows (coupons and redemption value) are fixed when it is issued. Its price is the present value of those cash flows at the current market (required) rate: P = C × PVIFA(r, n) + F × PVIF(r, n). When market rates rise, each cash flow is discounted more heavily, so the price falls; when rates fall, the price rises. Economically, if new bonds pay more, nobody pays full price for an old bond that pays less. All illustrations below use FV ₹1,000, 5 years, annual coupons.

Factors used: PVIFA(8%, 5) = 3.9927, PVIF(8%, 5) = 0.6806; PVIFA(10%, 5) = 3.7908, PVIF(10%, 5) = 0.6209.

  1. Premium bond (rates fall) (2.5). 10% coupon bond when the required return falls to 8%: P = 100 × 3.9927 + 1,000 × 0.6806 = 399.27 + 680.58 = ₹1,079.85 > ₹1,000. Coupon rate > market rate ⇒ premium.
  2. Discount bond (rates rise) (2.5). 8% coupon bond when the required return rises to 10%: P = 80 × 3.7908 + 1,000 × 0.6209 = 303.26 + 620.92 = ₹924.18 < ₹1,000. Coupon rate < market rate ⇒ discount.
  3. Par bond (2). 9% coupon bond valued at a 9% required return: every ₹90 coupon and the ₹1,000 principal are discounted at exactly the coupon rate, so P = ₹1,000. If rates then fall to 8%, the same bond is worth 90 × 3.9927 + 680.58 = ₹1,039.93 (premium); if they rise to 10%, 90 × 3.7908 + 620.92 = ₹962.09 (discount).

Conclusion. Rate ↓ ⇒ price ↑ (premium when coupon > yield); rate ↑ ⇒ price ↓ (discount when coupon < yield); coupon = yield ⇒ par. Note also the asymmetry: for the 9% bond a 1-point fall adds ₹39.93 but a 1-point rise removes only ₹37.91 – convexity (Malkiel's theorem, see 2.6).

Mid-sem August 2026 — Section C (compulsory case, 15 marks)

From your course materialQ6 – ABC Financial Services: ₹30 crore in Bond A or Bond B

The treasury wants to invest ₹30 crore to maximise return while managing interest-rate risk. Bond A: FV ₹1,000, coupon 8%, 5 years, price ₹960. Bond B: FV ₹1,000, coupon 10%, 5 years, price ₹1,040. Annual coupons. (a) YTM of Bond A (5). (b) Duration of Bond A (5). (c) How duration measures sensitivity to rate changes (3). (d) Recommend A or B (2).

Full solution (aligned with the official scheme: 1 + 1 + 1.5 + 0.5 + 1; 1 + 1.5 + 1.5 + 1; 1 + 1 + 1; 2)

(a) YTM of Bond A

  1. YTM ≈ [C + (F − P)/n] / [(F + P)/2]. (1)
  2. C = 80, F = 1,000, P = 960, n = 5. (1)
  3. Numerator = 80 + (1,000 − 960)/5 = 80 + 8 = 88. (1.5)
  4. Denominator = (1,000 + 960)/2 = 980. (0.5)
  5. YTM ≈ 88/980 = 8.98% ≈ 9%. (1) (Exact IRR = 9.03%; the scheme accepts 8.8%–9.2% and gives full marks for trial-and-error.)

(b) Macaulay duration of Bond A at 9%

Year tCash flow (₹)PV factor @9%PV (₹)t × PV
1800.917473.3973.39
2800.841767.33134.67
3800.772261.77185.32
4800.708456.67226.70
51,0800.6499701.933,509.63
Total961.104,129.71
  1. Cash flows: ₹80 in years 1–4 and ₹1,080 (coupon + redemption) in year 5. (1)
  2. Discount each at 9% (PV column). (1.5)
  3. Multiply each PV by t and add: Σt × PV = 4,129.71. (1.5)
  4. Duration = 4,129.71 / 961.10 = 4.30 years. (1) (The scheme's own table prints ΣPV ≈ 961.86 and Σt×PV ≈ 4,133 because of rounded factors; the duration is 4.30 either way and 4.2–4.4 is accepted.)

(c) Using duration to assess sensitivity

  1. Concept (1): duration is the weighted-average time to receive the bond's cash flows and a measure of its price sensitivity; the higher the duration, the more the price moves when rates change.
  2. Modified duration (1) = D / (1 + YTM) = 4.30 / 1.09 = 3.94.
  3. Application (1): %ΔP ≈ −DMod × Δy. A 1-percentage-point rise in rates lowers Bond A's price by about 3.94% (about ₹37.9 on ₹961.10). Full repricing at 10% gives ₹924.18, a 3.84% fall; the small gap is convexity.

(d) Recommendation (2) – either bond is accepted with sound reasoning.

  • Bond A (preferred here): its YTM is about 9%, level with Bond B (approximate YTM of B = [100 + (1,000 − 1,040)/5]/1,020 = 92/1,020 ≈ 9.02%; exact IRRs are A 9.03% and B 8.97%, so A is marginally ahead). It is bought at a discount (₹960) and gains about ₹40 as it is pulled to par, instead of losing a premium. Its duration (4.30 years) is only slightly above B's, so the extra rate risk is small relative to the return advantage.
  • Bond B (also acceptable): higher 10% coupon gives more current cash income and slightly lower duration (about 4.19 years; modified about 3.84), so a little less price risk; but as a premium bond (₹1,040) its price will fall towards ₹1,000 by maturity.

₹30 crore buys 3,12,500 units of A at ₹960, or about 2,88,462 units of B at ₹1,040.

CIA components (context only)

CIA 1 (individual assignment, 20 marks, Units 1–2) asked for a comparative study of one Government bond and one corporate bond using 5–10 years of RBI/SEBI/NSE/BSE/CCIL data, applying at least two valuation techniques (TVM, YTM, duration), auction and repo interpretation, and a link to SDG 8. It is an assignment brief with no exam questions, but it confirms the emphasis: G-Sec vs corporate bond comparison, auctions, repo, YTM and duration. CIA 3 (group presentation, 20 marks, CO4–CO5) is marked on research quality, conceptual understanding of sustainability and digital bonds, and comparison of Indian and global practice with SDG linkage – so in Unit 5 answers always add an India-vs-global comparison and an SDG link.

Additional question bank — 30 MCQs

Attempt all 30 before opening any answer. Each answer explains why the correct option is right and why every other option is wrong – that second part is where the learning is. Numbers were computed exactly; rounding is to two decimals unless stated.

Unit 1 — Markets, instruments, auctions, repo (Q1–Q7)

1. In India, primary auctions of Central Government dated securities are conducted by:

  1. SEBI
  2. The Reserve Bank of India, as the Government's debt manager
  3. NSE through its bond platform
  4. CCIL
Answer and reasoning

(b). RBI manages the public debt of the Central Government (and of State Governments by agreement) and conducts the auctions on its core banking/e-Kuber system. (a) SEBI regulates corporate bond issuance and markets, not G-Sec auctions. (c) Exchanges offer non-competitive bidding access for retail investors but do not conduct the auction. (d) CCIL is the clearing corporation / central counterparty that settles trades; it does not issue or auction securities.

2. In a uniform-price (Dutch) auction, successful competitive bidders pay:

  1. Their own bid price
  2. The highest bid price received
  3. The single cut-off price
  4. The weighted-average price of all bids received
Answer and reasoning

(c). All winners pay the same (cut-off) price. (a) Paying your own bid is the discriminatory (multiple-price) auction. (b) No format charges everyone the highest bid. (d) The weighted-average price of accepted competitive bids is what non-competitive bidders pay under a multiple-price auction – and it is computed on accepted, not all, bids.

3. The "winner's curse" is most associated with:

  1. Uniform-price auctions
  2. Discriminatory (multiple-price) auctions
  3. Non-competitive bids
  4. Tap issues
Answer and reasoning

(b). Winners pay their own bids, so the most aggressive (highest) bidder overpays relative to others; bidders therefore shade their bids down. (a) In a uniform auction everyone pays the cut-off, so bidding high is not punished. (c) Non-competitive bidders do not quote a price, so they cannot overbid. (d) A tap issue sells at a fixed announced price, not through competitive bidding.

4. NDS-OM is best described as:

  1. A stock exchange for corporate bonds
  2. RBI's anonymous screen-based order-matching platform for secondary-market G-Sec investing
  3. A credit rating agency
  4. The depository for corporate bonds
Answer and reasoning

(b). NDS-OM (Negotiated Dealing System – Order Matching) is owned by RBI; trades settle through CCIL. (a) Corporate bonds trade OTC and report to exchange platforms (RFQ), not on NDS-OM. (c) CRISIL, ICRA, CARE, India Ratings are rating agencies. (d) Corporate bonds are held in demat form at NSDL/CDSL; G-Secs are held in SGL accounts with RBI.

5. A bank borrows through repo against G-Secs with a market value of ₹200 crore and a 5% haircut. The cash it receives is:

  1. ₹210 crore
  2. ₹200 crore
  3. ₹190 crore
  4. ₹195 crore
Answer and reasoning

(c). Cash = MV × (1 − haircut) = 200 × 0.95 = ₹190 crore. (a) Adds the haircut – the lender never lends more than the collateral. (b) Ignores the haircut, which protects the lender against a fall in collateral value. (d) Applies only half the haircut.

6. Which instrument is issued at a discount and redeemed at face value, with maturities of 91, 182 or 364 days?

  1. State Development Loan
  2. Treasury Bill
  3. Floating Rate Bond
  4. Commercial Paper issued by a bank
Answer and reasoning

(b). T-Bills are the Central Government's zero-coupon short-term instruments. (a) SDLs are dated securities of State Governments paying semi-annual coupons. (c) FRBs are dated securities whose coupon resets to a benchmark. (d) CP is issued by companies, primary dealers and all-India financial institutions – banks raise short-term funds through Certificates of Deposit instead.

7. The main purpose of the repo market is:

  1. Raising long-term capital for companies
  2. Short-term collateralised borrowing and lending, i.e. liquidity management
  3. Transferring credit risk between banks
  4. Issuing new government securities
Answer and reasoning

(b). Repo lets holders of securities raise overnight-to-short-term cash against collateral; RBI's repo rate is also the policy rate. (a) Long-term capital is raised by bonds/equity in the primary market. (c) Credit risk is transferred by credit derivatives; in repo the collateral reduces credit risk. (d) New securities are issued through primary auctions.

Unit 2 — Valuation, yields, Malkiel, duration, convexity, risk (Q8–Q18)

8. A 5-year zero-coupon bond with face value ₹1,000 and a required return of 10% is worth:

  1. ₹500.00
  2. ₹620.92
  3. ₹683.01
  4. ₹1,000.00
Answer and reasoning

(b). 1,000/1.15 = 1,000/1.61051 = ₹620.92. (a) Uses simple interest (1,000 − 5 × 10% × 1,000). (c) ₹683.01 is 1,000/1.14 – one period short. (d) Face value is the value only at maturity or at a 0% yield.

9. A 9% coupon bond (face ₹1,000) trades at ₹900. Its current yield is:

  1. 9.00%
  2. 10.00%
  3. 8.10%
  4. 11.11%
Answer and reasoning

(b). CY = 90/900 = 10%. (a) 9% is the coupon rate (coupon/face), not coupon/price. (c) 8.1% multiplies 9% by 0.9 instead of dividing. (d) 11.11% is 100/900 – the discount divided by price, not the coupon.

10. For a bond trading at a premium, the correct ordering is:

  1. Coupon rate < current yield < YTM
  2. Coupon rate > current yield > YTM
  3. Coupon rate = current yield = YTM
  4. YTM > coupon rate > current yield
Answer and reasoning

(b). Price above face lowers CY below the coupon rate, and the certain capital loss to maturity pushes YTM lower still. (a) is the discount-bond ordering. (c) holds only for a par bond. (d) mixes the two cases and is never correct.

11. Which statement about yield to call (YTC) is correct?

  1. YTC is relevant mainly when a callable bond trades well below its call price
  2. YTC assumes the bond is redeemed at the call price on the call date
  3. YTC is always higher than YTM
  4. YTC ignores coupons
Answer and reasoning

(b). YTC is the IRR assuming redemption at the call price on the first (or stated) call date. (a) It matters most when the bond trades above the call price (rates have fallen, so a call is likely). (c) For a premium callable bond YTC is usually lower than YTM (see Extra Numerical 4: 7.30% vs 7.82%). (d) YTC includes every coupon up to the call date.

12. Malkiel's third theorem states that, other things equal:

  1. Longer maturity means greater price sensitivity
  2. Lower coupon means greater price sensitivity
  3. Price gains exceed price losses for equal yield changes
  4. Lower starting yield means greater price sensitivity
Answer and reasoning

(b). Order in your handout: I-M-C-Y-C (Inverse, Maturity, Coupon, Yield level, Convexity). (a) is theorem 2. (c) is theorem 5. (d) is theorem 4.

13. A bond has a Macaulay duration of 5.4 years and a YTM of 8% compounded semi-annually. Its modified duration is:

  1. 5.00
  2. 5.19
  3. 5.40
  4. 5.83
Answer and reasoning

(b). DMod = DM/(1 + y/m) = 5.4/1.04 = 5.19. (a) Divides by 1.08 – forgets that y/m = 4% for semi-annual compounding. (c) Confuses Macaulay and modified duration. (d) Multiplies by 1.08 instead of dividing.

14. A bond has DMod = 5. If its yield rises by 40 basis points, the approximate price change is:

  1. −0.40%
  2. −2.00%
  3. +2.00%
  4. −20.00%
Answer and reasoning

(b). ΔP/P ≈ −5 × 0.004 = −2%. (a) is the "1-for-1" misconception from your handout. (c) has the wrong sign – yields up means price down. (d) uses 0.04 (4%) instead of 0.004 (40 bp).

15. The duration of a 7-year zero-coupon bond is:

  1. Less than 7 years
  2. Exactly 7 years
  3. More than 7 years
  4. Cannot be determined without the yield
Answer and reasoning

(b). All the value sits in one payment at year 7, so the PV-weighted average time is 7. (a) applies to coupon bonds, which return cash early. (c) Macaulay duration of an option-free bond can never exceed maturity. (d) The yield changes modified duration (7/(1+y)) but not a zero's Macaulay duration.

16. Positive convexity means that, for equal up and down moves in yield:

  1. Price gains are smaller than price losses
  2. Price gains are larger than price losses
  3. Gains and losses are equal
  4. Price does not change
Answer and reasoning

(b). The price-yield curve bows outward, so duration understates gains and overstates losses (Malkiel theorem 5). (a) describes negative convexity (e.g. callable bonds near the call price). (c) would be a straight-line relationship – exactly what duration alone assumes. (d) is false for any bond with fixed cash flows.

17. DMod = 6, convexity = 60, yield rises 1%. The estimated % price change is:

  1. −6.0%
  2. −5.7%
  3. −6.3%
  4. −3.0%
Answer and reasoning

(b). −6 × 0.01 + ½ × 60 × 0.0001 = −0.06 + 0.003 = −5.7%. (a) is duration only. (c) subtracts the convexity term – it is always added because (Δy)2 > 0. (d) wrongly applies the ½ to the duration term (−½ × 6 × 0.01) and drops convexity.

18. An investor who holds a bond to maturity and fears that interest rates will fall is chiefly worried about:

  1. Price risk
  2. Reinvestment risk
  3. Default risk
  4. Liquidity risk
Answer and reasoning

(b). Coupons will be reinvested at lower rates, cutting the realised yield below the YTM. (a) Price risk hurts when rates rise, and a hold-to-maturity investor does not realise price changes. (c) Default risk is about the issuer's ability to pay, not the rate level. (d) Liquidity risk matters only if the investor must sell before maturity.

Unit 3 — Yield curve (Q19–Q24)

19. On a yield curve, the X-axis shows:

  1. Yield
  2. Price
  3. Time to maturity
  4. Coupon rate
Answer and reasoning

(c). Handout memory anchor: X = maturity, Y = yield. (a) Yield is the Y-axis. (b) Price appears on a price-yield (convexity) chart, not a yield curve. (d) Coupon is fixed and is not plotted.

20. The 1-year spot rate is 5% and the 2-year spot rate is 6% (annual compounding). The 1-year forward rate one year from now is closest to:

  1. 5.50%
  2. 6.00%
  3. 7.01%
  4. 11.00%
Answer and reasoning

(c). (1.06)2/1.05 − 1 = 1.1236/1.05 − 1 = 7.01%. (a) is the average of the two spot rates, not the forward. (b) is the 2-year spot itself. (d) adds the two rates.

21. Which theory says long yields are the geometric average of current and expected future short rates, with no premium?

  1. Liquidity preference theory
  2. Pure expectations theory
  3. Market segmentation theory
  4. Preferred habitat theory
Answer and reasoning

(b). (a) adds a positive liquidity (term) premium that rises with maturity. (c) says each maturity segment's yield is set by its own supply and demand, with no link to expectations. (d) lets investors leave their preferred maturity if paid a premium – a blend of (a), (b) and (c).

22. An inverted yield curve most commonly signals:

  1. Expected rate cuts or an economic slowdown
  2. Expected strong growth and rising inflation
  3. A guaranteed recession
  4. That long bonds are riskier than short bonds
Answer and reasoning

(a). (b) is the reading of a normal (steep) curve. (c) Your handout is explicit: an inversion does not guarantee a recession. (d) Long bonds carry more duration risk whatever the shape; the shape does not change that.

23. Bootstrapping is used to:

  1. Estimate a bond's credit rating
  2. Derive zero-coupon (spot) rates step by step from coupon-bond prices or par yields
  3. Compute accrued interest
  4. Allocate bids in an auction
Answer and reasoning

(b). Each maturity's discount factor is solved using those already found for shorter maturities. (a) Ratings come from rating agencies' credit analysis. (c) Accrued interest uses day counts. (d) Auction allotment ranks bids against the notified amount.

24. "Riding the yield curve" earns extra return when:

  1. The curve is inverted and stays unchanged
  2. The curve is upward-sloping and remains broadly unchanged over the holding period
  3. Rates rise sharply
  4. The investor holds every bond to maturity
Answer and reasoning

(b). As time passes a bond "rolls down" to a shorter maturity with a lower yield, so its price rises more than pull-to-par alone (Extra Numerical 10: 8.01% vs 6%). (a) On an inverted curve rolling down means moving to a higher yield – a loss. (c) A sharp rise in rates can wipe out the roll-down gain. (d) Holding to maturity simply earns the YTM; riding requires selling before maturity.

Unit 4 — Portfolio management (Q25–Q27)

25. A barbell is built from a 2-year and a 10-year zero to match a 5-year duration target. The weight in the 2-year zero is:

  1. 37.5%
  2. 50.0%
  3. 62.5%
  4. 40.0%
Answer and reasoning

(c). wS = (DL − DT)/(DL − DS) = (10 − 5)/(10 − 2) = 0.625. Check: 0.625 × 2 + 0.375 × 10 = 5. (a) is the weight in the 10-year zero. (b) gives duration 6. (d) gives 0.4 × 2 + 0.6 × 10 = 6.8.

26. Classical immunisation requires that:

  1. Portfolio maturity equals the liability date
  2. The PV of assets equals the PV of liabilities and asset duration equals the liability horizon
  3. All bonds are held to maturity
  4. The portfolio holds only zero-coupon bonds
Answer and reasoning

(b). Matching PV and duration makes price risk and reinvestment risk offset for a small parallel shift (and asset convexity should be at least the liability's). (a) Maturity matching of a coupon bond leaves reinvestment risk. (c) Immunised portfolios must be rebalanced as time passes and yields move. (d) A single zero is one way to immunise but not a requirement.

27. Fund return 9%, risk-free 6%, standard deviation 4%, beta 0.75. The Sharpe ratio is:

  1. 0.75
  2. 4.00
  3. 2.25
  4. 1.33
Answer and reasoning

(a). (9 − 6)/4 = 0.75. (b) is the Treynor ratio (9 − 6)/0.75 = 4. (c) multiplies excess return by beta. (d) divides the standard deviation by the excess return (inverted ratio).

Unit 5 — Sustainability and innovation (Q28–Q30)

28. Which of the following is not one of the four core components of the ICMA Green Bond Principles?

  1. Use of proceeds
  2. Process for project evaluation and selection
  3. Calibration of sustainability performance targets
  4. Management of proceeds
Answer and reasoning

(c). Calibration of SPTs belongs to the Sustainability-Linked Bond Principles (five components: KPIs, SPTs, bond characteristics, reporting, verification). The GBP's four components are use of proceeds, project evaluation and selection, management of proceeds and reporting – so (a), (b) and (d) are all genuine GBP components.

29. The key difference between a green bond and a sustainability-linked bond (SLB) is that:

  1. Green bonds are always issued by governments
  2. A green bond ring-fences proceeds for eligible green projects, whereas an SLB's proceeds are for general purposes and its coupon depends on meeting issuer-level targets
  3. SLBs cannot be issued in India
  4. Green bonds carry a step-up coupon
Answer and reasoning

(b). Green = use-of-proceeds instrument; SLB = performance-linked instrument. (a) Companies, banks and development institutions issue green bonds too. (c) Indian companies have issued SLBs. (d) The step-up coupon is a feature of SLBs, not green bonds.

30. A tokenised bond is best described as:

  1. A bond whose ownership is recorded as digital tokens on a distributed ledger, allowing fractional holdings and automated processes
  2. A bond paid for in cryptocurrency only
  3. An unregulated bond with no legal claim
  4. A bond that pays no coupon
Answer and reasoning

(a). (b) Settlement may be in tokenised cash or ordinary money; cryptocurrency is not required. (c) A properly structured tokenised bond carries the same legal claim as a conventional bond and remains subject to securities law. (d) Coupon structure is unrelated to tokenisation; smart contracts can automate coupon payments.

Short-answer questions (18) — 2 to 3 marks each

A full-marks short answer = a crisp definition + one or two distinguishing points + a tiny example or formula. Aim for 60–90 words. Answer aloud, then open.

1. Define a bond and name its four basic elements.

A bond is a debt instrument through which an issuer borrows money from investors and promises to pay periodic interest (coupons) and to repay the principal on a fixed date. Elements: face value (amount repaid, e.g. ₹100 for G-Secs, ₹1,000 in textbook problems), coupon rate (annual interest as % of face), maturity (repayment date) and issuer (Government of India, a State, a PSU or a company). Price and yield are then determined by the market.

2. What is a G-Sec? Give its three main types.

A Government Security is a tradeable instrument issued by the Central or a State Government acknowledging its debt; it is treated as free of credit risk in rupee terms. Types: Treasury Bills (91/182/364-day zero-coupon), dated securities (fixed or floating coupon, e.g. the 10-year benchmark), and State Development Loans (SDLs) issued by States. Cash Management Bills are a short-term variant of T-Bills.

3. Distinguish competitive and non-competitive bids.

Competitive bidders (banks, primary dealers, mutual funds) quote both price/yield and amount and may get full, partial or no allotment. Non-competitive bidders (retail, small institutions, including via RBI Retail Direct) quote only an amount, are assured allotment within a reserved portion, and pay the weighted-average price/yield of accepted competitive bids. Non-competitive bidding widens retail participation.

4. What is the cut-off price in an auction?

The lowest accepted price (highest accepted yield) at which the cumulative competitive bids, ranked from best to worst, first reach the notified amount. Bids above the cut-off price are allotted in full; bids at the cut-off are allotted pro-rata; bids below are rejected. In Practice Q5 the cut-off was ₹100.10 and bidder E received ₹1,000 crore of ₹3,000 crore.

5. What is a repo and a reverse repo?

A repo is a sale of securities with an agreement to buy them back on a later date at a pre-agreed price; economically it is a collateralised loan to the seller. The same deal viewed from the cash lender's side is a reverse repo. RBI's policy repo rate is the rate at which it lends to banks against G-Secs; TREPS (tri-party repo) on CCIL is the main market repo segment.

6. Define YTM and state its two key assumptions.

YTM is the single discount rate that equates the present value of all promised cash flows to the bond's current price – the bond's internal rate of return. It assumes that (1) the bond is held to maturity and the issuer pays in full and on time, and (2) every coupon is reinvested at the YTM itself. If reinvestment rates differ, the realised yield differs from the YTM.

7. Coupon rate vs current yield.

Coupon rate = annual coupon ÷ face value; fixed in the contract. Current yield = annual coupon ÷ market price; changes whenever price changes. An 8% bond priced at ₹950 has coupon rate 8% and current yield 80/950 = 8.42% (handout Section 4).

8. What is the pull-to-par effect?

As a bond approaches maturity, its price converges to face value, whatever the yield. With a constant yield, a discount bond's price rises each year towards par and a premium bond's price falls towards par. At maturity every (non-defaulted) bond is worth exactly its face value. It explains why a discount bond earns part of its return as capital gain.

9. State Malkiel's five theorems in one line each.

(1) Price and yield move inversely. (2) Longer maturity ⇒ larger % price change (at a decreasing rate). (3) Lower coupon ⇒ larger % price change. (4) Lower starting yield ⇒ larger % price change. (5) For equal yield moves, the price gain from a fall exceeds the loss from a rise (convexity). Memory aid: I-M-C-Y-C.

10. Macaulay vs modified duration.

Macaulay duration is the PV-weighted average time (in years) at which the bond's cash flows are received: DM = Σt·PV(CFt)/P. Modified duration = DM/(1 + y/m) and measures the approximate % price change for a 1-percentage-point change in yield: ΔP/P ≈ −DModΔy. Example: DM 6.2 at 8% gives DMod 5.74.

11. Why is convexity desirable for a bond investor?

With positive convexity, the price rises more when yields fall and falls less when yields rise than duration predicts. So, between two bonds with equal duration and yield, the more convex bond performs better for any large yield move in either direction. Correction term: +½C(Δy)2, always added.

12. What is DV01 (PVBP)?

The rupee change in a bond's (or portfolio's) value for a one-basis-point change in yield: DV01 = DMod × Market value × 0.0001. A ₹50 crore holding with DMod 6 has DV01 = ₹3,00,000 – each 1 bp rise in yields costs about ₹3 lakh. Dealers and bank treasuries set risk limits in DV01.

13. Term structure of interest rates vs yield curve.

The term structure is the relationship between yields and time to maturity for bonds of the same credit quality; the yield curve is the graph of that relationship with maturity on the X-axis and yield on the Y-axis (handout Section 18).

14. What is a forward rate?

An interest rate for a future period implied by today's spot rates under no-arbitrage: (1 + s2)2 = (1 + s1)(1 + f1,1). With a 6% 1-year and 7% 2-year rate, f ≈ 8.01% (handout Section 7). It is what the market locks in today, not a forecast guarantee.

15. Laddering vs barbell strategy.

A ladder spreads money evenly across maturities (e.g. ₹2 lakh each in 1, 3, 5, 7 and 10-year bonds) and reinvests each maturing rung – steady liquidity and averaged reinvestment rates. A barbell concentrates money at the short and long ends, avoiding the middle – higher convexity and flexibility, but more exposure to changes in curve shape (it gains if the curve flattens).

16. What is stress testing of a bond portfolio?

Estimating the loss a portfolio would suffer under severe but plausible scenarios – e.g. a +200 bp parallel shift, a steepening, or corporate spreads widening by 150 bp – using ΔV ≈ MV[−DModΔy + ½C(Δy)2] or full repricing. It shows whether capital and limits are adequate. RBI requires banks to run stress tests, and the Financial Stability Report publishes system-level results.

17. Define a green bond.

A bond whose proceeds are used exclusively to finance or refinance new or existing eligible green projects (renewable energy, clean transport, energy efficiency, climate adaptation, etc.), aligned with the four core components of the ICMA Green Bond Principles: use of proceeds, project evaluation and selection, management of proceeds and reporting. India's Sovereign Green Bonds (first auctioned by RBI in 2023) are an example.

18. What is a smart contract in bond markets?

Self-executing code on a blockchain that automatically performs pre-agreed actions when conditions are met – e.g. paying coupons to current token holders on coupon dates, redeeming at maturity, enforcing transfer restrictions, or stepping up an SLB coupon if a verified KPI is missed. It reduces manual processing, reconciliation and settlement time, but depends on correct code and reliable data inputs (oracles).

Long-answer and case-study questions (9) with full model answers

Each question shows (i) the answer structure an examiner looks for and (ii) a full model answer. For 10 marks write about 1½–2 pages; for the 15-mark Section C case, 2–3 pages with a numerical core. Always: introduction → headed points → Indian example/number → conclusion.

L1. Explain the structure of the Indian bond market – participants, instruments, primary and secondary segments. (10 marks)

Structure: definition → two segments (G-Sec vs corporate) → participants → instruments → primary market → secondary market and infrastructure → regulators → conclusion.

Full model answer

Introduction. The bond (debt) market is the market in which governments, public-sector bodies and companies borrow by issuing fixed-income securities, and in which those securities are subsequently bought and sold. In India it is dominated by government securities, with a smaller but growing corporate bond segment.

1. Two broad segments. (a) Government securities market – T-Bills, Cash Management Bills, dated G-Secs, State Development Loans, Sovereign Green Bonds; regulated by RBI. (b) Corporate / non-government bond market – bonds of PSUs, banks, NBFCs and companies, commercial paper and certificates of deposit; issuance and listing regulated by SEBI (money-market instruments by RBI).

2. Participants. Issuers: Central and State Governments, PSUs, banks, NBFCs, companies. Investors: commercial banks (the largest holders of G-Secs, partly because of the Statutory Liquidity Ratio), insurance companies and pension/provident funds (long-duration buyers), mutual funds, primary dealers (underwrite auctions and make markets), foreign portfolio investors, RBI itself (open-market operations) and retail investors (RBI Retail Direct, exchange platforms). Intermediaries: arrangers, credit rating agencies (CRISIL, ICRA, CARE, India Ratings), debenture trustees, depositories.

3. Instruments. Zero-coupon T-Bills (91/182/364 days); fixed-coupon dated G-Secs (up to 40 years or longer); floating-rate bonds; inflation-indexed bonds; STRIPS; SDLs; corporate NCDs (secured/unsecured), tax-free bonds, perpetual bank bonds, CP and CDs; green and sustainability-linked bonds.

4. Primary market. G-Secs are sold through RBI auctions on its e-Kuber platform against a published issuance calendar – competitive bids from banks and primary dealers, non-competitive bids for retail within a reserved share; auctions may be uniform-price or multiple-price. Corporate bonds are mostly privately placed through the exchanges' Electronic Book Provider (EBP) platforms; public issues follow SEBI's prospectus and disclosure rules.

5. Secondary market and infrastructure. G-Secs trade on RBI's NDS-OM (anonymous order matching) and OTC (reported), with CCIL as central counterparty guaranteeing settlement on a delivery-versus-payment basis; holdings sit in SGL/CSGL accounts at RBI. Corporate bonds trade OTC, are reported on the NSE/BSE platforms and settle through the clearing corporations. Repo and TREPS (tri-party repo on CCIL) provide liquidity.

6. Market dynamics. Prices respond to RBI policy (MPC rate decisions, liquidity operations), inflation, fiscal borrowing announced in the Union Budget, global rates, and index inclusion flows (Indian G-Secs entered JP Morgan's emerging-market bond index from June 2024). Liquidity is concentrated in a few benchmark G-Secs; the corporate market is shallower and concentrated in highly rated issuers.

Conclusion. India has a large, well-regulated sovereign bond market with strong infrastructure (NDS-OM, CCIL, e-Kuber) and a developing corporate market; deepening the latter – through retail access, EBP, credit enhancement and green instruments – is a stated policy objective.

L2. Compare uniform-price and discriminatory-price auctions. Discuss their implications with an illustration. (10 marks)

Structure: define auction & cut-off → define both formats → numerical illustration → comparison table → implications for issuer, bidders, market → Indian practice → conclusion.

Full model answer

Introduction. Governments sell securities by auction so that price is discovered competitively. Bids are ranked from the highest price (lowest yield) downwards; the price at which cumulative bids reach the notified amount is the cut-off price.

Uniform-price (Dutch) auction. All successful bidders pay the same cut-off price. Discriminatory (multiple-price, French) auction. Each successful bidder pays its own bid price.

Illustration (Practice Q5). Notified ₹15,000 crore; bids A 100.60 (3,000), B 100.45 (4,000), C 100.30 (2,500), D 100.20 (4,500), E 100.10 (3,000). Cumulative reaches 14,000 after D, so the cut-off is ₹100.10 and E receives ₹1,000 crore. Uniform proceeds = 15,000 × 1.0010 = ₹15,015 crore. Discriminatory proceeds = ₹15,053.5 crore, ₹38.5 crore more for the same bids.

BasisUniformDiscriminatory
Price paidOne cut-off priceOwn bid
Bidding behaviourBid close to true valueShade bids down
Winner's curseLowHigh
Issuer revenueLower for given bids; may be higher once behaviour adjustsHigher for given bids
TransparencyHigh – one priceLower – many prices
ParticipationEncourages smaller, less-informed biddersFavours sophisticated bidders
Price discoveryBetter reflects consensus valueCan be distorted by strategic bidding

Implications. (1) For the issuer: discriminatory auctions extract more consumer surplus if bids are unchanged, but bidders anticipate this and shade down, so the revenue advantage may disappear. (2) For bidders: uniform auctions reduce the risk of overpaying and suit volatile markets or new/long instruments where valuation is uncertain. (3) For the market: uniform pricing produces a single clear signal for the secondary market; wide dispersion in discriminatory results signals uncertainty. (4) Collusion: uniform auctions can be more vulnerable to bid-rigging among large players, since all winners gain from a low cut-off.

Indian practice. RBI uses both formats and announces the method to be used for each auction in its press release; uniform pricing is the natural choice where price uncertainty is high (new or very long securities), multiple pricing where the security is well established and liquid. Non-competitive bidders are allotted at the weighted-average price/yield of accepted competitive bids.

Conclusion. Neither format is universally superior: discriminatory auctions maximise revenue for given bids; uniform auctions maximise transparency, participation and price-discovery quality. The choice depends on market conditions and the maturity of the instrument.

L3. Case: A bank treasury expects yields to rise. (15 marks, Section C style)

Sahyadri Bank holds a ₹500 crore investment portfolio: ₹300 crore in G-Secs (DMod 6, convexity 50) and ₹200 crore in AA corporate bonds (DMod 4, convexity 22). The treasury head expects the RBI to tighten policy and yields to rise by 100 bp, with corporate spreads possibly widening by a further 150 bp. (a) Compute portfolio duration and DV01. (b) Estimate the loss under +100 bp and under +100 bp plus 150 bp spread widening. (c) Recommend actions and evaluate them.

Structure: identify the issue (interest-rate and spread risk) → compute → interpret → options with pros/cons → recommendation → conclusion.

Full model answer

Issue. The bank is exposed to interest-rate (price) risk on both holdings and to credit-spread risk on the corporate bonds. Duration, convexity and scenario (stress) analysis quantify both.

(a) Duration and DV01. Dp = (300 × 6 + 200 × 4)/500 = 2,600/500 = 5.2. DV01 = 5.2 × ₹500 crore × 0.0001 = ₹0.26 crore = ₹26 lakh per basis point.

(b) Scenario losses using ΔV ≈ MV[−DModΔy + ½C(Δy)2]:

  • +100 bp parallel: G-Sec 300 × (−0.06 + 0.0025) = −₹17.25 cr; corporate 200 × (−0.04 + 0.0011) = −₹7.78 cr; total −₹25.03 crore (−5.01%).
  • +100 bp plus 150 bp spread widening (corporate yields +250 bp): G-Sec −₹17.25 cr; corporate 200 × (−0.10 + 0.006875) = −₹18.625 cr; total −₹35.875 crore (−7.18%).

(c) Options.

  1. Shorten duration – e.g. switch ₹150 crore of G-Secs (DMod 6) into T-Bills (DMod about 0.25): new Dp = (150 × 6 + 150 × 0.25 + 200 × 4)/500 = 3.475, cutting the +100 bp duration loss to about 3.475% × 500 = ₹17.4 crore. Cost: lower running yield if the curve is upward sloping; transaction costs; loss realised on sale if yields have already risen.
  2. Hedge with interest-rate futures or pay-fixed OIS swaps – keeps the bonds (and the SLR eligibility of G-Secs) while offsetting DV01. Cost: basis risk, margin requirements, board-approved limits needed.
  3. Reduce credit exposure – move part of the AA holdings into AAA or G-Secs to cut spread risk. Cost: lower yield (the credit spread given up).
  4. Re-classify / hold to maturity – securities held to maturity are not marked to market under RBI's investment norms, but this does not remove economic risk and is subject to RBI's classification limits and rules.
  5. Do nothing – justified only if the treasury's view is uncertain and the loss is within risk appetite and capital.

Recommendation. Combine (1) and (2): bring portfolio duration from 5.2 down to about 3.5 by shifting part of the long G-Secs to T-Bills and hedging a further slice with OIS, and trim the AA exposure moderately. This roughly halves the rate-shock loss while retaining most income. Reinvest the short positions at higher yields once the rise materialises (a reinvestment benefit). Set a DV01 limit and repeat the stress test monthly.

Conclusion. Duration converts a view on rates into a rupee number (₹26 lakh per bp); convexity refines it; stress testing shows the combined rate-plus-credit shock is about 43% worse than the rate shock alone. Treasury decisions should be based on these numbers, not on yield alone.

L4. Explain the shapes of the yield curve and the theories that explain them. (10 marks)

Structure: define → four shapes with data and meaning → four theories with what each explains and fails to explain → Indian example → conclusion.

Full model answer

Introduction. A yield curve plots yields of bonds of the same credit quality against their time to maturity (handout definition). Its shape summarises the market's expectations about rates, inflation and growth.

Shapes. (1) Normal – short < long (e.g. 1Y 5.5%, 20Y 7.2%): growth, higher future rates or inflation, term premium; the most common shape. (2) Flat – short ≈ long (6.0% to 6.2%): uncertainty or transition. (3) Inverted – short > long (7.5% to 6.0%): expected rate cuts or slowdown; tight current policy. (4) Humped – medium maturities highest (5Y 6.8% vs 1Y 5.5% and 20Y 6.0%): mixed signals.

Theories.

  1. Pure expectations theory: long rates are the geometric average of current and expected future short rates, (1 + Rn)n = (1 + r1)(1 + E r2)…; forward rates are unbiased forecasts. Explains why curves move together and why expected cuts invert the curve; fails to explain why the curve is usually upward sloping.
  2. Liquidity preference theory (Hicks): investors prefer short bonds, so long bonds must pay a term premium increasing with maturity. Explains the usual upward slope; forward rates overstate expected rates.
  3. Market segmentation theory: different investors keep to their own maturity segments (banks short, insurers and pension funds long); each segment's yield is set by its own supply and demand. Explains kinks and humps (e.g. heavy long-dated issuance); fails to explain why yields across maturities move together.
  4. Preferred habitat theory (Modigliani–Sutch): investors prefer a segment but will move if paid a premium. Combines the strengths of the others and can explain every shape.

Indian example. When the RBI raised the policy repo rate sharply during 2022–23, short-term yields rose faster than long-term yields and the G-Sec curve flattened; heavy demand from insurers and pension funds at the very long end has at times kept 30–40-year yields close to 10-year yields – a segmentation/habitat effect.

Conclusion. Shapes describe what the market is pricing; the theories explain why. Preferred habitat is the most complete, but no curve should be read as a guaranteed forecast.

L5. What is bootstrapping? Derive spot and forward rates from par yields of 6%, 6.5% and 7% and use them to price a bond. (10 marks)

Structure: need for spot rates → method → step-by-step numbers → forward rates → pricing → uses and limitations → conclusion.

Full model answer

Introduction. Coupon bonds pay cash at many dates, so their YTM is an average of rates for different maturities. To value each cash flow correctly we need the spot (zero-coupon) rate for each date. Bootstrapping derives them one maturity at a time from observed coupon-bond prices or par yields.

Method. For a par bond (price 1, coupon cn): 1 = cn(d1 + … + dn−1) + (1 + cn)dn, so dn = [1 − cnΣdt]/(1 + cn) and sn = dn−1/n − 1.

Calculation (full steps in Extra Numerical 9): d1 = 1/1.06 = 0.943396, s1 = 6.00%; d2 = (1 − 0.065 × 0.943396)/1.065 = 0.881389, s2 = 6.52%; d3 = (1 − 0.07 × 1.824785)/1.07 = 0.815201, s3 = 7.05%.

Forward rates. f1,1 = 1.0651632/1.06 − 1 = 7.04%; f2,1 = 1.0704803/1.0651632 − 1 = 8.12%. Forwards lie above spots because the curve slopes upward.

Pricing. A 3-year 8% bond (face ₹1,000): 80 × 0.943396 + 80 × 0.881389 + 1,080 × 0.815201 = 75.47 + 70.51 + 880.42 = ₹1,026.40.

Uses. Arbitrage-free pricing of any bond; identifying rich/cheap bonds; implied forward rates for expectations and forward-rate agreements; discounting for fair-value accounting (FBIL and data vendors publish zero-coupon curves). Limitations. Needs a bond at every maturity (interpolation otherwise); errors propagate to longer maturities; illiquid bonds give noisy prices; smoothing models (Nelson–Siegel) are used in practice.

Conclusion. Bootstrapping turns a par curve into a spot curve, the foundation of all yield-curve analytics.

L6. Distinguish active and passive bond portfolio strategies and explain immunisation. (10 marks)

Structure: define both → active strategies (list) → passive strategies (list) → comparison table → immunisation (conditions, example, limitations) → conclusion.

Full model answer

Introduction. Bond portfolio management is either active – trying to beat a benchmark by forecasting rates, curve shape or spreads – or passive – accepting market returns or meeting defined liabilities at low cost.

Active strategies. (1) Interest-rate anticipation (lengthen duration before expected falls, shorten before rises). (2) Yield-curve strategies: bullet, barbell, ladder positioning; riding the curve. (3) Sector and credit-spread strategies (overweight corporates when spreads are expected to narrow). (4) Bond swaps: substitution, pure yield pickup, tax swaps. (5) Security selection of undervalued bonds.

Passive strategies. (1) Buy-and-hold. (2) Indexing (e.g. debt index funds and target-maturity funds tracking Bharat Bond or SDL indices). (3) Immunisation and cash-flow matching for liabilities. (4) Laddering.

BasisActivePassive
ObjectiveBeat benchmark (alpha)Match benchmark or liabilities
BeliefMarket can be forecastMarkets are efficient
Turnover and costHighLow
RiskForecast error, tracking errorTracking the market down
ExamplesDynamic bond fundsTarget maturity / index funds, pension ALM

Immunisation. Protecting a portfolio's value at a horizon against interest-rate changes by setting (i) PV of assets = PV of the liability and (ii) asset Macaulay duration = liability horizon (and asset convexity ≥ liability convexity). If yields rise, price falls but coupons are reinvested at higher rates; if yields fall, the reverse – the two effects offset. Example (Extra Numerical 8): a ₹10 crore liability in 5 years at 8% needs ₹6.806 crore today; 60% in a 3-year zero and 40% in an 8-year zero gives duration 5, and the portfolio is worth about ₹10.0026 crore at year 5 if yields move to 7% (₹10.0025 crore at 9%). Limitations: works for small parallel shifts only; needs rebalancing as time passes and yields change; ignores default and call risk.

Conclusion. Active management seeks extra return at extra risk; passive management seeks certainty at low cost. Contingent immunisation blends them: manage actively while a cushion exists, switch to immunisation when the floor is reached.

L7. Case: A pension fund must pay ₹10 crore in five years. (15 marks)

Ganga Pension Trust must pay ₹10 crore in exactly five years. The flat yield curve is at 8%. Its adviser offers (i) 3-year and 8-year zero-coupon G-Secs, or (ii) putting everything in the 8-year zero because it "yields the same and is simpler". (a) How much must be invested today? (b) Construct an immunised portfolio. (c) Show the year-5 value under 7%, 8% and 9% for the immunised portfolio and for the 8-year-only and 3-year-only alternatives. (d) Advise the trustees.

Full model answer

(a) PV = 10/1.085 = 10/1.469328 = ₹6.8058 crore.

(b) Weight in 3-year zero: w3 = (8 − 5)/(8 − 3) = 0.6. Invest 0.6 × 6.8058 = ₹4.0835 crore in the 3-year zero (face value 4.0835 × 1.083 = ₹5.1440 crore) and ₹2.7223 crore in the 8-year zero (face value 2.7223 × 1.088 = ₹5.0388 crore). Duration = 0.6 × 3 + 0.4 × 8 = 5 years = horizon.

(c) Year-5 value if yields shift immediately and stay there (3-year proceeds reinvested for 2 years; 8-year zero sold with 3 years left):

YieldImmunised (3Y + 8Y)All in 8Y zeroAll in 3Y zero
7%10.002610.28309.8157
8%10.000010.000010.0000
9%10.00259.727310.1860

(₹ crore. Immunised at 7%: 5.1440 × 1.072 + 5.0388/1.073; at 9%: 5.1440 × 1.092 + 5.0388/1.093.)

(d) Advice. The 8-year-only portfolio has duration 8 > 5: it gains if rates fall but is short by ₹27 lakh if rates rise to 9% – a bet, not a hedge. The 3-year-only portfolio has the opposite (reinvestment) risk: short by ₹18 lakh if rates fall to 7%. The duration-matched barbell meets the liability in all three cases and even shows a small surplus (convexity of the barbell exceeds that of the liability). Recommend the immunised portfolio, rebalanced annually (duration drifts as time passes and yields change), using G-Sec STRIPS or zero-coupon G-Secs to avoid credit risk. Note limitations: non-parallel shifts (e.g. a steepening) can still hurt a barbell; a single 5-year zero (cash-flow matching) would remove even that risk if available.

L8. Green bonds vs sustainability-linked bonds; greenwashing; India's Sovereign Green Bonds. (10/15 marks)

Full model answer

Introduction. Sustainable finance channels capital to environmental and social goals. In bond markets the two main labels are use-of-proceeds bonds (green, social, sustainability) and performance-linked bonds (SLBs).

Green bonds. Proceeds are ring-fenced for eligible green projects (renewable energy, clean transport, energy efficiency, water, green buildings, climate adaptation). ICMA Green Bond Principles: (1) use of proceeds, (2) project evaluation and selection, (3) management of proceeds (tracking/sub-account), (4) reporting (allocation and impact); external review recommended. SEBI regulates "green debt securities" for listed issuers.

Sustainability-linked bonds. Proceeds are for general corporate purposes; the coupon (or redemption) changes depending on whether the issuer meets pre-set sustainability performance targets (SPTs) on material KPIs (e.g. emissions intensity) by a target date – typically a coupon step-up of 25 bp if missed. SLB Principles: KPI selection, SPT calibration, bond characteristics, reporting, verification.

BasisGreen bondSLB
FocusWhat the money is used forHow the issuer performs
Use of proceedsRing-fenced to eligible projectsGeneral purposes
CouponFixedLinked to SPTs (step-up/down)
SuitsIssuers with identifiable green projectsHard-to-abate issuers transitioning (steel, cement)
Key riskProceeds diverted, weak impactUnambitious targets, small penalty

Greenwashing. Presenting an instrument or issuer as greener than it is – vague use-of-proceeds, refinancing old assets, weak reporting, unambitious SLB targets, or penalties too small to matter. Safeguards: alignment with GBP/SLBP, taxonomies, second-party opinions, third-party verification, regular impact reporting, SEBI disclosure rules.

India's SGrBs. Announced in the Union Budget 2022–23; the Government published a Sovereign Green Bond Framework (reviewed by an external reviewer), and RBI auctioned the first SGrBs in January and February 2023 (₹8,000 crore each, 5-year and 10-year) at a small greenium. Proceeds finance public-sector projects such as renewable energy and clean transport; allocation is reported by the Government.

Conclusion. Green bonds finance green projects; SLBs finance green transitions. Both are only as credible as their frameworks, verification and reporting.

L9. Discuss digital and tokenised bonds and the role of blockchain/DLT and smart contracts. (10 marks)

Full model answer

Introduction. Technology is changing how bonds are issued, recorded and settled. A digital bond is issued, recorded and serviced electronically end-to-end; a tokenised bond goes further, representing ownership as tokens on a distributed ledger.

How it works. The issuer creates tokens on a (usually permissioned) blockchain, each representing a unit of the bond; investors hold them in digital wallets; a smart contract encodes coupon, maturity and transfer rules; settlement is delivery-versus-payment against tokenised cash or a central-bank digital currency.

Role of DLT. A shared, tamper-evident ledger replaces separate records at registrar, depository and custodian; all parties see the same "golden record", reducing reconciliation.

Role of smart contracts. Automatic coupon payment to current holders, redemption, corporate-action processing, compliance checks (KYC-whitelisted wallets), and SLB coupon adjustments triggered by verified KPI data.

Benefits. (1) Faster settlement (potentially T+0) and lower counterparty risk; (2) fractional ownership, widening retail access; (3) lower issuance and servicing costs; (4) transparency and auditability; (5) programmability; (6) potential 24×7 markets.

Challenges. Legal recognition of tokens as securities; interoperability with existing depositories and payment systems; cyber and code risk; data (oracle) reliability; scalability and privacy; small secondary-market liquidity so far; regulatory approvals.

Examples. The European Investment Bank has issued digital bonds on blockchain platforms; Hong Kong's government has issued tokenised green bonds. In India, RBI's e-rupee (CBDC) pilots, including a wholesale segment used for settling secondary-market G-Sec transactions, and IFSCA's work on tokenisation at GIFT City point in this direction; conventional Indian securities remain dematerialised with NSDL/CDSL and RBI.

Conclusion. Tokenisation does not change the bond's economics – price is still the PV of cash flows – but it can change the plumbing: cheaper, faster, more inclusive, provided legal and technical risks are managed.

Extra numericals (14) — fully solved

These cover every numerical type not already drilled by the Practice Questions: semi-annual pricing, exact YTM by interpolation, realised yield, T-Bills, YTC, accrued interest, full duration-convexity tables, portfolio DV01, immunisation, bootstrapping, riding the curve, repo, yield-based auctions, performance ratios and stress testing. Rounding: intermediate factors to 6 decimals, rupee amounts to 2 decimals, rates to 2 decimals unless stated.

Extra Numerical 1 – Semi-annual G-Sec pricing

A 7.26% G-Sec (face ₹100) has exactly 10 years to maturity and pays semi-annual coupons. (a) Find its price at a YTM of 7.10%. (b) What happens to price if the YTM rises to 7.40%?

Full solution
  1. Semi-annual coupon = 7.26/2 = ₹3.63; periods = 10 × 2 = 20; periodic yield = 7.10%/2 = 3.55%.
  2. PVIFA(3.55%, 20) = [1 − 1.0355−20]/0.0355 = 14.148318; PV of coupons = 3.63 × 14.148318 = ₹51.36.
  3. Discount factor 1.0355−20 = 0.497735; PV of face = 100 × 0.497735 = ₹49.77.
  4. Price = 51.36 + 49.77 = ₹101.13 – a premium, because coupon 7.26% > yield 7.10%.
  5. (b) Repeating at 3.70% per half-year gives ₹99.02: the bond moves to a discount because the yield now exceeds the coupon. A 30 bp rise cost ₹2.11 (about 2.1%), consistent with a modified duration of roughly 7.

Extra Numerical 2 – Exact YTM by interpolation (reverse problem) and realised yield

A 6-year bond, face ₹1,000, 8.5% annual coupon, trades at ₹935. Required return 9%. (a) Approximate YTM. (b) YTM by interpolation. (c) Is it worth buying? (d) Realised compound yield if coupons can be reinvested at only 7%.

Full solution
  1. (a) Approx YTM = [85 + (1,000 − 935)/6] / [(1,000 + 935)/2] = (85 + 10.8333)/967.5 = 95.8333/967.5 = 9.91%.
  2. (b) Try 9%: PVIFA = 4.485919, DF = 0.596267; P = 85 × 4.485919 + 1,000 × 0.596267 = 381.30 + 596.27 = ₹977.57.
  3. Try 10%: PVIFA = 4.355261, DF = 0.564474; P = 370.20 + 564.47 = ₹934.67.
  4. Interpolate: 9% + (977.57 − 935)/(977.57 − 934.67) × 1% = 9% + (42.57/42.90) × 1% = 9.99% (Excel RATE(6,85,-935,1000) gives 9.99%).
  5. (c) Value at the required 9% = ₹977.57 > price ₹935 ⇒ undervalued by ₹42.57; equivalently YTM 9.99% > 9% ⇒ buy.
  6. (d) FV of coupons at 7% = 85 × FVIFA(7%, 6) = 85 × 7.153291 = ₹608.03. Terminal value = 608.03 + 1,000 = ₹1,608.03.
  7. Realised compound yield = (1,608.03/935)1/6 − 1 = 9.46% – below the 9.99% YTM, because reinvestment at 7% is below the YTM (reinvestment risk).

Extra Numerical 3 – Treasury Bill yield and price

(a) A 91-day T-Bill (face ₹100) is allotted at ₹98.30. Find its annualised yield. (b) What price corresponds to a 6.50% yield on a 91-day bill? (c) Price of a 364-day bill at 6.60%.

Full solution
  1. Formula (Actual/365): y = [(F − P)/P] × 365/d.
  2. (a) y = (1.70/98.30) × 365/91 = 0.017294 × 4.010989 = 6.94%.
  3. (b) P = F/(1 + y × d/365) = 100/(1 + 0.065 × 91/365) = 100/1.016205 = ₹98.41.
  4. (c) P = 100/(1 + 0.066 × 364/365) = 100/1.065819 = ₹93.82.
  5. Check of the inverse relation: the lower yield in (b) (6.50% vs 6.94%) gives the higher price (98.41 vs 98.30).

Extra Numerical 4 – Yield to call and yield to worst

A 10-year 9% annual-coupon bond (face ₹1,000) is callable after 4 years at ₹1,030. It trades at ₹1,080. Find (a) approximate and interpolated YTM, (b) approximate and interpolated YTC, (c) yield to worst.

Full solution
  1. (a) Approx YTM = [90 + (1,000 − 1,080)/10]/[(1,000 + 1,080)/2] = 82/1,040 = 7.88%.
  2. Interpolation: at 7%, P = 90 × 7.023582 + 1,000 × 0.508349 = 632.12 + 508.35 = ₹1,140.47; at 8%, P = 90 × 6.710081 + 1,000 × 0.463193 = 603.91 + 463.19 = ₹1,067.10.
  3. YTM = 7% + (1,140.47 − 1,080)/(1,140.47 − 1,067.10) × 1% = 7% + (60.47/73.37)% = 7.82%.
  4. (b) Approx YTC = [90 + (1,030 − 1,080)/4]/[(1,030 + 1,080)/2] = 77.5/1,055 = 7.35%.
  5. Interpolation with n = 4 and redemption ₹1,030: at 7%, 90 × 3.387211 + 1,030 × 0.762895 = 304.85 + 785.78 = ₹1,090.63; at 8%, 90 × 3.312127 + 1,030 × 0.735030 = 298.09 + 757.08 = ₹1,055.17.
  6. YTC = 7% + (1,090.63 − 1,080)/(1,090.63 − 1,055.17) × 1% = 7% + (10.63/35.46)% = 7.30%.
  7. (c) Yield to worst = lower of YTM and YTC = 7.30%. The bond trades well above the call price, so the issuer is likely to call; investors should price it on YTC.

Extra Numerical 5 – Accrued interest and dirty price

A mutual fund buys ₹5 crore face value of a 7.18% G-Sec (semi-annual coupons, 30/360) at a clean price of ₹101.25. 58 days (30/360 count) have passed since the last coupon. Find the accrued interest and the total amount payable.

Full solution
  1. Coupon per half-year per ₹100 = 7.18/2 = ₹3.59; days in the half-year = 180.
  2. Accrued interest per ₹100 = 3.59 × 58/180 = ₹1.1568 (to 4 decimals).
  3. Dirty price = 101.25 + 1.1568 = ₹102.4068 per ₹100.
  4. Clean consideration = 5,00,00,000 × 101.25/100 = ₹5,06,25,000; accrued interest = 5,00,00,000 × 3.59 × 58/180/100 = ₹5,78,389 (rounded to the rupee).
  5. Total payable = 5,06,25,000 + 5,78,389 = ₹5,12,03,389. The seller receives the interest earned for 58 days; the buyer receives the full ₹17,95,000 coupon (5 crore × 3.59%) on the next coupon date.

Extra Numerical 6 – Full duration and convexity table, and testing the estimates

A 4-year 10% annual-coupon bond (face ₹1,000) yields 12%. Find price, Macaulay and modified duration, and convexity. Estimate the price for yield changes of +1.5% and −1.5% using duration alone and duration + convexity, and compare with the actual prices.

Full solution

  1. Price = ₹939.25 (discount bond: coupon 10% < yield 12%).
  2. DM = 3,258.54/939.253 = 3.4693 years.
  3. DMod = 3.4693/1.12 = 3.0976.
  4. Convexity = 15,492.421/[939.253 × 1.122] = 15,492.421/(939.253 × 1.2544) = 13.149.
