Duration alone overstates the loss and understates the gain; adding convexity brings both estimates within about ₹0.05 of the true price. Note also the asymmetry: the gain (+₹45.07) exceeds the loss (−₹42.29) – Malkiel's fifth theorem.

Extra Numerical 7 – Portfolio duration, DV01 and duration adjustment

A debt fund holds ₹200 crore of bonds with DMod 2.5, ₹300 crore with DMod 5 and ₹500 crore with DMod 8. (a) Portfolio modified duration. (b) DV01. (c) Estimated loss for a 50 bp rise. (d) How much of the 8-duration holding must be switched into T-Bills (DMod 0.25) to bring portfolio duration to 4.5?

Full solution
  1. (a) Dp = (200 × 2.5 + 300 × 5 + 500 × 8)/1,000 = (500 + 1,500 + 4,000)/1,000 = 6.0.
  2. (b) DV01 = 6.0 × ₹1,000 crore × 0.0001 = ₹0.6 crore = ₹60 lakh per basis point.
  3. (c) Loss ≈ 6.0 × 0.005 × 1,000 = ₹30 crore (3%).
  4. (d) Switching ₹x crore from D 8 to D 0.25: (6,000 − 8x + 0.25x)/1,000 = 4.5 ⇒ 7.75x = 1,500 ⇒ x = ₹193.55 crore.
  5. Check: (500 + 1,500 + (500 − 193.55) × 8 + 193.55 × 0.25)/1,000 = (500 + 1,500 + 2,451.61 + 48.39)/1,000 = 4.50.

Extra Numerical 8 – Immunisation with a barbell of zeros

A liability of ₹10 crore is due in 5 years; the flat yield is 8%. Using 3-year and 8-year zero-coupon G-Secs, build an immunised portfolio and test it at 7% and 9%.

Full solution
  1. PV of liability = 10/1.085 = 10/1.469328 = ₹6.8058 crore.
  2. Duration match: w3 × 3 + (1 − w3) × 8 = 5 ⇒ w3 = (8 − 5)/(8 − 3) = 0.6.
  3. Invest 0.6 × 6.8058 = ₹4.0835 crore in the 3-year zero (maturity value 4.0835 × 1.083 = ₹5.1440 crore) and 0.4 × 6.8058 = ₹2.7223 crore in the 8-year zero (maturity value 2.7223 × 1.088 = ₹5.0388 crore).
  4. If yields move to 7% immediately: value at year 5 = 5.1440 × 1.072 + 5.0388/1.073 = 5.8894 + 4.1132 = ₹10.0026 crore.
  5. If yields move to 9%: 5.1440 × 1.092 + 5.0388/1.093 = 6.1116 + 3.8909 = ₹10.0025 crore.
  6. The liability is covered in both cases: reinvestment gains and price losses offset. (All-in-8-year would give only ₹9.7273 crore at 9%; all-in-3-year only ₹9.8157 crore at 7%.)

Extra Numerical 9 – Bootstrapping spot rates, forward rates and arbitrage-free price

Annual-pay par yields: 1-year 6%, 2-year 6.5%, 3-year 7%. (a) Bootstrap discount factors and spot rates. (b) Find f1,1, f2,1 and f1,2. (c) Price a 3-year 8% bond (face ₹1,000) and find its YTM.

Full solution
  1. d1 = 1/1.06 = 0.943396 ⇒ s1 = 6.00%.
  2. 2-year par bond: 1 = 0.065d1 + 1.065d2 ⇒ d2 = (1 − 0.065 × 0.943396)/1.065 = 0.938679/1.065 = 0.881389 ⇒ s2 = 0.881389−1/2 − 1 = 6.52%.
  3. 3-year: d3 = [1 − 0.07 × (0.943396 + 0.881389)]/1.07 = (1 − 0.127735)/1.07 = 0.872265/1.07 = 0.815201 ⇒ s3 = 0.815201−1/3 − 1 = 7.05%.
  4. (b) f1,1 = d1/d2 − 1 = 0.943396/0.881389 − 1 = 7.04%; f2,1 = d2/d3 − 1 = 0.881389/0.815201 − 1 = 8.12%; f1,2 = (d1/d3)1/2 − 1 = (1.157255)1/2 − 1 = 7.58%.
  5. (c) Price = 80 × 0.943396 + 80 × 0.881389 + 1,080 × 0.815201 = 75.47 + 70.51 + 880.42 = ₹1,026.40.
  6. Its YTM (IRR at ₹1,026.40) = 6.99% – slightly below the 7% 3-year par yield, because this high-coupon bond receives more of its cash early, when spot rates are lower. Valuing it at the 7% par yield would give ₹1,026.24 – a small mispricing that bootstrapping avoids.

Extra Numerical 10 – Riding the yield curve

Zero-coupon yields: 1-year 6%, 2-year 7%. An investor with ₹10 lakh has a 1-year horizon. Compare (a) buying the 1-year zero with (b) buying the 2-year zero and selling it after one year, if the curve is unchanged. (c) What if the 1-year yield rises to 9%? (d) What 1-year yield next year makes both equal?

Full solution
  1. (a) ₹10,00,000 × 1.06 = ₹10,60,000 (6%).
  2. (b) Price of 2-year zero per ₹100 = 100/1.072 = 100/1.1449 = ₹87.3439; units bought = 10,00,000/87.3439 = 11,449.
  3. After one year it is a 1-year zero; with the curve unchanged it is priced at the 1-year yield of 6%: 100/1.06 = ₹94.3396.
  4. Value = 11,449 × 94.3396 = ₹10,80,094; return = 94.3396/87.3439 − 1 = 8.01% – the 7% yield plus the "roll-down" gain from the bond's yield falling from 7% to 6%.
  5. (c) At 9%: sale price 100/1.09 = ₹91.7431; return = 91.7431/87.3439 − 1 = 5.04% – worse than the 6% bill.
  6. (d) Break-even 1-year rate next year = the forward rate f1,1 = 1.1449/1.06 − 1 = 8.01%. Riding pays if next year's 1-year yield is below 8.01%.

Extra Numerical 11 – Repo transaction

A bank needs funds for 7 days. It sells G-Secs with a market value of ₹100 crore under repo at 6.50% with a 2% haircut. Find the cash received, the repo interest and the repurchase amount.

Full solution
  1. Cash received (first leg) = 100 × (1 − 0.02) = ₹98 crore.
  2. Repo interest = 98 × 0.065 × 7/365 = ₹0.122164 crore = ₹12,21,644.
  3. Repurchase amount (second leg) = 98 + 0.122164 = ₹98.1222 crore.
  4. For the cash lender this is a reverse repo earning 6.50% for 7 days, protected by collateral worth ₹2 crore more than the cash lent.

Extra Numerical 12 – Yield-based auction with non-competitive bids

RBI notifies ₹6,000 crore of a new 10-year G-Sec, reserving 5% for non-competitive bids (fully subscribed). Competitive bids (yield, ₹ crore): A 7.02% 1,500; B 7.04% 2,000; C 7.05% 1,500; D 7.06% 1,800; E 7.08% 1,000. Find the cut-off yield, allotments, and the weighted-average yield; compare the annual interest cost under uniform and multiple-yield methods.

Full solution
  1. Non-competitive = 5% × 6,000 = ₹300 crore; competitive amount = ₹5,700 crore.
  2. Rank from the lowest yield (highest price): cumulative A 1,500; B 3,500; C 5,000; D 6,800 > 5,700 ⇒ cut-off yield = 7.06%.
  3. Allotments: A 1,500, B 2,000, C 1,500 in full; D gets 5,700 − 5,000 = ₹700 crore of 1,800 (pro-rata 38.89%); E rejected.
  4. Weighted-average yield of accepted competitive bids = (1,500 × 7.02 + 2,000 × 7.04 + 1,500 × 7.05 + 700 × 7.06)/5,700 = (10,530 + 14,080 + 10,575 + 4,942)/5,700 = 40,127/5,700 = 7.0398%.
  5. Uniform: everyone at 7.06% ⇒ annual cost ≈ 6,000 × 7.06% = ₹423.60 crore.
  6. Multiple-yield: competitive cost = 40,127/100 = ₹401.27 crore; non-competitive at the weighted average = 300 × 7.0398% = ₹21.12 crore; total ₹422.39 crore – ₹1.21 crore a year less for these bids (before any bid-shading response).

Extra Numerical 13 – Sharpe, Treynor and Jensen's alpha

Risk-free rate 6.5%, bond-index return 8.5%. Fund X: return 9.2%, SD 3.5%, beta 0.8. Fund Y: return 10.1%, SD 5.0%, beta 1.1. Evaluate both.

Full solution
MeasureFund XFund Y
Excess return Rp − Rf2.7%3.6%
Sharpe = excess/SD2.7/3.5 = 0.773.6/5.0 = 0.72
Treynor = excess/beta2.7/0.8 = 3.383.6/1.1 = 3.27
CAPM required return Rf + β(Rm − Rf)6.5 + 0.8 × 2 = 8.1%6.5 + 1.1 × 2 = 8.7%
Jensen's alpha9.2 − 8.1 = +1.10%10.1 − 8.7 = +1.40%

Interpretation. Both funds beat the market on a risk-adjusted basis (positive alphas). Per unit of total risk (Sharpe) and of systematic risk (Treynor), Fund X is better; Fund Y delivers the larger absolute alpha but takes proportionately more risk to do it. For an investor holding only one fund, choose X (Sharpe); for a fund added to a diversified portfolio, the Treynor ranking also favours X.

Extra Numerical 14 – Stress test and credit expected loss

Portfolio: ₹300 crore G-Secs (DMod 6, C 50) and ₹200 crore AA corporates (DMod 4, C 22). Estimate value changes under: S1 +100 bp parallel; S2 +200 bp parallel; S3 −75 bp parallel; S4 corporate spreads +150 bp only. Also find the one-year expected credit loss on the corporates if PD = 2% and LGD = 60%.

Full solution

Formula: ΔV ≈ MV × [−DModΔy + ½C(Δy)2].

ScenarioG-Sec (₹ cr)Corporate (₹ cr)Total (₹ cr)% of ₹500 cr
S1 +100 bp300(−0.06 + 0.0025) = −17.25200(−0.04 + 0.0011) = −7.78−25.03−5.01%
S2 +200 bp300(−0.12 + 0.01) = −33.00200(−0.08 + 0.0044) = −15.12−48.12−9.62%
S3 −75 bp300(0.045 + 0.00140625) = +13.92200(0.03 + 0.00061875) = +6.12+20.05+4.01%
S4 spreads +150 bp0.00200(−0.06 + 0.002475) = −11.51−11.51−2.30%
  1. Portfolio duration = (300 × 6 + 200 × 4)/500 = 5.2: a 1% parallel rise costs about 5%, and doubling the shock (S2) costs slightly less than double (9.62% vs 2 × 5.01%) because of convexity.
  2. Expected loss = PD × LGD × EAD = 0.02 × 0.60 × 200 = ₹2.4 crore a year – the spread on the AA bonds must at least cover about 1.2% (2.4/200) for credit risk before any risk premium.
  3. Conclusion: the portfolio's largest vulnerability is a parallel rate rise (G-Sec duration); spread widening adds a smaller but separate risk.

Differences-between master table

Every "distinguish between" pair the examiner can set, in one place. In the exam, draw a table with 5–6 bases for 5 marks, add a one-line definition of each term above the table and an example below it.

#PairFirst termSecond termKey basis of difference
1Bond vs equity shareDebt; fixed coupon; repaid at maturity; priority in liquidation; no voting rightsOwnership; variable dividend; no maturity; residual claim; voting rightsNature of claim
2Primary vs secondary marketNew issues; money goes to issuer (RBI auctions, EBP)Existing securities change hands; money goes to seller (NDS-OM, OTC)Who receives the cash
3G-Sec vs corporate bondSovereign, no credit risk in rupees; RBI-regulated; SLR-eligible; very liquid benchmarksCredit risk; rated; SEBI-regulated; spread over G-Sec; thinner liquidityIssuer and credit risk
4T-Bill vs dated security≤ 364 days; zero coupon; issued at discountUsually 2–40+ years; semi-annual couponTenor and coupon
5Uniform vs discriminatory auctionAll winners pay cut-off priceEach winner pays own bidPrice paid; winner's curse; transparency
6Competitive vs non-competitive bidQuote price/yield and amount; may be rejectedQuote amount only; assured allotment at weighted-average pricePrice quoted; allotment certainty
7Repo vs reverse repoSell now, buy back later = borrow cashBuy now, sell back later = lend cashDirection of cash
8Repo vs outright saleTemporary; ownership returns; collateralised loanPermanent transfer of ownershipReversal agreement
9Coupon rate vs current yield vs YTMCoupon/face; fixedCY = coupon/price; YTM = IRR of all flows incl. capital gainWhat the denominator is; whether capital gain/time value is included
10YTM vs YTCAssumes held to maturity, redeemed at faceAssumes called on call date at call priceRedemption date and amount
11YTM vs realised (holding-period) yieldPromised, ex-ante; reinvestment at YTMActual, ex-post; actual reinvestment rates and sale pricePromise vs outcome
12Coupon bond vs zero-coupon bondPeriodic coupons; duration < maturity; reinvestment riskSingle payment; duration = maturity; no reinvestment risk; more price-sensitiveCash-flow pattern
13Premium vs discount vs par bondPrice > face: coupon > YTMPrice < face: coupon < YTM; at par coupon = YTMCoupon vs market yield
14Clean vs dirty priceQuoted price excluding accrued interestAmount paid = clean + accrued interestAccrued interest
15Macaulay vs modified durationWeighted-average time, in yearsDM/(1 + y/m); % price change per 1% yieldUnit and meaning
16Modified vs effective durationFrom fixed cash flows (analytic)From repricing at y ± Δy; handles optionsOption-free vs callable/putable bonds
17Duration vs convexitySlope; first-order; linear estimateCurvature; second-order; correction termOrder of approximation (handout table)
18Positive vs negative convexityGains > losses; option-free bondsGains capped; callable bonds, MBS near callEmbedded options
19Interest-rate (price) risk vs reinvestment riskLoss when rates rise; long, low-coupon bondsLoss when rates fall; high-coupon, short bondsDirection of rate move; opposite effects (basis of immunisation)
20Credit (default) risk vs credit-spread riskIssuer fails to paySpread widens (e.g. downgrade) and price falls without defaultActual loss vs mark-to-market loss
21Term structure vs yield curveThe relationship between yield and maturityIts graphConcept vs picture (handout)
22Spot rate vs forward rateRate today for a loan starting todayRate agreed today for a loan starting in the futureStart date
23Spot rate vs par yield vs YTMZero-coupon rate for one datePar yield: coupon making price = par; YTM: single IRR for one bondWhat is being averaged
24Normal vs inverted curveShort < long; expansionShort > long; expected cuts/slowdownSlope sign
25Flat vs humped curveShort ≈ longMiddle highestWhere the peak is
26Expectations vs liquidity preference theoryNo premium; forwards = expected ratesTerm premium rising with maturityTerm premium
27Market segmentation vs preferred habitatInvestors never leave their segmentInvestors leave their habitat if paid a premiumMobility across maturities
28Active vs passive managementBeat the benchmark; forecasts; high turnoverMatch benchmark/liabilities; low costObjective
29Ladder vs barbell vs bulletLadder: even spread across maturitiesBarbell: short + long ends; bullet: concentrated at one maturityMaturity distribution, convexity
30Immunisation vs cash-flow matchingMatch PV and duration; needs rebalancingMatch each liability with a bond cash flow; no rebalancing; costlierPrecision and cost
31Buy-and-hold vs indexingHold selected bonds to maturityReplicate an index's risk characteristicsBenchmark tracking
32Stress testing vs scenario analysis vs sensitivity analysisStress: extreme but plausible shocksScenario: coherent set of changes (rates + spreads + curve); sensitivity: one factor at a timeSeverity and number of factors
33Sharpe vs TreynorExcess return per unit of total risk (σ)Excess return per unit of systematic risk (β)Risk measure
34Alpha vs betaReturn above what CAPM predicts (skill)Sensitivity to the market/benchmarkSkill vs market exposure
35Green bond vs SLBUse-of-proceeds; fixed coupon; GBP 4 componentsGeneral purpose; coupon linked to SPTs; SLBP 5 componentsProceeds vs performance
36Green bond vs conventional bondRing-fenced green use; reporting; external review; possible greeniumAny purpose; no impact reportingUse and disclosure
37KPI vs SPTThe metric (e.g. tCO2e per tonne of steel)The target level of that metric by a dateMeasure vs target
38Digital bond vs tokenised bondElectronic issuance and record-keeping (may use conventional systems)Ownership represented as tokens on a DLT; fractional, programmableLedger technology
39Permissioned vs permissionless blockchainKnown, approved validators (suits regulated bonds)Open to anyoneAccess and control
40Traditional vs DLT-based settlementMultiple intermediaries; T+1 and reconciliationsShared ledger; atomic DvP; potentially T+0Speed, cost, counterparty risk

Common mistakes and examiner traps (20)

  1. Using the coupon rate as the discount rate. Discount at the required return/YTM; the coupon rate only sets the cash flow.
  2. Forgetting to halve for semi-annual bonds. Halve the coupon and the yield, double the periods. Modified duration = DM/(1 + y/2).
  3. Percentage vs percentage points. 5% → 6% is a 1 percentage-point (100 bp) rise, not a 1% rise (handout). Always convert bp to decimals: 50 bp = 0.005.
  4. Dropping the minus sign in the duration formula. ΔP/P ≈ −DModΔy: yields up, price down.
  5. Using Macaulay duration in the price-change formula. Use modified duration. Macaulay is for timing and immunisation.
  6. Subtracting the convexity term. ½C(Δy)2 is always added, whichever way yield moves.
  7. Calling a bond "undervalued" because it trades below face value. Undervalued means price < intrinsic value at the required return; "discount" means price < face. Practice Q3 needs both words, separately.
  8. Ranking auction bids the wrong way. Price-based auctions: highest price first. Yield-based auctions: lowest yield first. The cut-off bid is allotted pro-rata, not in full.
  9. Computing uniform-auction proceeds at the weighted-average price. Uniform = total allotted × cut-off price; weighted average is for non-competitive bidders in multiple-price auctions.
  10. Believing the approximate YTM is exact. State "approximately"; for 10 marks refine by interpolation (Extra Numerical 2).
  11. Saying "YTM is the return I will earn". Only if held to maturity, no default and reinvestment at YTM; otherwise compute realised yield.
  12. Using YTM for a callable premium bond. Quote yield to worst (usually YTC).
  13. Humped curve misread as normal. Look at the middle, not just the ends.
  14. "An inverted curve guarantees a recession." It is a signal, not a guarantee (handout quiz A7).
  15. "Highest yield = best bond." Consider horizon, duration, credit, liquidity and expectations (handout Section 12).
  16. Treating forward rates as forecasts. They are no-arbitrage implied rates and include term premiums.
  17. Immunising by matching maturity instead of duration. A 5-year coupon bond has duration below 5; match duration and PV, and rebalance.
  18. Mixing up Sharpe and Treynor. Sharpe divides by standard deviation, Treynor by beta.
  19. Confusing green bonds with SLBs. Green = where the money goes; SLB = what the issuer achieves (coupon step-up if targets missed). Mixing their principles (4 vs 5 components) costs marks.
  20. Writing formulas without defining variables or rounding. Examiners award method marks: write the formula, define symbols, show substitution, state the answer with units (₹, %, years) and a one-line interpretation.

Glossary A–Z

Every technical term used in this guide, in one line each. Use the filter box in the sidebar to jump to a section, or your browser's find (Ctrl+F) for a term.

Accrued interest
Coupon earned by the seller since the last coupon date, paid by the buyer on top of the clean price.
Active management
Managing a bond portfolio to beat a benchmark through rate, curve, sector or security views.
Alpha (Jensen's)
Actual return minus the CAPM-required return Rf + β(Rm − Rf); a measure of manager skill.
Amortised cost
Carrying value that moves steadily from purchase price to face value; used for held-to-maturity securities.
Approximate YTM
[C + (F − P)/n]/[(F + P)/2]; quick estimate of YTM.
Auction (G-Sec)
Competitive sale of government securities by RBI; bids ranked by price or yield up to the notified amount.
Barbell
Portfolio concentrated in short and long maturities, avoiding the middle; high convexity.
Basis point (bp)
One-hundredth of a percentage point: 0.01% = 0.0001.
Benchmark bond
The most liquid G-Sec at a key maturity (e.g. the 10-year) used as the reference yield.
Beta
Sensitivity of a portfolio's return to the benchmark's return: Cov(Rp, Rm)/Var(Rm).
Bloomberg Terminal
Professional data and analytics platform; bond functions include DES, YAS, GC (curves), SRCH and PORT.
Bootstrapping
Deriving zero-coupon (spot) rates maturity by maturity from coupon-bond prices or par yields.
Bullet
Portfolio concentrated around one maturity.
Buy-and-hold
Passive strategy of buying bonds and holding them to maturity.
Call option (in a bond)
Issuer's right to redeem early at the call price, usually after a lock-in period.
CCIL
Clearing Corporation of India Ltd: central counterparty for G-Sec, repo, TREPS and forex trades.
Certificate of Deposit (CD)
Short-term discounted instrument issued by banks.
Clean price
Quoted bond price excluding accrued interest.
Commercial Paper (CP)
Unsecured short-term discounted note issued by companies, NBFCs and primary dealers.
Contingent immunisation
Active management while a surplus above a floor exists; switch to immunisation when the cushion is used up.
Convexity
Curvature of the price-yield curve; C = (1/P)d2P/dy2; correction term +½C(Δy)2.
Coupon
Periodic interest payment; coupon rate = annual coupon/face value.
Credit rating
Agency opinion (CRISIL, ICRA, CARE, India Ratings) on the likelihood of timely payment, AAA to D.
Credit risk
Risk that the issuer defaults or is downgraded.
Credit spread
Yield of a bond minus the G-Sec yield of the same maturity; compensation for credit and liquidity risk.
Current yield
Annual coupon/market price × 100.
Cut-off price / yield
The lowest accepted price (highest accepted yield) in an auction.
Dated security
Government bond with a fixed maturity, usually paying semi-annual coupons.
Day count
Rule for counting days for interest: 30/360 (G-Secs), Actual/365 (T-Bills), Actual/Actual (corporates).
Digital bond
Bond issued, recorded and serviced electronically end-to-end.
Dirty (full) price
Clean price + accrued interest; the amount really paid.
Discount bond
Bond priced below face value (coupon < YTM).
Discount factor
1/(1 + st)t: today's value of ₹1 due at time t.
Discriminatory (multiple-price) auction
Each winning bidder pays its own bid price.
DLT (distributed ledger technology)
A shared, synchronised, tamper-evident record kept by many nodes; blockchain is one type.
Duration (Macaulay)
PV-weighted average time to receive a bond's cash flows, in years.
Duration (modified)
DM/(1 + y/m); approximate % price change for a 1% change in yield.
Duration (effective)
(P− − P+)/(2P0Δy); used for bonds with embedded options.
Duration theorem
ΔP/P ≈ −DMod × Δy.
DV01 / PVBP
Rupee change in value for a 1 bp change in yield: DMod × value × 0.0001.
EBP (Electronic Book Provider)
Exchange platforms (NSE, BSE) through which private placements of debt are bid and allotted.
e-Kuber
RBI's core banking platform on which G-Sec and T-Bill auctions are conducted.
Expected loss
PD × LGD × EAD.
Expectations theory
Long rates equal the average of current and expected short rates; no term premium.
Face (par) value
Amount repaid at maturity; basis for the coupon.
FBIL
Financial Benchmarks India Ltd; publishes G-Sec valuation prices, yield curves and benchmark rates.
Floating rate bond (FRB)
Bond whose coupon resets periodically to a benchmark plus a spread.
Forward rate
Rate for a future period implied by today's spot rates: f = (1 + sn)n/(1 + sm)m, annualised.
Fractional ownership
Holding a small slice of a bond, made practical by tokenisation.
G-Sec
Government security: T-Bills, dated securities, SDLs, SGrBs.
Green bond
Bond whose proceeds finance eligible green projects; follows the ICMA Green Bond Principles.
Green Bond Principles (GBP)
ICMA voluntary guidelines: use of proceeds, project evaluation and selection, management of proceeds, reporting.
Greenium
Yield discount on a green bond relative to a comparable conventional bond.
Greenwashing
Overstating the environmental benefits of an instrument or issuer.
Haircut
Percentage by which collateral value exceeds cash lent in a repo.
Humped curve
Yield curve with medium maturities yielding most.
Immunisation
Matching PV and duration of assets to a liability so that rate changes do not affect the ability to pay it.
Interest-rate risk
Risk that rising yields reduce bond prices; measured by duration.
Interpolation (YTM)
Linear estimate of YTM between two trial rates bracketing the price.
Inverted curve
Short-term yields above long-term yields.
KPI (in SLBs)
Key performance indicator: the sustainability metric an SLB is linked to.
Ladder
Equal amounts spread across successive maturities, each reinvested at the long end as it matures.
LGD
Loss given default: share of exposure lost if default occurs (1 − recovery rate).
Liquidity preference theory
Long yields include a term premium that increases with maturity.
Malkiel's theorems
Five rules of bond price behaviour: inverse, maturity, coupon, yield level, convexity (I-M-C-Y-C).
Market segmentation theory
Each maturity segment's yield is set by its own supply and demand.
Maturity
Date (or remaining time) when principal is repaid.
NDS-OM
RBI's anonymous electronic order-matching system for secondary-market G-Secs.
Non-competitive bid
Amount-only bid, allotted at the weighted-average price/yield of accepted competitive bids.
Normal curve
Upward-sloping yield curve: long yields above short yields.
Notified amount
Amount RBI announces it will raise in an auction.
OIS (overnight indexed swap)
Swap exchanging a fixed rate for a compounded overnight rate; used to hedge rate risk.
Oracle
Trusted data feed that supplies real-world information (e.g. a verified KPI) to a smart contract.
Par yield
Coupon rate at which a bond would price exactly at par.
Passive management
Buy-and-hold, indexing, immunisation or cash-flow matching.
PD
Probability of default over a period.
Preferred habitat theory
Investors prefer a maturity range but move for a sufficient premium.
Premium bond
Bond priced above face value (coupon > YTM).
Primary dealer
RBI-authorised entity that underwrites G-Sec auctions and makes markets.
Pull-to-par
Convergence of a bond's price to face value as maturity approaches.
Realised compound yield
(Terminal value/price)1/n − 1 using actual reinvestment rates.
Reinvestment risk
Risk that coupons are reinvested at lower rates than the YTM.
Repo
Sale of securities with an agreement to repurchase; a collateralised loan.
Repo rate (policy)
Rate at which RBI lends to banks against G-Secs; the main policy rate.
Retail Direct
RBI scheme (2021) letting individuals hold G-Secs directly and bid non-competitively.
Riding the yield curve
Buying a bond longer than the horizon on a positively sloped curve and selling it as it rolls down to lower yields.
SDL
State Development Loan: dated security issued by a State Government.
SGrB
Sovereign Green Bond issued by the Government of India (first auctioned 2023).
Sharpe ratio
(Rp − Rf)/σp: excess return per unit of total risk.
SLB
Sustainability-linked bond: coupon depends on meeting SPTs; proceeds for general purposes.
SLR
Statutory Liquidity Ratio: share of banks' deposits that must be held in liquid assets such as G-Secs.
Smart contract
Self-executing code on a blockchain that automates payments and rules.
Spot rate
Yield on a zero-coupon bond for a given maturity, for a loan starting today.
SPT
Sustainability performance target: the level a KPI must reach by a date.
Stress test
Estimating losses under severe but plausible shocks to rates, spreads or liquidity.
STRIPS
Separate Trading of Registered Interest and Principal of Securities: zero-coupon pieces of a G-Sec.
T-Bill
Treasury Bill: 91-, 182- or 364-day discounted Central Government instrument.
Term premium
Extra yield demanded for holding longer maturities.
Term structure of interest rates
Relationship between yield and time to maturity; the yield curve is its graph.
Tokenised bond
Bond whose ownership is represented by digital tokens on a DLT.
TREPS
Tri-party repo dealing and settlement on CCIL, India's main money-market repo segment.
Treynor ratio
(Rp − Rf)/βp: excess return per unit of systematic risk.
Uniform-price auction
All winners pay the cut-off price.
Winner's curse
In discriminatory auctions, the most aggressive bidder overpays relative to others.
Yield curve
Graph of yields of bonds of similar credit quality against maturity.
Yield to call (YTC)
IRR assuming the bond is called on the call date at the call price.
Yield to maturity (YTM)
Discount rate equating PV of all promised cash flows to price; the bond's IRR.
Yield to worst
Lowest of YTM and all yields to call.
Zero-coupon bond
Bond paying no coupons, issued at a discount and redeemed at face; duration = maturity.

One-page cheat sheet

Print this page (the sidebar hides automatically). Everything you need in the last 30 minutes.

Pricing

P = C·PVIFA(y,n) + F/(1+y)n; PVIFA = [1 − (1+y)−n]/y. Semi-annual: C/2, y/2, 2n. Zero: P = F/(1+y)n; y = (F/P)1/n − 1. T-Bill: y = (F−P)/P × 365/d. Coupon > y ⇒ premium; < ⇒ discount; = ⇒ par.

Yields

CY = C/P. Approx YTM = [C + (F−P)/n]/[(F+P)/2]; refine by interpolation rL + (PL−P)/(PL−PH)×(rH−rL). YTC: replace F by call price, n by years to call. Yield to worst = min. Realised = (TV/P)1/n − 1. Discount bond: coupon < CY < YTM.

Malkiel: I-M-C-Y-C

Inverse; longer Maturity ↑ sensitivity (decreasing rate); lower Coupon ↑; lower Yield level ↑; Convexity: gain from fall > loss from rise.

Duration and convexity

DM = Σt·PV/P; DMod = DM/(1+y/m). ΔP/P ≈ −DModΔy + ½C(Δy)2. DV01 = DMod×V×0.0001. Zero: D = n. Perpetuity: (1+y)/y. Portfolio: weighted average. C = Σt(t+1)PV/[P(1+y)2].

Risks

Rates ↑ ⇒ price risk; rates ↓ ⇒ reinvestment risk (they offset at D = horizon). Credit: EL = PD×LGD×EAD; spread = corp − G-Sec. Also liquidity, call, inflation risk.

Auctions and repo

Rank best to worst (price high→low; yield low→high); cut-off where cumulative ≥ notified; pro-rata at cut-off. Uniform = amount × cut-off; discriminatory = Σ own bids. Non-competitive: weighted average. Repo cash = MV(1 − h); interest = cash×r×d/365.

Yield curve

X = maturity, Y = yield. Normal (short < long), flat, inverted (short > long), humped (middle highest). Theories: expectations, liquidity preference, segmentation, preferred habitat. Forward: (1+s2)2 = (1+s1)(1+f). Bootstrap: dn = (1 − cΣd)/(1+c). Corporate yield ≈ G-Sec + spread.

Portfolio strategies

Active: rate anticipation, curve (bullet/barbell), riding, spread, swaps. Passive: buy-and-hold, indexing, ladder, immunisation (PV and D matched; rebalance), cash-flow matching. Barbell wS = (DL−DT)/(DL−DS). Stress ΔV ≈ MV[−DΔy + ½CΔy2].

Performance

Sharpe = (Rp−Rf)/σ; Treynor = (Rp−Rf)/β; alpha = Rp − [Rf + β(Rm−Rf)]; beta = Cov/Var. Excel: SLOPE = beta, INTERCEPT = alpha.

Unit 5 in six lines

Green bond = use of proceeds; GBP 4: use, evaluation/selection, management, reporting. SLB = KPI + SPT + step-up; SLBP 5: KPIs, SPTs, characteristics, reporting, verification. Greenwashing ⇒ external review. SGrBs: Budget 2022–23, first RBI auctions Jan/Feb 2023. Tokenised bond = tokens on DLT, fractional, smart-contract coupons, faster DvP.

Excel functions

PRICE, YIELD, RATE, PV, DURATION, MDURATION, ACCRINT, TBILLYIELD, TBILLPRICE, IRR, STDEV.S, SLOPE, Goal Seek. Basis: 0 = 30/360, 1 = Act/Act, 3 = Act/365.

Indian anchors to quote

RBI (debt manager, e-Kuber auctions, NDS-OM, Retail Direct 2021), CCIL (CCP, TREPS), SEBI (corporate bonds, EBP, green debt securities), FBIL (valuation), CRISIL/ICRA/CARE (ratings), JP Morgan index inclusion of Indian G-Secs (2024).

Exam-day checklist

  • Read the cheat sheet and the master formula sheet once, slowly.
  • Re-solve Practice Q1, Q3, Q5 and Q7 from memory – one of each type (zero, coupon, auction, duration).
  • Recite I-M-C-Y-C, the four curve shapes, the four theories, GBP (4) and SLBP (5).
  • Re-read the 20 examiner traps and the "What the examiner rewards" box in Past papers solved; re-solve mid-sem Q3(a), Q4(a) and Q6 from memory.
  • Pack: scientific calculator (fresh battery), two pens, pencil, ruler, hall ticket, ID card, watch.
  • Spend 5 minutes reading the whole paper; choose internal-choice questions where you can write numbers.
  • Budget time in proportion to marks (Section A 15, B 20, C 15 of 50) and keep 10 minutes to review. For example, if the paper is 2 hours (as your mid-sem was): about 11 minutes per 5-mark answer, 22 minutes per 10-mark answer and 33 minutes for the 15-mark question (110 minutes + 10 to review). Check the duration printed on the question paper.
  • Numericals: write the formula, define symbols, show the substitution, box the answer with units, and add one line of interpretation (buy/sell, premium/discount, which bond).
  • Show discount factors or PVIFA values to 4–6 decimals; round money to 2 decimals; state "approximately" for approximate YTM and duration estimates.
  • Theory: introduction (definition) → headed points → Indian example → diagram if possible (price-yield curve, yield-curve shapes) → conclusion.
  • Case study: identify the problem, compute, compare alternatives, recommend, state limitations.
  • Check signs (yields up ⇒ prices down), units (bp vs %), semi-annual adjustments and that auction allotments add up to the notified amount.
